Rocket Report: FAA eyes more Starbase traffic; India's Skyroot snares US customer
2026-10-16

Rocket Report: FAA eyes more Starbase traffic; India's Skyroot snares US customer

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Welcome to Edition 9.15 of the Rocket Report! The launch I’m most looking forward to in the next few weeks is that of Japan’s Martian Moons Exploration (MMX) mission. MMX is one of the marquee space science missions launching this year, and it’s set to leave Earth as soon as Monday (US time) aboard a Japanese H3 rocket. The spacecraft will visit Phobos and Deimos, the two asteroid-like moons of Mars, deploying a rover and collecting specimens for return to Earth in 2031. Exploration at its finest. As always, we welcome reader submissions, and if you don’t want to miss an issue, please subscribe using the box below (the form will not appear on AMP-enabled versions of the site). Each report will include information on small-, medium-, and heavy-lift rockets as well as a quick look ahead at the next three launches on the calendar. Reflect Orbital looks to India for launch capacity. California-based Reflect Orbital, the startup attempting to offer sunlight on-demand with in-space mirrors, announced the purchase of two launches Wednesday from Indian launcher Skyroot Aerospace, Payload Space reports. One of the launches will use Skyroot’s Vikram-1 rocket, which became the first privately developed Indian rocket to reach orbit in July. The other will fly on an upgraded rocket called the Vikram-1U. Shining a light … Reflect Orbital plans to launch its first satellite on a SpaceX Falcon 9 rideshare next year. The company’s business model involves flying a constellation of some 50,000 mirrors in orbit to beam sunlight to the ground for power generation and illumination. The FCC approved Reflect’s first in-space mirror satellite earlier this year. The company’s selection of India’s Skyroot also shines a light on what satellite operators fear is a coming under-supply of launch capacity in the United States as SpaceX phases out its Falcon 9 rocket in favor of the much larger, but relatively unproven, Starship. LEAP targets smallsat launch market. A Colorado rocket startup named LEAP is aiming to enter the market for dedicated small-satellite launches in 2028 as growing demand for access to orbit creates new opportunities for smaller rockets, Space News reports. LEAP’s Bighorn rocket is designed for an initial payload capacity of 320 pounds (145 kilograms) to low-Earth orbit, with a first orbital launch slated for 2028. Bighorn is sized for dedicated launch services, with a single-engine booster stage and a kick stage named Cottontail for orbit insertion. Rockets of mass production … LEAP says its Bighorn rocket is engineered for mass production. The company’s new factory, located in a Denver suburb, will support initial production of six Bighorn vehicles per year, with capacity to scale to two per month. “We know how to build remarkable vehicles. The challenge is building enough of them fast enough,” said Chris Beckman, CEO of LEAP, in a statement last month announcing the company’s new headquarters. LEAP launched a smaller suborbital rocket, named Bullfrog, for the first time in May, reaching an altitude of approximately 130,000 feet (40 kilometers). Neutron debut delayed. Rocket Lab announced Wednesday that its Neutron launch vehicle won’t fly until 2027, citing an issue discovered during manufacturing of the rocket’s carbon composite structures, Ars reports. The announcement was public confirmation of what nearly everyone inside Rocket Lab and the wider space industry already knew. The most recent official update from Rocket Lab in August suggested the company still held hope of delivering the first Neutron rocket to its launch pad at Wallops Island, Virginia, before the end of this year, a schedule that would have almost certainly pushed the first flight into 2027. Altered carbon … Rocket Lab’s statement Wednesday about Neutron’s schedule suggests the company is still struggling with manufacturing the rocket’s large booster stage. Neutron’s reusable first stage is made of carbon composite structures, which offer low weight, high strength, and corrosion resistance. But carbon composites in aerospace are generally harder to manufacture than metallic structures. That latest problem discovered in Rocket Lab’s factory involves small air pockets, or voids, amid carbon fiber layers making up the forward dome of Neutron’s first stage propellant tank. (submitted by zapman987) Stoke ships second stage for testing. The reusable upper stage for Stoke Space’s Nova rocket has left the company’s factory in Kent, Washington, for what would be the last time, Stoke wrote on X this week. Now at Stoke’s test facility in Moses Lake, the second stage will undergo cryo-proof testing and a hotfire campaign with its Andromeda engine. The milestone follows the completion of “proto-qualification” testing on Nova’s first stage earlier this year. Nova’s booster section is a rather conventional reusable first stage, but the upper stage is what sets the rocket apart. Stoke designed Nova for full reusability, with the upper stage protected by a regeneratively cooled heat shield and powered by a ring of 24 thrusters, an engine collectively named Andromeda. Next stop, Cape Canaveral … The upcoming tests at Moses Lake will be a linchpin for Stoke’s ambition to launch the Nova rocket next year. If all goes according to plan, Stoke will ship the reusable rocket stage to Cape Canaveral, Florida, where it will meet the other elements of the rocket for final launch preparations. The medium-lift Nova is the only fully reusable Western rocket other than SpaceX’s Starship in an advanced stage of development. Falcon 9 launches US military data transport satellites. A SpaceX Falcon 9 rocket carried the Space Development Agency’s fourth plane of Tranche 1 Transport Layer satellites to low-Earth orbit from Vandenberg Space Force Base, California, on Saturday, Aviation Week & Space Technology reports. The launch continues the deployment of the SDA’s network of data relay satellites to support space-based missile warning and tracking and tactical communications and targeting. The SDA has contracts with multiple companies to build the network’s data relay and missile-tracking satellites. This was the first launch of a batch of satellites from Northrop Grumman. Overcoming an abort … That mission was originally slated to launch October 5, but the Falcon 9’s automated systems halted the initial countdown just before liftoff. The launch was then pushed off two more times as SpaceX conducted extra safety checks on the vehicle and worked with the US Space Force to deconflict with other mission deployments. SpaceX completes 35th ISS cargo delivery. On Tuesday morning, SpaceX’s Falcon 9 rocket roared away from Pad 40 at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station to begin a cargo run to the International Space Station, Spaceflight Now reports. The 35th Commercial Resupply Services (CRS-35) mission carried 6,322 pounds (2,868 kilograms) of science and supplies to the orbiting outpost, where it arrived Thursday with an automated docking of the Dragon capsule to the lab’s Harmony module. Bringing the goods … SpaceX’s latest resupply mission delivered two new roll-out solar arrays to augment the ISS power system. The other payloads will support more than 40 scientific investigations in orbit, including a materials science experiment testing a more flexible and durable type of glass in the environment of space. Dragon also brought up fresh food for the lab’s seven-person crew. Let us launch more, SpaceX asks FAA. Federal aviation regulators are reviewing a plan that could allow hundreds more Starship rocket launches and landings a year at SpaceX’s Starbase in South Texas, the San Antonio Express-News reports. The proposal calls for as many as 456 launches and 912 landings a year at the complex outside Brownsville, roughly 18 times the currently allowed 25 launches and 50 landings. Starship recovery coming soon … The giant rockets have an upper stage, named Starship, and a lower stage booster, called Super Heavy. Both sections are expected to eventually land at Starbase, hence twice the number of landings. SpaceX has successfully landed boosters back at Starbase, but it has not attempted the feat with a Starship. That could happen as soon as the next Starship test flight later this year, according to Elon Musk, SpaceX’s founder and CEO. Ship 40 is back at Starbase. In other news at Starbase, SpaceX has returned the first Starship spacecraft to survive an ocean splashdown back to the South Texas launch site for inspection, Space.com reports. The rocket completed a trip halfway around the world in July before descending to a pinpoint splashdown in the Indian Ocean northwest of Australia. Surprisingly, the rocket survived splashdown without exploding or breaking apart, as SpaceX expects Starships to do after ocean landings. The company towed the rocket to calmer waters off Christmas Island, then loaded it onto a barge for the journey back to Texas. Vertical again … SpaceX teams lifted Starship upright after unloading it from its transport ship, moving it inside Starbase’s Megabay hangar alongside a trio of its successor spacecraft awaiting their opportunities to fly to space. Inspections of the flight-proven Starship will “provide first-of-its-kind data on a flown Starship heatshield, Raptor engines, avionics, and more,” SpaceX wrote in a post on X. October 19: Falcon 9 | Bandwagon-5 | Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida | 10:30 UTC October 19: H3 | Martian Moons Exploration mission | Tanegashima Space Center, Japan | 19:41 UTC October 20: Falcon 9 | Starlink 15-15 | Vandenberg Space Force Base, California | 22:14 UTC

Blue Origin is going all in on the New Glenn rocket
2026-10-16

Blue Origin is going all in on the New Glenn rocket

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Blue Origin announced a major expansion of its already considerable Florida launch facilities on Friday morning with the intent of eventually flying 150 rockets a year from there. The company said it would add two additional launch pads for the New Glenn rocket, in addition to two already under construction at Cape Canaveral. The company is also investing heavily in a new manufacturing facility that will produce up to 150 upper stages for a larger variant of the New Glenn rocket. Additionally, the company plans to procure two additional landing ships to catch and return the first stage of the New Glenn rocket (it currently has one, named Jacklyn). Finally, Blue Origin said it would add a new customer integration facility, built in conjunction with the US Space Force, that will feature four payload bays to process multiple missions at the same time. “These investments are centered on one goal: building the capacity to support more missions, more reliably, and more efficiently,” wrote Ian Richardson, senior vice president of operations at Blue Origin, in an update on the company’s website this morning. “As we look ahead, this infrastructure is designed to support multiple flights per week by the end of the decade. To date, Blue Origin has invested about $3 billion in facilities and infrastructure in Florida, largely to support the construction and launch of the heavy-lift New Glenn rocket. The company now has more than 5,000 employees working in Florida and 500 suppliers in the state. “Our manufacturing and operations footprint has more than doubled in the last couple of years, now spanning more than 20 sites across Brevard and Orange Counties,” Richardson wrote. “Blue Origin is the only company—and the largest employer—to manufacture, launch, and refurbish rockets on the Space Coast.” That would seem to be a reference to superseding the 800-pound gorilla on the Florida Space Coast, SpaceX, which has launched hundreds of rockets into orbit over the years from Florida. Blue Origin has launched to orbit twice. But Blue Origin is expanding its rocket facilities while SpaceX is pulling back from the commercial launch market with its Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy vehicles. As Ars has reported, a growing launch crisis is emerging as commercial satellite customers have struggled to purchase a Falcon launch after 2028. This has created an opening for medium- and heavy-lift vehicles that, if Blue Origin can get New Glenn flying successfully and frequently, it is poised to fill. The company’s previous investments, along with the new infrastructure, suggest Blue Origin founder Jeff Bezos is deadly serious about making this a reality. After a promising start, the New Glenn rocket program was stalled in late May, when a problem with one of the rocket’s BE-4 engines led to the catastrophic loss of the vehicle and its only launch site, Launch Complex 36A at Cape Canaveral. Since then, the company has been urgently rebuilding the site at 36A to support a return-to-flight mission for New Glenn before the end of 2026. While this is unlikely, the company appears on track to resume New Glenn launches during the first few months of 2027. Blue Origin has previously announced its plans to rebuild 36A as rapidly as possible, as well as completing the nearby Launch Complex 36B site to support a larger version of New Glenn. This 9×4 variant of the rocket has nine first-stage engines compared to the smaller 7×2 variant, with seven engines. The new launch pads announced Friday morning are likely intended to support the larger 9×4 variant, as Richardson wrote, “These pads will be rapidly sited and constructed by replicating the design and construction approach being demonstrated with the build of LC-36B.” The 9×4 rocket is designed to carry 70 metric tons to low-Earth orbit, and more than 20 tons to trans-lunar injection. The company has not confirmed this, but Blue Origin likely needs the 9×4 variant as part of its plans to launch a human lander to the Moon for NASA as part of the Artemis Program. It could debut in 2028 if development goes well.

Googlebook review: Android on a laptop is still Android
2026-10-16

Googlebook review: Android on a laptop is still Android

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Android was designed for phones, but that hasn’t stopped Google from trying to adapt the OS and its apps to larger machines over the years. Googlebooks are just the latest attempt after tablets, Chromebooks, and foldables induced little change in how people interact with Android software. These new Android-powered laptops are designed to pick up where the web-focused ChromeOS left off, offering premium hardware and native support for Android apps. Google sees Googlebooks as an alternative to Windows and macOS, but these platforms are entrenched with decades of software and UI conventions that keep users locked in. Googlebooks have to offer something very special to steal users away from either of the dominant operating systems. For devoted Android phone users, there are some cool things in Googlebook OS, but the Android roots of these machines are impossible to miss. Sometimes it feels like using a Chromebook, and sometimes it feels like using a giant phone. It almost never feels like using a premium laptop, and that’s the problem. While there were a few premium Chromebooks, the platform was really only successful in the budget segment. With Googlebooks, the company is almost conspicuously ignoring lower-cost options. And as most of the lineup clocks in at well over a thousand dollars, Googlebooks are not just a new take on budget-oriented Chromebooks. All of the launch Googlebooks have excellent touchscreen displays, aluminum and magnesium bodies, biometric unlock (fingerprint or face), and big trackpads. There’s also the Glowbar, which doesn’t do very much right now. It lights up in Google colors when Gemini is activated with the wake word and during boot-up. It also shows the battery level when you plug in a Googlebook. The latter is modestly useful, but it’s not really a selling point. There’s something in the lineup for most premium laptop buyers. If you want a convertible, there’s the Acer machine. Sleek and spendy? Asus and HP have you covered. Want something a little bigger? Lenovo has a 15.3-inch model with an HDMI port and USB-A. The least-spendy model, the Acer Googlebook 14, starts at $899, and that’s a limited-time deal. Even this one gets a lot of the hardware right. It has a vibrant OLED screen, a metal chassis, and three USB-C ports (all of which can charge the laptop). However, you won’t get a haptic trackpad, just the old-fashioned kind that physically clicks. The supposedly DTS-tuned speakers also don’t sound great. Still, the hardware is at least as nice as any $900 Windows laptop. Acer hasn’t decided when the price will change or what it will be, but it’s at $899 for the rest of the year at least. The next step up from that is the Dell XPS Googlebook at $999, but this model has an LCD instead of OLED. If you want the most capable Google laptops with OLED and haptic trackpads, you’re looking at spending at least $1,100 for the Lenovo version. This machine is solid, and the larger footprint allows for vents in the hinge instead of on the bottom. But I don’t love the slightly wobbly feel of the keys or the flex in the middle of the keyboard. The HP Googlebook 14 has a much nicer keyboard, a better overall look, and a Snapdragon X Elite chip inside. However, it’s also $1,300. I appreciate that Google is pushing for things like haptic trackpads and high-resolution OLED screens, but the more expensive Googlebooks suffer from the same issue as premium Windows laptops—they don’t exist in a vacuum. If you’re spending $1,300 on a laptop, the entry-level MacBook Air is probably a better overall option for most people. All of the launch Googlebooks have the same keyboard layout, which is a bit different from what you get on other laptops. There’s a G button that works similarly to the Windows button, and the top F-row has been revamped. You get the usual Windows staples like brightness and volume control, but there are also Android navigation buttons and a voice input key that activates the AI-powered Rambler. Probably the best change to the Googlebook keyboard is ‘quick insert’ in place of ‘caps lock’ (you can still get caps lock with the Fn toggle). Just press this key to add emoji, recent screenshots, clipboard contents, recently visited URLs, and more. Admittedly, I don’t mind using function layers on a keyboard, so the extra step to access caps isn’t a concern. You may feel differently, and that’s okay. Googlebooks come in both Intel and Snapdragon variants. I’ve been using both, and general performance feels about the same. There have been some reports of app issues with Intel-powered Googlebooks, but I haven’t run into any apps that will only run on Snapdragon. The Snapdragon model (HP) seems to have a higher performance ceiling, if you can find a way to reach it. Gaming performance also appears better on Snapdragon, but even the heaviest Android games seem fine on the Intel models (Lenovo and Acer). It can be hard to push these machines when you’re relying on software that was originally designed to run on phones. Unsurprisingly, software proves to be the biggest hurdle for Googlebooks. Google has flirted with Android on larger screens on a few occasions, mostly with tablets and tablets that impersonate laptops. This is the first time it has totally revamped the OS for a laptop, but it’s still Android under the hood. Sometimes that’s good, and other times not so much. Setting up a Googlebook is a snap for Android users. Just like configuring a new phone, you can scan a QR code with your current phone to copy your accounts, data, and apps over automatically. You can be up and running in minutes, and your phone will integrate deeply with the OS. Well, provided it runs Android 17. That’s going to be a sticking point, since most phones don’t have that update yet. With Google’s “Better Together” services, a Googlebook can access files on your Android 17 phone, sync notifications, remember where you left off in apps, and even mirror apps from your phone. This all sounds great, and when it works, the experience can be good. However, there are various limitations and bugs that get in the way. To remain in contact, the phone and Googlebook need to be on the same Wi-Fi network, and switching to other networks can break the connection in annoying ways. Even when the devices appear to have a solid connection, I have rarely seen any hint of Google’s Continue On feature, which is supposed to let you pick up where you left off in a phone app. Mirroring phone apps on your laptop can also be handy, but the performance is varying degrees of bad, and apps with system-level security features won’t work at all. You are also limited to one app at a time, and they won’t stay open in the background. Accessing the files on your phone from the laptop is a good idea, but the transfer speed is often sluggish. More annoyingly, the connection only goes one way—the Files app on the Googlebook can see your phone, but the Google Files app on a phone can’t see the Googlebook. Speaking of the Files app, this is emblematic of how far behind Google is with desktop software. The app lacks basic things like tabs and support for network shares. It’s nice that it can access my phone, but it feels very restrictive if you’re used to Finder or Windows Explorer. Google has at least done a good job of making Android look like a desktop OS. There’s a proper desktop that you can fill with app shortcuts, folders, widgets, and files. There’s a folder in the storage tree where you can manage that content, or you can simply drag things onto it from a file manager. You also get ChromeOS-style split-screen support and multiple desktops. These features work, but they’re not as intuitive or capable as what you get on either macOS or Windows. AI is a big part of the Googlebook experience. Google talks a lot about Magic Pointer, which you can activate by wiggling the cursor. This allows you to hand any content on your screen over to Gemini, but calling it magic is a little silly. You can have it translate text, make images, create calendar appointments, and so on. You’re basically feeding Gemini screenshots and asking it to do things. Magic Pointer can be useful sometimes, but it’s hardly revolutionary to streamline the process of feeding screen content into an AI by attaching it to the mouse. You still have to contend with the inaccuracy of Gemini. Even adding calendar appointments can fail, which means you have to double-check almost everything the AI does. Magic Pointer is an optional feature, and you can adjust the sensitivity of the gesture. However, I never found a level that I was entirely happy to use. Too much sensitivity, and the gesture triggers when you don’t intend it. Too little, and you have to shake the mouse so hard that the cursor is probably going to be nowhere near what you wanted to highlight. Rambler, which debuted on the Pixel 11 lineup, is included with all Googlebooks. This AI-powered voice input feature works in any text field on the laptop, which is handy if you want to add a lot of text without typing and don’t mind sounding like a robot. This has been popular on phones, and most people using it on a Googlebook will probably be impressed. Create My Widget is another head-scratcher, though. The pitch is that you can simply describe the widget you want, and the AI will place it on your desktop. It sounds cool, but in reality, the content you can rope in is so limited as to make the feature largely useless. If you want calendar countdowns and sports scores, great. If you want system info or app content, you’re out of luck. Googlebooks come with the Play Store, which is crammed full of all the Android apps you could ever want. But desktop apps? Google says it has worked with devs to optimize apps for Googlebooks, and some software has been newly built from the ground up for a desktop experience. For example, Chrome on Googlebooks is a mostly desktop-class experience. It’s missing a few niche features like split windows, tab groups, and multiple profiles, but it will work with all of your extensions. On other platforms, I’ve routinely used desktop clients for Dropbox and Drive to sync files. I had hoped something similar would be possible on these Android laptops, but no such luck. Dropbox runs on a Googlebook, but it’s not optimized. The UI is clunky, and even if you save a folder offline, it only updates when you refresh the app manually (just like a phone running the app). Meanwhile, the Drive app isn’t even an Android app—it’s a progressive web app (PWA). So you can’t set it up to sync a folder to the device. All you have is the option to make individual files in Drive available offline. Without a real desktop app experience here, you actually get a lot more functionality out of Google’s cloud storage system on Windows and macOS. Drive isn’t the only Google web app masquerading as a native app. Gmail and Calendar are also PWAs on the Googlebook, offering the same experience you’d get opening them in a browser tab. Gmail exists as an Android app with a large screen format. Why not adapt that for Googlebooks? Real operating systems have email apps. If you’re just going to end up in a web app, why not buy a Chromebook for a third of the price? The Play Store uses labels to let you know which apps are best on a laptop, but most apps in the Play Store are very obviously built around a phone experience. Google is in a tough spot here. Developers only build for what people use, and so far, no one has been demanding desktop-style Android apps. As you peruse the Play Store, you’ll see a few of those “optimized” labels, but there are many more “Compatible with desktop” tags, which means you can expect only nominal support for a keyboard and mouse. The most problematic apps are flagged in the Play Store as “Built for mobile” or even “Better on the website.” Some popular apps fall into these categories. For example, team chat app Slack is an abysmal experience on anything other than a phone. The app runs on Googlebooks, but the web UI is much more desktop-friendly. You are supposed to be able to install Slack as a progressive web app, essentially what Google is doing with Gmail, Drive, and other apps. However, this doesn’t work currently for unknown reasons. Again, you could just use a Chromebook if you wanted everything in a browser tab. Even apps that have been optimized for desktop can be frustrating on a Googlebook. These apps are aware you have a keyboard, but they can respond to button presses in ways you don’t expect. Photoshop is a major offender here. The app has a poorly scaled mobile UI shifted into landscape, and there are only a few keyboard shortcuts. Sometimes, you’ll see a UI element that looks like it should be controllable with the arrows or enter key, but you just end up moving the cursor around and selecting the wrong thing. In fairness, Android’s Photoshop app is free, whereas the real Adobe suite on other platforms costs money. But calling this a desktop app is a stretch. You’ll find lots of pbone-first apps that don’t fully understand that you have a trackpad, ignoring hover interactions and right-clicks. Many apps also expect you to frequently trigger the Android back gesture to navigate the UI. On that last count, Google has a solution. Googlebooks have Back, Home, and Multitasking buttons on F1-F3. So you can make phone apps work, but reaching up to mash F1 constantly is annoying. The Android lineage of Googlebook OS also means that even apps built for desktop may not operate the way you’d expect on a desktop PC. Android was designed for a memory-constrained environment, so the system closes apps in the background whenever it needs to free up RAM. It’s common to come back to an app you left in the background to find that it has lost your place and has to reload. This is a phone-like experience that doesn’t really fit with a real computer. Google is aware that Googlebook apps may not fulfill all needs. One possible solution to that, say reps, is to simply make what you need with Antigravity. The new Android version of Google’s AI-centric development app is included on every Googlebook, giving you the option to vibe code apps for your laptop. The AI can generate plans, mockups, and line after line of code to bring your idea to life. Antigravity can autonomously download all the Android developer tools it needs and builds APKs ready for installation on your device. Installing your apps is technically sideloading, as they’re not hosted in the Play Store, but Google gives these special treatment. They get scanned by Play Protect, but you don’t have to enable unknown sources in the settings. Google also confirms these apps won’t be affected by developer verification when that rolls out on Googlebooks next year. After struggling with the Files app for a few days, I decided the lack of network shares was a deal-breaker. I keep most of my files on a NAS so I can access them from any device, and Googlebooks were cramping my style. I first tried to find an alternative in the Play Store, but the apps with sufficient features were totally unoptimized for a laptop, so I decided to test Antigravity on Googlebooks by making one. Using Google’s default Gemini Flash model, I was able to get a functional but very buggy file manager installed in a few minutes. Making it work reliably and support all the features I needed took several hours of prompting over a few days, but the result is something I like using more than Google’s default Files app. My custom file manager is essentially Windows Explorer on Android. While I did not set out with that intent, that’s where I ended up. The app has a desktop-friendly UI, tabs, network share support, expandable file trees, file previews, and more buttons. It has more features than Google’s default app, although it lacks the deep phone and system integration of the default file manager. On one hand, it’s cool that you can create your own apps with the included Antigravity software. However, you’ll need to pay someone (probably Google) for enough AI compute to get the job done. It took a lot of iterations and bug hunting with Gemini 3.8 Flash to get my app where I needed it to be, and lighter models will struggle to produce something functional. It’s also not ideal to expect people buying a laptop to spend hours chatting with a robot to make it do basic laptop things. It should do those things out of the box. I keep coming back to one inescapable question that hangs over everything Google is doing with these new laptops: Do Googlebooks need to exist? This seems like a lot of effort just to deliver deeper Android phone integration and more AI features when Chromebooks are already established in the market. True, that’s not a premium part of the market, but going after premium laptops means you need a premium experience, and currently, Googlebooks don’t offer that. The hardware is solid—it’s smart that Google is pushing for things like OLED and haptic trackpads on Googlebooks. Its OEM partners have made some very nice-feeling laptops for Google’s software, but the OS isn’t doing its part. I just don’t feel more productive on a Googlebook than I do on a Chromebook. I’ve been using Android since the beginning—this should be a good laptop for someone like me. However, the rough edges of the Googlebook experience are still wearing me down. It’s great that it talks to my phone, but that conversation is too often one-sided and buggy. It’s good, in theory, that Googlebooks have a deep software library with the Play Store, but too few apps are actually good on a laptop. And it’s cool that Googlebooks have such nice hardware, but they’re too expensive for what you get. Maybe Google can fix this—ChromeOS looked a lot different a year or two after its consumer launch, after all. But Google has a lot of work ahead to make the Googlebook experience what it needs to be, and in the meantime, it’s not a viable alternative to macOS and Windows. Does Google have the patience to spend years doing this work? The company’s recent history leaves me feeling skeptical, and that makes it difficult to recommend a Googlebook to even the most ardent Google customers.

KDE turns 30 with Plasma 6.8, remote upgrades, and an X11 farewell
2026-10-15

KDE turns 30 with Plasma 6.8, remote upgrades, and an X11 farewell

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Marking the 30th anniversary of software developer KDE on October 14, the company released Plasma 6.8, debuted a movie, and retired Plasma’s native X11 session support. The announcement from KDE dubs the new Plasma 6.8 as the “30th Anniversary Edition” and describes the update as preparing Plasma for the future. Legacy X11 applications will still function via the XWayland compatibility layer, and KDE applications will also continue supporting X11 under other desktop environments. After 42 years in service, the X11 protocol is losing another major desktop environment. Plasma will fully adopt the Wayland system and put support for starting new X11 sessions in Plasma to bed. Users opting to stay on Plasma 6.7 will keep their existing sessions for now. The move was expected, with GNOME officially dropping X11 sessions by default in 2025. The new Plasma 6.8 brings many improvements, including increased support for adding window decorations (like shadows and rounded corners) to third-party apps, and a more streamlined Union theming system. Other notable additions include Nvidia triple buffering being enabled by default, Kup backup integration (KDE’s backup utility—now integrated into Plasma), and kscreenctl, a command-line utility that allows power users to adjust display configurations. An updated System Monitor gives KDE users more control over their system processes, too. The revamped monitor allows users to pin processes directly to CPU cores through the GUI. Homelabbers should find plenty to be excited about in the release. KDE revamped Plasma’s built-in remote desktop server to cater to clients accessing virtual displays across a network. Highlighted new features include clipboard synchronization between client and server, “Exclusive Mode” that locks the server’s physical display and allows the client to set a virtual one that matches their PC, and adaptive remote streaming that aids stability on slower networks. Linux enthusiasts can also check out a new movie released alongside Plasma 6.8. The KDE team debuted The KDE Documentary — A Story of Free Software Underdogs. An amusing opening-minute interview montage features KDE contributors laughing as they struggle to answer the question “What is KDE?”

Plex now helps sell movie theater tickets
2026-10-15

Plex now helps sell movie theater tickets

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Plex, which made a name for itself by letting users share movies they own, is now helping sell movie theater tickets. The company is looking to monetize its content-discovery capabilities and expand its business beyond its media server software. Plex partnered with Fandango, which has sold movie tickets since 2000, to drive ticket sales from people searching for movies to stream. Per the announcement: Starting today, Plex users in the US will see a Fandango ticketing option on title pages for movies currently playing in theaters, allowing them to click through to Fandango to find showtimes and purchase tickets. The integration also enables direct attribution through Kochava, giving studios and advertisers greater visibility into how campaigns and audience engagement translate into ticket sales. Fandango says it sells tickets for 31,000 movie theater screens in the US. The Plex deal allows it to leverage data from Plex’s streaming service (Plex claims users of the service “make more than 100 million streaming decisions each month”), including “ratings and cross-platform viewing” to gain “insight into audience intent,” the announcement said. “Plex has a unique view into what movie fans are actively considering, from what they’re searching for to what they’re watching across services,” Jerramy Hainline, executive vice president at Fandango, said in a statement. “By connecting those discovery signals with Fandango ticketing, we’re making it easier for fans to turn that interest into a trip to the theater, while giving studios and advertisers a more direct, measurable path to ticket sales.” Plex, meanwhile, is aiming to be “the place where people can discover what to watch, regardless of where that content lives,” Scott Olechowski, Plex’s co-founder and chief product officer, said in a statement. Content discovery is a frequently cited challenge in the streaming industry, with various players, including smart TV OS operators, streaming service providers, and generative AI chatbots, trying to crack it. If Plex could use its user data to drive worthwhile recommendations, monetizing its content discovery could prove lucrative. “For studios and advertisers, we’re also creating a more measurable path from discovery to purchase, helping marketers understand which campaigns are converting,” Olechowski said. Plex’s Fandango partnership contrasts with its media server business, which lets users share movies they own with friends and family without paying Hollywood licensing fees. The foray into movie ticket sales comes over two-and-a-half years after Plex launched its streaming service, where people can rent movies. Plex also launched a free ad-supported television (FAST) streaming service and has added social media capabilities to its platform. These ventures give Plex additional revenue streams beyond its media server software. As it stands, the businesses coexist peacefully: Someone who only wants to use Plex for media hosting doesn’t have to use Plex’s on-demand streaming services. They can also opt out of some of Plex’s data sharing. (Notably, Plex says it collects data from media server users “searching on Plex.”) On the media-hosting side, Plex has discussed challenges in making lifetime passes financially sustainable. Its new businesses may help fund advancements and/or maintenance to Plex’s media software. At the same time, the company appears largely focused on building out the non-server-software aspects of its business.
Ars Technica - All content
Hegseth’s dubious low-T screening may flag up to 25 percent of the Army
2026-10-15

Hegseth’s dubious low-T screening may flag up to 25 percent of the Army

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Up to a quarter of active-duty Army members could receive a low testosterone result from mandatory military screening, despite the true prevalence of the condition likely being a tiny fraction of that figure, according to a study published Thursday in JAMA. The finding adds to concerns that the mandatory screening announced by Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth in July will lead to a cascade of unnecessary medical evaluations for military members. Those evaluations could result in inappropriate testing, inaccurate results and diagnoses, and overtreatment that could cause harm, including infertility. Medical experts, including the Endocrine Society and the American Urological Association (AUA), have recommended against blanket screenings for these reasons. Still, the Pentagon has moved forward with the plans, releasing clinical guidance in September. The JAMA study highlights the reasons why testosterone levels are not measured in men without symptoms—and also offers a glimpse of how many Army members could become ensnared in the clinically dubious screenings. Researchers at the Pennington Biomedical Research Center in Louisiana and the US Army Research Institute of Environmental Medicine in Massachusetts conducted the study. The researchers drew data from a large military health survey, which included members at five US Army installations. The survey data included morning blood draws from fasting military members. The samples were collected between August 2024 and January 2026. From those samples, total testosterone levels were measured from 479 male, active-duty Army service members. (Those receiving testosterone treatment were excluded from the study—three men were excluded for this reason.) With some statistical weighting, the 479 included members were representative of the active-duty US Army personnel; the weighted mean age was about 30 years old, and the weighted mean body mass index (BMI) was 28.4, which falls in the “overweight” category. Generally, experts are wary of universally screening men for low testosterone because testosterone measurements can be highly variable—and unhelpful. Levels can dip for a number of different reasons, including age, time of day, obesity, illness, physical stress, poor sleep, trauma, and various medications. Moreover, the conditions that can cause clinically significant low testosterone—a condition called male hypogonadism—are rare, affecting 1 percent of men at most. They include genetic conditions and problems with the pituitary gland. Given the complexity, experts recommend that evaluations be prompted by patients self-reporting symptoms. In those cases, doctors use repeat testing and comprehensive evaluations to come to a firm diagnosis of male hypogonadism. With that being the current norm, researchers aren’t sure exactly what the military’s new universal screen will yield. According to the new data, a large portion of the military may face alarming results. From the Army members in the survey, the weighted mean for morning total testosterone was 405 ng/dL. The Pentagon’s clinical guidance uses a threshold of less than 300 ng/dL for low testosterone, in line with recommendations from the AUA. Using that cutoff and extrapolating the data to the entire Army population, the researchers estimate that about 26 percent of active-duty Army service members would get a test result suggesting low testosterone. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention has a harmonized reference cut-off that’s a little lower, at 264 ng/dL. With that threshold, about 15 percent of Army service members would be estimated to have a low testosterone result. As expected, the likelihood of low results scaled with weight and age; the higher the age and/or higher the BMI, the greater the odds of having a testosterone reading under either threshold. Of men under age 30, 19.5 percent had a low measurement, while 33 percent of men aged 30 and older had low levels. “Published estimates of clinical hypogonadism are substantially lower than the prevalence of low testosterone observed here, underscoring that many men with a single low screening measurement do not have clinical hypogonadism,” the researchers write. But each positive screening result will require thorough follow-up, including repeat testing, further evaluation, counseling on modifiable factors (such as losing weight or improving sleep), and treatment considerations, the researchers note. “Because testosterone replacement therapy is long-term and has potential adverse effects, distinguishing an isolated low screening value from clinically significant hypogonadism is essential,” they write. While the study looked at results from all men—without first assessing possible symptoms of male hypogonadism—the military’s screening will likely be more limited. The mandatory screening only applies to men aged 30 and above, while younger men are allowed to opt in. It’s unclear how many will do so. The first step in the screening is to ask members about possible symptoms and risk factors. From there, some men might not proceed to having a testosterone test. Still, the symptoms and risk-factor screening will likely catch a large swath of the military, leading to far more service members getting tested than before. Possible symptoms include vague problems that aren’t closely linked to male hypogonadism, such as “diminished physical performance,” “disturbed sleep,” and “low motivation.” The risk factors considered also include common conditions, such as obesity, type 2 diabetes, and low-trauma fractures. Those with such risk factors can go on to be tested even in the absence of symptoms, according to the Pentagon’s guidance.
Fortune | FORTUNE
‘Europtimism’ clashes with 1 in 3 founders wanting out. We need the EU to finally become a true single market
2026-10-16

‘Europtimism’ clashes with 1 in 3 founders wanting out. We need the EU to finally become a true single market

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Sophie Dembinski is Managing Director, Norrsken Brussels. As winter draws in, Europeans could be forgiven for feeling the summer glow fade as headlines warn of yet higher energy bills and renewed inflation. Yet Europe’s founders and investors see good reason to be optimistic – as long as EU leaders take further action, fast. New data from Norrsken’s European Impact Index 2026 (EII) finds that founders and funders are doubling down on building the clean electro-economy Europe needs to break its dependence on fossil fuels, and their confidence in Europe’s ability to deliver is growing. The EII is an annual survey of 750 entrepreneurs and investors behind the companies working to solve Europe’s hardest problems. Of the investors surveyed, one in two said Europe has become a more attractive place to invest over the past 12 months, against one in four who said it has become less attractive. Founders are also gearing up for growth, with 85% expecting revenues to rise over the next 12 months and three in four planning to increase headcount. That is a marked shift in mood at a time when the wider economic picture would suggest the opposite. However, the same data came with a clear warning attached for Brussels. It revealed that a significant share of European founders are keeping one foot out of the door, and showed investors remain only cautiously optimistic about the political backdrop in which they operate. A stark one in three founders said they had considered relocating their company outside Europe in the past twelve months, including 15% who considered it seriously. Many said they were driven by high energy costs and other factors making it difficult to compete globally. Others cited recruitment challenges – almost four in ten said attracting top talent is harder in Europe than in the US. Meanwhile, around 36% of investors surveyed in the EII rated the EU’s approach to innovation and entrepreneurship poorly or very poorly. Just 6% said the EU prioritises it very well. Over half (52%) of founders also said regulatory differences between EU countries have hindered their ability to scale across borders, up from 47.5% last year. That is not a resounding endorsement. Energy is where the tension between optimism and caution remains sharpest. Investors now see access to cheap and reliable power as a precondition for European companies to compete long-term. 84% said they consider a company’s access to affordable, clean energy when assessing its long-term competitiveness, with almost six in ten calling it very important. Europe is now home to some of the most promising companies building clean power, while remaining an expensive place to buy it. Investors’ money is following that conviction. Nearly 37% of investors surveyed named energy and electrification as their top sector for impact capital allocation, more than three times the next closest sector. The Norrsken/100 2026 – the annual list of the world’s most promising impact companies, as selected by 80 top early-stage investment funds – published today, saw clean energy and electrification startups make up the single largest sector with 26 companies – a figure up 44% on 2025. Companies appearing on the Norrsken/100 included those working on overhauling and strengthening grid capacity and transforming renewables storage. Swiss company Metafuels is working to convert clean electricity into jet fuel, while RIFT and Enerin, based in the Netherlands and Norway respectively, are delivering high-temperature industrial heat from electrons instead of gas.  These companies are opening up the markets that stand between Europe and an electrified economy. They are also exactly the kind of capital-intensive businesses that will go where power is cheap, permits are quick and markets are open. The IEA projects $2.2 trillion will flow into clean energy and electrification this year, against $1.2 trillion into fossil fuels. So for every dollar going into oil, gas and coal, close to two now go into clean power. As my colleague Fabian Erici, Partner at Norrsken VC, put it: “Europe’s hardest problems are becoming its biggest markets.” In responding to the EII, both founders and investors were clear that, in order to seize this momentum, their renewed Europtimism and commitment to decarbonisation, policymakers must now give investors and founders a better framework in which to grow and become global category leaders. The continent’s political leaders must seize the moment to act, and not let this moment of Europtimism slide. Founders and investors were clear on what would keep them here. Their top ask is to make the EU a genuinely single market, named by 45% of founders and almost six in ten investors as the key priority for European policymakers. Companies delivering Europe’s electro-economy need easier access to Europe’s 450 million-strong market, and regulatory differences between EU countries continue to make it harder to scale. Brussels appears to be getting the message. During her State of the Union address on 16th September, President von der Leyen committed to overhauling single market rules by the end of next year and advancing EU Inc to streamline cross-border expansion. Alongside the Capital Markets Union, the Savings and Investments Union and reforms like EuVECA, the Commission’s focus on grid connections, energy costs and permitting backlogs points in the right direction. None of these commitments are new, however, and founders have heard versions of them before. The rising share reporting fragmentation as a barrier suggests the gap between announcement and delivery is now what they are measuring their decision to stay by. The same applies to electrification. At Norrsken, we have called for Europe to become the world’s first “electro-continent”, underpinned by a binding target of 50% clean electrification. The Commission’s proposed 46% target falls short of that ambition, and of its own analysis, which identifies 50% as the economically optimal pathway to 2040. A strong target in the post-2030 Energy Union Package, backed by a strong ETS review and national implementation measures, would give investors the certainty they are asking for. The founders who told us they had considered leaving Europe are still building here, still hiring and still expecting to grow.  Europtimism is real, but it is conditional. It rests on founders and investors believing Europe will follow through, and the clearest measure of that belief will be whether fewer of them are thinking about the exit when we run the index again next year.
