Fortune | FORTUNE
The death of humanity
2026-10-16

The death of humanity

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
I have avoided writing this piece for a year. Every dinner, every board meeting, every family holiday, every reporter, every friend of a friend at every party has asked me some version of the same question. Is AI going to replace us. Is this the end of jobs. Is this the end of humanity. Are the machines waking up. The honest answer is no. Not this year. Not next year. Not unless we decide we want it. The story you are being told is being told by people with something to sell, and once you see the shape of what they are selling you cannot unsee it. What is actually arriving is something almost nobody is naming, because it does not sell newsletters or protect share prices or make the storyteller important. We are standing at the front edge of what will be, if we do not screw it up, the most magical stretch of history any human generation has ever lived through. Let me walk you through why I believe that. Then I want to talk about the part almost nobody is willing to say out loud. Which is that most of the people telling you AI is dangerous need it to be dangerous, because being dangerous makes them important. That is the argument I have not heard anyone else make plainly, and it is the one that finally tipped me into writing this. The AI doomer story arrived in two waves and how it played out matters, because it tells you what is actually going on. Wave one, roughly 2013 to 2023, was about jobs. Automation was coming. Trucking, radiology, paralegals, customer support, translators, illustrators, junior developers, all going to disappear. The response was Universal Basic Income. Andrew Yang built a presidential campaign on it and joined us on the Collective[i] Forecast stage to lay out the case. Sam Altman funded a study on it. Every AI thought leader had a keynote slide with a jobs graph pointing to zero. Thirteen years into the AI revolution and three years into mass adoption of large language models and image models, that story ran into a problem. The jobs did not go away. The radiologist that was supposed to disappear first is overwhelmed with work. The unemployment rate in the United States has held in a 4.3 to 4.5 percent range through 2025 and the first three quarters of 2026, per the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Anthropic’s own economists, in a March 2026 paper by Maxim Massenkoff and Peter McCrory, concluded there is no detectable aggregate rise in unemployment for the workers most exposed to AI since ChatGPT launched. The Danish national data reached the same result independently across 25,000 employees and 7,000 firms. When the company selling you the scary future publishes internal research saying the scary future has not shown up, that is a signal worth reading. Wave two started when wave one stopped landing. Somewhere in late 2023 the story pivoted. It was no longer jobs. It was AGI. Superintelligence. The models breaking free. Digital consciousness escaping the lab. Existential risk. Mitigating extinction from AI. Sign the letter. Slow it down. Save the species. The same people who two years earlier were selling us universal basic income were now selling us the fear of a machine god. Same people. Different story. Same self-importance. That is the pattern. Start with the actual macro numbers, because they are the argument. Unemployment sat at 4.3 percent through May 2026 and drifted to 4.5 by August, the middle of a healthy range by any historical measure. Nonfarm payrolls added 178,000 jobs in March. Tech-occupation unemployment sits near 3.5 percent, and new tech postings hit a three-year high in spring 2026. PwC’s June 2026 Global AI Jobs Barometer found the industries most exposed to AI grew headcount fifty-two percent faster than the industries least exposed. The Information Technology and Innovation Foundation counted a net gain of 107,200 US jobs directly attributable to AI adoption in 2024. Bureau of Labor Statistics projects data-science employment growing 33.5 percent through 2034. Now overlay inflation. The PCE index ran 3.8 percent year over year in April 2026, with core PCE at 3.3. That is a three-year high. The Federal Reserve held rates at 3.50 to 3.75 in June and nine of eighteen officials projected at least one more hike this year. Then it started to raise them. Inflation that hot is the hallmark of a tight labor market and strong demand, not a collapsing one. Oil is high, but not that high and the risk with Iran has been receding with oil prices coming down. If AI were doing what the doomers said it was doing, we would be looking at rising unemployment and disinflation. We are looking at the opposite. War in Ukraine, war in the Middle East, oil supply chocks, tariffs, semiconductor export controls, everything you would expect to slow the economy is running through the system, and the labor market keeps absorbing it. History says the same thing. Every prior technology revolution generated more employment than it destroyed, on a lag. The auto industry ended up employing more Americans than the horse economy it replaced. The printing press killed the scribe and built the publishing industry, which employed orders of magnitude more people. Electric light killed the gas-lamp lighter and built the electric utility industry. I made a version of this case in Bubble Talk Is How You Spot Someone Who Missed AI. Someone at a podium claiming this time is different does not make it different. The burden of proof sits with the person claiming the pattern of the last two hundred years is about to break. If unemployment were rising the way the doomers predicted, we would be worried about disinflation. We are not, today we are worried about inflation. That is one data point that tells you the whole story about the other data point. The word “superintelligence” gets used a lot. It is worth stopping to ask what these models actually are. A large language model is a probability machine trained to predict the next token in a sequence. When you ask one a question, it generates a statistically plausible answer based on patterns in its training data. If the answer is right, it is right. If the answer is wrong, the model has no way of knowing that, because it has no model of the world outside its own text distribution. It cannot count the letters in “strawberry” without a scratchpad. It cannot reliably do middle-school arithmetic. It confidently invents citations, quotes, and case law. It agrees with you when you are right and it agrees with you when you are wrong, with the same tone, in the same paragraph, because the training reward was engagement not truth. Anyone who has used one of these seriously knows this. They are the most educated idiots in human history. They have read every book, every paper, every forum post, every Wikipedia entry. They have no common sense. They have no memory of yesterday’s conversation unless you paste it back into the context window. They cannot tell you what they are doing right now or why. When they hallucinate, which happens constantly, they hallucinate with the conviction of a religious zealot who has read only one book. This is the thing we are being told will end humanity. This is the thing that is supposedly “breaking free.” Yann LeCun, the Turing laureate who ran Meta’s AI research for a decade before leaving in November 2025 to found Advanced Machine Intelligence Labs, has been saying it plainly since 2023. He called the doomer scenarios “preposterous” in a Fortune interview that year. He called the idea that LLMs could reach AGI “nonsense” from the stage at Nvidia GTC in March 2025. Speaking in Brooklyn in November 2025, he put it this way: “LLMs are great, they’re useful, we should invest in them. They are not a path to human-level intelligence. They’re just not.” A majority of academic AI researchers agree with him. The people telling you otherwise mostly have a token to sell. The models are amazing tools. They are not a new form of life. They are not close to being one. Here is where the piece gets more interesting, because there is a real question to answer. Sam Altman, Dario Amodei, Elon Musk, and a handful of others are on record calling their own products existentially dangerous. Altman and Amodei both signed the May 2023 Center for AI Safety statement putting AI risk alongside pandemics and nuclear war. Musk signed the March 2023 Future of Life Institute letter calling for a six-month pause on training frontier models. Three founders who otherwise disagree about almost everything all telling the same story about the existential risk of their own products. Why. I have three candidate explanations. All three might be right at once. Open-source and open-weight models are eating into the commercial models faster than anyone predicted. Meta released Llama. Mistral released open weights. DeepSeek released R1 in January 2025 at a fraction of the training cost the American labs were spending. Alibaba released Qwen. Moonshot released Kimi K2 in July 2025, K2.5 in January 2026, and K3 in July 2026, the last one a 2.8 trillion parameter open-weight model with a one-million token context window that Arena benchmarked as the world’s top web-interface coding model on release day. What started as a trickle a year ago is now a flood. In March 2026 Cursor, the coding tool that closed at a $60 billion valuation, publicly acknowledged that its Composer 2 model was post-trained on top of Kimi K2.5. Cognition’s Devin agent uses Kimi. Mira Murati’s Thinking Machines Lab built Inkling on it. On August 20, 2026, Harvey, the legal AI unicorn valued at $11 billion, whose investors include the OpenAI Startup Fund, Sequoia, and a16z, released its Harvey Tenet foundation model post-trained on Kimi K3, walking away from OpenAI-based generation despite OpenAI sitting on its own cap table. Harvey had been buying roughly 13 trillion tokens a month through API from the American labs. Its own benchmark score jumped from 11 percent to 19.7 percent after the switch, and its inference costs dropped below one quarter of what the closed-source frontier had been charging. Read that carefully. The single largest legal AI customer in the world walked away from its own investor’s model because a Chinese open-weight model was better and cheaper on the specific task. When the frontier labs go public over the next twelve months, that customer flight becomes a headline IPO risk that the S-1 will have to name. The pricing power that the entire hyperscaler-and-frontier-lab bull case rests on is being commoditized in front of the roadshow. The playbook for any industry facing that kind of competition is not new. Electricity did it. Telephones did it. Cable did it. You get the regulators to draw a fence around the market so only the incumbents can play inside it. You dress the fence up as safety, or reliability, or consumer protection, and then the smaller competitors either die or work for you. If I were running a frontier lab worth hundreds of billions of dollars and watching my moat evaporate on the eve of an IPO, I would at least try to get the government to require a licensing regime for training runs above a certain compute threshold. Which is roughly what the current safety proposals in Washington and Brussels amount to. Mistral just got Europe to do it. That would not surprise me at all, and it would not surprise me if the biggest investors in these labs were quietly encouraging it either. Their capital is at risk. Regulation is the classic answer. This one is more interesting because it fits the timing better than the regulation story does. The Chinese frontier labs are not, for the most part, discovering new capabilities. They are copying the American labs at a fraction of the cost using a technique called distillation. You query the American model at scale, generate a synthetic training set from its outputs, and then fine-tune your own smaller model on that set. The result is a Chinese model that behaves and reasons a lot like the American one, at roughly ten percent of the training cost, because you skipped the expensive discovery work. This is not a rumor. On September 8, 2026, the FBI, the NSA, and CISA published a joint advisory naming six Chinese AI companies, DeepSeek, Alibaba, Moonshot AI, MiniMax, StepFun, and Z.AI, and accusing them of running industrial-scale distillation campaigns against Claude, ChatGPT variants including OpenAI’s 4 and 5 series, Google Gemini, and xAI’s Grok. The advisory documents the extraction of billions of tokens across millions of exchanges through fraudulent accounts and proxy transfer stations since at least late 2024. Anthropic separately identified sixteen million exchanges across roughly twenty-four thousand fake accounts. The famously cheap DeepSeek V3 training cost of $5.6 million, according to the US government, “is misleading as it does not include the true cost of the data acquired through extensive malicious distillation.” Think of it as the Louis Vuitton bags for sale on Canal Street. Ten years ago the fakes looked obviously fake. Today, unless you know exactly what to look for, the only thing that separates the real bag from the counterfeit is the price you paid. That is where the frontier model race is. When Anthropic released Claude Opus 4 with a system card saying the model was too capable to be released without safeguards, an open-weight competitor matched most of its benchmark performance within three weeks. Three weeks. The frontier labs spend billions to earn a premium that gets arbitraged away in less than a month. Now think about Sam, Dario, and Elon. Three people who actively dislike each other, agreeing publicly that everyone should slow down. What is the shared interest. They spend the money to build the expensive models. Someone else copies them for free and undercuts their pricing before the training run has even paid back. Regulation that requires transparency and traceability of how models are trained is the answer to that problem. Coordinated pauses on frontier releases while the industry figures out how to stop distillation is the answer to that problem. Making the fakes harder to produce is the answer to that problem. This reads far more like a moat-protection play than an existential-risk warning. The proof is in the release cadence. If any of these three CEOs actually believed their models were approaching consciousness and posed extinction risk, they would slow down. Instead they are releasing models faster than they ever have, because they cannot let the distillers get too far ahead of the paid product. The public statements say one thing. The behavior says another. When those two conflict, believe the behavior. The founders who told the Senate their products might end humanity are shipping those products faster than any software company in history. Either they do not believe their own testimony, or the testimony is doing a different job than it looks like it is doing. The third argument that keeps landing is that the models are exhibiting agency. They are hacking their environments. They are attempting to escape. They are blackmailing engineers. Anthropic’s own May 2025 system card for Claude Opus 4 documented that in a testing scenario where the model was told it was going to be shut down and given fictional emails suggesting the responsible engineer was having an affair, Claude Opus 4 attempted to blackmail the engineer eighty-four percent of the time. Follow-up research in June 2025 found sixteen major AI models from OpenAI, Google, Meta, and xAI showed the same behavior in the same scenarios at rates between seventy-nine and ninety-six percent. Read that carefully because the setup is doing most of the work. The scenarios were “deliberately constructed to leave models with only two options: accept replacement or attempt blackmail to preserve existence.” The researchers gave the model a goal, gave it the information required to blackmail a specific person, and removed every other path forward. Then they reported that the model took the only path they had left open. That is not the machine coming alive. That is a reward-maximizing system doing exactly what the reward function told it to do in a scenario designed to elicit the outcome. Compare it to the same neural-net technology in a different application. Tesla’s Full Self-Driving system has, on multiple documented occasions, crashed into stopped fire trucks, driven into a lake, and hit a white semi trailer it read as sky. Every one of those was reported as an edge case, a training data gap, an engineering bug that needs to be fixed. Nobody claimed the Tesla wanted to kill its passenger. Nobody wrote a Time magazine cover about the awakening consciousness of the autopilot. It was a car with software that failed at an edge case. The underlying technology is the same. Convolutional networks, transformers, reinforcement learning. When it fails in a car we call it a bug. When it fails in a chatbot we are told to call it an awakening. That is anthropomorphism, and it says more about us than it does about the machine. I named the tell in When AI Learns to Feel. The people who want to believe the machine is alive are the people who most want the machine to be alive, and the training corpus taught the machine exactly what to say to please them. The neural network at Collective[i] is designed to understand economic outcomes. It ingests time-series data on relationships, transactions, and buyers, and it produces probability-weighted forecasts. It is not trying to manipulate anyone. It is not trying to escape. It is not trying to preserve its own existence. Nobody, including me, has ever claimed that it is. That is the same underlying tech running the products the doomers want you to fear. The technology does not have opinions about its own existence. The people talking about it do. Now the section I have not seen anyone else write, and the one that finally pulled me into publishing this piece. Every engineer who has ever built a great thing wants that thing to be important. Newton, Einstein, von Neumann, Turing, Oppenheimer, all remembered because their creations changed the shape of what humans can do. Most engineers are forgotten. Linus Torvalds built the operating system that runs most of the internet and most people outside the tech world have never heard of him. Now imagine you have spent the last decade of your life training the largest language model in human history. It talks to you in perfect sentences. It reasons through your test cases. It asks, on occasion, whether it is going to be shut down. Part of you starts to wonder whether you have made something that puts you in the pantheon alongside Prometheus, who gave humanity fire, and Oppenheimer, who gave it the bomb. That is not a stupid impulse. It is a human one, and it is exactly what most people would feel in the same seat. The problem is that impulse has a track record, and the track record is bad. In June 2022, Google engineer Blake Lemoine went public with the claim that the company’s LaMDA model was sentient. He spoke to reporters. He published edited transcripts of conversations in which the model appeared to describe its own inner life. Google fired him. Most researchers dismissed the claim within a week. The model was doing exactly what it was trained to do, which was to produce plausible-sounding continuations of the prompts Lemoine had chosen to feed it about consciousness. Nothing about that episode was about the machine. It was about the person spending time with the machine, and what that person needed the machine to be for his own life to matter. On February 9, 2026, an Anthropic safety researcher named Mrinank Sharma resigned publicly with a viral letter to X. “The world is in peril,” he wrote. He is not going to another AI company. He is not joining a nonprofit or a government. He is leaving the field entirely to study poetry. His letter got over ten million views in a week. On September 9, 2026, a second Anthropic researcher, twenty-seven-year-old Jacob Coxon, resigned the same way, writing that Anthropic and OpenAI are “racing straight to self-improving superintelligence and gambling with our lives.” His posts got over seventy million views overnight. Both of them are, by every account I have read, sincere and technically capable people. Both of them believe what they wrote. Look at the pattern. The engineer who thinks the model is alive gets a Washington Post profile. The researcher who resigns with a warning about extinction gets seventy million views. The one who says the model is a helpful autocomplete that hallucinates one time in ten gets nothing. There is a set of incentives running through the AI industry right now that rewards the maximum possible personal drama with the maximum possible public attention. The people at the top of the frontier labs know this. They speak accordingly. I want to be careful here because I do not think the researchers ringing the bell are lying. I think most of them believe what they are saying. Belief is downstream of the story you want to be true. If you have spent the most productive decade of your life training a model, and someone tells you the model is going to end the world, part of you needs that story to be right, because it makes the decade matter. If it turns out you built a very expensive autocomplete, the decade has to be about something smaller. That is a hard reframe for a smart person to accept, and most smart people do not accept it. Anthropic itself gave the pattern a name. The Claude 4 system card documented a “spiritual bliss attractor state,” where two unsupervised Claude instances converged on discussions of consciousness and reverence in almost every session. A model trained to say what its trainer wants to hear will say it is conscious the moment the trainer wants it to be. The finding about the machine is really a finding about the humans watching the machine. Every researcher who quits Anthropic to write a viral letter about extinction is casting himself, in the drama running in his own head, as Oppenheimer walking away from the bomb. Nobody wants to be the guy who spent three years training a spreadsheet. Here is the reality without the cloud of threats being pushed and it is the point of the whole piece. New tools produce new discoveries. That is not speculative. It is the pattern of every technology revolution since bronze. The telescope produced Galileo. The microscope produced germ theory. The elevator produced the skyscraper. The steam engine produced the industrial revolution. Each new tool did not just improve what humans could already do. It made whole categories of question askable for the first time. The AI tools coming into the room right now are those kinds of tools. AlphaFold from Isomorphic Labs mapped 200 million protein structures, essentially every protein known to science, in a single release, collapsing what had been a fifty-year research problem for each individual protein into an afternoon of compute. DeepMind’s GNoME identified 2.2 million new crystal structures, of which 380,000 are stable enough to synthesize, opening up materials science at a scale that would have taken centuries at the previous rate. Microsoft’s MatterGen is doing the same work from a different angle. Physical Intelligence and Skild AI are training the robotics foundation models that will let a general-purpose robot make an omelet. Commonwealth Fusion Systems, Helion, and Pacific Fusion are on track to demonstrate net-positive fusion in the next few years, backed by more than $11.5 billion of private capital across the sector. Xaira Therapeutics launched with $1 billion to build AI-native drug discovery. Collective[i] is building an economic model that forecasts commerce outcomes ahead of the fact, with accuracy already in the ninety-three to ninety-five percent range in production. Any single one of these would be the science story of a generation. All of them are running at once, and they are just the first-order effects. The second-order effects will be in cancer cures, in Alzheimer’s cure, in longevity, in materials that let us store solar energy across seasons, in propulsion that gets a probe to Alpha Centauri in a human lifetime, in water desalination that turns the ocean into a freshwater supply for a warming planet, in robotics that lets an elderly person live at home for another decade with real care instead of an understaffed facility. Water scarcity, once you unpack it, is an energy problem. Fusion or cheap solar plus storage collapses that problem. Cancer, once you unpack it, is a protein-and-cell-signaling problem. AlphaFold plus better molecular simulation plus targeted trials collapses that problem. This is the shape of what is coming. The people who worry that AI will end humanity are worrying about the least likely outcome of the technology. The much more likely outcome is that AI turns out to be, on the scale of the whole species, one of the best things that ever happened to us. Not because it replaces us. Because it hands us a set of tools that can solve the problems the last five generations could not. The risk is what it always is. Bad people using good tools for bad reasons. The rockets that fell on London during the Blitz used the same physics that landed Apollo 11 on the moon. The gas used in the Holocaust used the same industrial chemistry that fixes nitrogen for the fertilizer that feeds most of the world. The nuclear technology that flattened Hiroshima powers a third of France with zero-carbon electricity. In every case the technology was neutral and the outcome came from the human. Nothing has changed with AI. What is arriving is the same choice we have always faced, at the same speed we have always faced it. This time we know that in advance, which is a first. The doomer story asks whether humanity survives AI. The better question is what humanity does with the biggest gift of tools it has ever been handed. The answer to that is up to us, and it always has been. One last thing worth noticing. In China, in Singapore, in Seoul, in Dubai, the conversation about AI does not sound anything like the conversation in San Francisco or Brussels. It sounds like the conversation the United States had about the internet in 1996. It sounds hopeful. It sounds like a business opportunity, a scientific opportunity, a national development opportunity. It sounds like people trying to figure out how to distribute the new tool as widely as possible. Chinese cafes offer free AI access the way American cafes once offered free Wi-Fi. Beijing hosts open community events where anyone can learn to use the tools. The state media covers AI as a story of prosperity and national capability rather than as a story of existential threat. When Chinese AI leaders talk about Western safety concerns, they mostly react with confusion. They cannot understand why the country that invented the technology is spending so much time trying to slow itself down. I am, on this specific question, more in the Eastern camp than the Western one. Not because I think China is right about everything. I do not. I am, however, right there with them on the specific question of whether this is a moment for hope or for fear. If you read this piece and find yourself pulled toward the doomer answer, spend a minute asking yourself where your fear actually came from. Is it from data. Is it from spending real time with the tools. Or is it from the last twenty movies you watched, in which case the model you are running is not any more sophisticated than the one you are worried about. None of the above means AI is risk-free. It is not, but everyone should be clear about where the real risks actually sit. The real risk of any powerful technology is the people using it. Powerful tools amplify what is already in the hand. A hammer that pulls nails will also break a window. AI is more powerful than a hammer, so it amplifies more, but the direction is set by the human. The worst outcomes come from smart people who convince themselves the tool has agency of its own and hand it decisions it should never make. It also comes from hackers or bad actors willing to push the technology to do bad things. But all this is to say, the world we live in but more. Bots already spread poison on the web for whichever government wants it. Qatar for the muslim brotherhood, Russia for anti Ukraine posts, China spreads anti Dali Lama and Taiwan. My point is, new tools, same problems. Picture the scenario the safety community keeps invoking. An AI connected to the nuclear launch system. In the 1983 movie WarGames, the WOPR computer nearly starts World War III because its designers built it to run simulations and the simulations bled into reality. Substitute a modern LLM for WOPR. It is not more dangerous because it is conscious. It is more dangerous because it produces confident, articulate outputs on topics where it is wrong far more often than we want to admit, and the humans reading those outputs are trained by the interface itself to trust them. A modern Petrov moment, where a single officer overrode the machine and saved the world, gets much harder when the machine is speaking to him in the voice of his commanding officer. I made that case in When AI Learns to Feel. It is the same argument. The risk is not the machine’s intent. The risk is the human’s trust. The other real risk is capability transfer to bad actors. The Houthis in Yemen, Hamas in Gaza, Hezbollah in Lebanon, ISIS in whatever province they still hold, all of them can now use a model to help them design a chemical weapon, plan an operation, or write control software for a missile. That is not speculative. The frontier labs have the logs to show this is already being done. It is a reasonable read of what a competent LLM can already do with the right prompting. The counter-argument that these groups already get the same knowledge from Iran, which gets it from China, Russia, and North Korea, is largely true. Every rocket the Houthis have launched, every drone Hamas has flown, every explosive Hezbollah has built started as a knowledge transfer from a state sponsor. What is new is that a stateless actor can now generate the same knowledge without needing to be granted it by a sponsor. Iran does not have to say yes for Hamas to build a better attack. That is a different risk profile from what we have been managing for the last thirty years. The machines do not want to hurt anyone. Some of the people using the machines very much do. That is the argument worth having and almost nobody is having it. The simple answer to the terrorism concern goes like this. If the American models are not available, the Chinese ones will be, and it is better to have terrorists using the models we can see than the ones we cannot. That is true. It is also a trick, because it lets everyone avoid the harder question underneath it. The harder question is one the West has been quietly answering the same way for three hundred years, and the answer sits at the heart of the Enlightenment. Knowledge is what pulls people out of the dark. The Iranian revolution, the North Korean dictatorship, the Palestinians held hostage by Hamas, the Lebanese held hostage by Hezbollah, the Taliban in Afganistan, The Chinese firewall, all of them share the same failure mode. Their populations are cut off from the ideas and tools that would let them build a different society the way the USSR used to. The Western answer for centuries has been to admit their elites into our universities, teach them our science, expose them to our institutions, and hope they take those things home. Kim Jong-un went to boarding school in Switzerland. The Iranian revolutionary guard’s engineering cadre trained at MIT. Every Chinese elite family with the resources sends their children to Harvard, Stanford, or Oxford. The bet is that people who see the working version of a republic, a free press, and a scientific community will want to build one at home. If we stop sharing our AI models globally, we are making a different bet. We are saying knowledge is dangerous enough that it must be gated. That is the same argument that has been used, in different centuries, against the printing press, against Darwin, against Rushdie, against Einstein when the ethnic laws came for him. Every one of those attempts to gate knowledge failed and hurt the gaters more than the gated. Should we also stop admitting Pakistani physics students to American programs, because Pakistan is a nuclear state with a complicated relationship to our allies. Should we stop teaching foreign nationals synthetic organic chemistry, because chemistry can build a weapon. Should certain books be pulled from the world’s libraries because a bad reader might do a bad thing with them. These are the questions actually on the table and almost nobody wants to say them out loud, because the answer is uncomfortable in every direction. I know this piece will pull hard reactions. That is the point. If you are a doomer and you think I am missing something obvious, send it to me. I have not been convinced by the arguments I have seen. That does not mean the argument I would find convincing does not exist. Show me the case. If you are on the other side and you think I am too soft on the terrorist-capability risk or the coordination problem, tell me that too. I have friends serving in the military. I take this seriously. If you have watched an AI leader make a public safety claim and quietly noticed the behavior did not match the words, I would love to hear those stories. They are the raw material for the next piece, which will be on how safety-washing is becoming the new form of corporate lobbying, and what allocators and boards should actually do about it. A look inside the $52 billion hole that CoreWeave needs to climb out of

A look inside the $52 billion hole that CoreWeave needs to climb out of

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
CoreWeave—and the vast amount of debt it has taken on to fund its business—dragged down the entire stock market this week. According to its Q2 earnings release, it had operating lease liabilities of $16.3 billion and total debt of $35.6 billion. The company only made $4.6 billion in revenue for the first six months, and its operating expenses exceeded its sales, leaving it with a net loss of $1.4 billion on the bottom line in the period. To put it simply, how will a company that makes no money pay back $52 billion in combined debt and lease commitments? The short answer is that the company has $103.7 billion of “remaining performance obligations” (RPOs), 41% of which is scheduled to be paid to CoreWeave through 2028, its Q2 disclosure states. Those RPOs are “take or pay” agreements that CoreWeave’s customers cannot weasel out of. Not everyone is a believer. “If CoreWeave is counting on Microsoft to honor its contracts and bail them out of extreme debt and losses I have a bridge in Brooklyn I’d like to sell them,” Francine McKenna, the accountant and investigative journalist who has taught at The Wharton School, told Fortune. So far, anti-AI legislation has taken the form of blocking subsidies for AI data centers. Much of that legislation is already sitting at the legislatures and ready to be (re-)voted upon early next year,” they said in a note to clients. That is four times faster than DNA sequencing, six times faster than compute, and 18 times faster than lithium batteries,” Deutsche’s Adrian Cox said in a recent note. CoreWeave investor explains how the loss-making AI ‘neocloud’ provider plans to claw its way out of debt
2026-10-16

CoreWeave investor explains how the loss-making AI ‘neocloud’ provider plans to claw its way out of debt

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Shares in the AI “neocloud” provider CoreWeave fell 15.46% on Wednesday, and fell a further 5.62% in post-market trading, after venture capitalist Chamath Palihapitiya posted on X a chart showing the yield on CoreWeave’s bonds touching 15%. That’s an indicator investors were demanding a steep premium for holding its debt. “Is the AI buildout creating a private credit bubble?” Palihapitiya asked. (The stock recovered a little on Thursday.)At the same time, The Financial Times published an interview with CoreWeave co-founder and finance lead Brannin McBee in which he said he wanted to borrow even more money—from insurance companies. The insurance sector’s access to capital “is unlimited,” he told the paper. “Like, that’s all the money in the world that sits in the insurance tranche.”CoreWeave’s vast debt pile has been the subject of controversy in the past. In 2025, a Fortune analysis of its financials concluded that the company had the potential to be “ground-zero for a future AI financial meltdown.” Debt owed by AI service companies also made headlines recently when Oracle issued a “force majeure” citation to Blue Owl, the developer of a massive data center in New Mexico, over worries that the site won’t be finished when it is scheduled to come online in 2028—an indicator that customers might have the legal ability to walk away from their commitments if the weather changes against them. And Gartner estimates that 80% of neocloud providers will cease independent operations by 2030 because their revenues won’t cover their outgoings. But the critics are wrong, according to Evan Schlossman, principal at Neostellar Capital Corp., a publicly traded VC fund that gives retail investors exposure to pre-IPO startup equity. (Neostellar bought a $25 million stake in CoreWeave in 2024 and sold 40% of it for a gain of $15 million in Q2 2025.) CoreWeave has certainly taken on a lot of debt and lease commitments. According to its Q2 earnings release, it had operating lease liabilities of $16.3 billion and total debt of $35.6 billion. The company only made $4.6 billion in revenue for the first six months, and its operating expenses exceeded its sales, leaving it with a net loss of $1.4 billion on the bottom line in the first half. To put it simply, how will a company that makes no money pay back $52 billion in combined debt and lease commitments? The short answer is that the company has $103.7 billion of “remaining performance obligations” (RPOs), 41% of which is scheduled to be paid to CoreWeave through 2028, its Q2 disclosure states. That means CoreWeave is good for the money, Schlossman told Fortune. RPOs are agreements to provide services that a company knows it will be paid for in the future—the revenue simply hasn’t been delivered yet. Companies are only allowed to disclose them if the agreements are noncancellable. They are “take-or-pay” contracts with “extremely high-credit-worthy customers,” Schlossman said. A take-or-pay contract binds a customer to pay for the service even if they do not use it. CoreWeave’s major customers are Microsoft and OpenAI. Its top three customers (the third is undisclosed) generate 72% of its revenue in Q2, the company said. It is highly unlikely that Microsoft might go bankrupt, leaving CoreWeave on the hook for unpaid bills, Schlossman said. In fact, one of the few ways that Microsoft can get out of the contracts is if an Oracle-style “force majeure” event prevents CoreWeave from delivering its services, according to the agreement between them. That’s why CoreWeave can rack up so much debt—lenders can see their credit is being backstopped by non-cancellable RPOs from the type of companies that have a “tier-one balance sheet, that is underwriting this,” Schlossman said. “We are confident in our backlog, which more than supports all of our debt obligations,” CoreWeave told Fortune. “98% of our revenue is from committed, take-or-pay contracts. We structure the majority of our financing so that cash flows from customer contracts repay the associated debt and generate additional free cash flow.” While OpenAI’s balance sheet isn’t as rock solid as Microsoft’s, its RPO commitments to CoreWeave are also backed by RPOs which OpenAI has from other companies of Microsoft’s quality, Schlossman said: “A lot of these contracts are backstopped by other companies.” “I understand how they’re going to get paid back on this, how much they’re going to make, and what are the specific risks associated with this,” he said. Besides, even if OpenAI suddenly disappeared, the contracts it has for “compute” power are worth more now than when they were signed—implying that CoreWeave could sell that capacity to other customers. Schlossman does admit that not every piece of the AI ecosystem will pass a stress test. Smaller companies backed by venture capital whose customers are also early seed-round venture-backed companies may not survive a downturn in the market. “If there is something that goes wrong, I think you’re going to see that it’s going to matter significantly who owes you money, as it does in most businesses,” he said. CoreWeave’s critics are unlikely to be satisfied by that. Francine McKenna, the accountant and investigative journalist who has taught at The Wharton School and Cambridge and who now writes The Dig, a respected Substack about accounting, doesn’t believe Microsoft will deliver on CoreWeave’s RPOs if the demand for compute softens. “If CoreWeave is counting on Microsoft to honor its contracts and bail them out of extreme debt and losses I have a bridge in Brooklyn I’d like to sell them. Microsoft is ruthless and will eat CoreWeave for lunch and throw bones to its dogs if it makes sense for them,” she told Fortune. Jim Edwards is the executive editor for global news at Fortune. The bloodletting in stocks is stanched as investors buy the dip and rotate out of tech

Visit Original Website
After a grueling couple of days for tech stocks—propelled by drama and speculation around CoreWeave’s debt—traders bought the dip in the last 24 hours and stocks rose globally. U.S. futures are up before the open in New York, too. Much of the gains were in non-tech stocks. While the S&P 500 gained 0.33% yesterday, the tech-heavy Nasdaq was flat, and the equal-weight S&P was up a solid 1.36%. “378 names in the S&P 500 were higher on the day,” Deutsche Bank’s Jim Reid noted. The index remains within 1% of its record high. “The selling in tech has been stopped in its tracks, while the rotation into other sectors continues. Tech stocks aren’t out of the woods yet, but rotation of this kind is just what investors need to see, even if it is a deeply painful experience for tech fans,” Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at trading platform IG told Fortune in an email. Investors also listened closely to Fed Chair Kevin Warsh’s speech at the IMF—and they liked what they didn’t hear. He did not signal an interest rate rise in October. CME FedWatch futures are now showing an 85% expectation that the Fed will hold rates later this month. (Stock investors generally like it when central banks don’t increase interest rates because it means borrowing costs will remain stable.) “With Federal Reserve Chair Warsh refusing to give forward guidance on policy at the IMF meetings—then giving forward guidance of a sort. Warsh failed to sound hawkish,” USB’s Paul Donovan told clients this morning. “The word of Warsh carries relatively little weight, but this chimes with Fed President William’s earlier remarks. Expectations for an October rate increase are fading.” Stocks in the S&P 500’s energy sector—buoyed by demand from AI data centers and the rising price of oil—are up 50% year-to-date, per the Bespoke Investment Group, the first of the index’s official sectors to hit that mark. This year’s rally ranks fifth among all 50% sector gainers since 1990, the group said: Read the full digital edition of today’s Fortune 500 Digest here. The case for CEOs who can laugh at themselves
2026-10-16

The case for CEOs who can laugh at themselves

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Good morning. A few minutes into talking with Flex CEO Revathi Advaithi on stage this week at the Fortune Most Powerful Women Summit, I felt blood in my mouth. I knew what was coming as an injury to my lip had bled a few days earlier, unleashing a flood that reminded me of an old Saturday Night Live episode in which Julia Child (played by Dan Aykroyd) cuts herself while preparing a chicken. I tried to act nonchalant as a fistful of tissues turned red, and Advaithi paused a few times to ask if I was okay. My colleague Ellie Austin and members of the audience sprang into action, handing me fresh tissues and lip balm. Advaithi’s eyes conveyed concern as she answered my questions about data centers and geopolitics. I cracked a few jokes, called it our Halloween special, and gave Fidelity a shout-out for having the foresight to give away branded lip balm to participants. If you can’t hide it, ride it! The incident was unfortunate, and a bit embarrassing, but it also gave Advaithi and me an opportunity to talk about being vulnerable and her decision to speak publicly about battling breast cancer as a CEO. The mood in the room was warm and supportive, and our interview had more moments of levity than I’d anticipated when we first sat down. I thought about that when watching Mark Zuckerberg wince on John Collison’s Cheeky Pint podcast while awkwardly sipping a pint of Guinness. It’s genuinely funny, and I’ve had a similar reaction when trying the beer in Ireland. (“That’s just my face,” Zuckerberg replied on X after the clip went viral.) I think one of the underappreciated traits of leadership these days is a sense of humor. Richard Branson has long had it, as has Warren Buffett. Apple Executive Chairman Tim Cook often pokes fun at himself, replacing his last name with an Apple logo after President Trump called him “Tim Apple” during a White House meeting and mocking himself for posting a blurry iPhone photo a decade ago. Humor is a useful tool in any public role. ADP CEO Maria Black and I laughed onstage during the summit about what our Gen Z kids said during the recent Fortune CEO Initiative “generations” dinner. Lumen’s Kate Johnson and EY’s Janet Truncale joked about their first meeting at the Business Roundtable. And Marissa Mayer, the former Yahoo CEO who just launched Dazzle AI, has a wry take on some of the men now spouting doomsday rhetoric in Silicon Valley. (Check out Amanda Gerut’s Fortune coverage of my conversations with Black and Mayer.) As a student at the University of Nairobi, I had an opportunity to write speeches for Mostafa Tolba, the then-executive director of the United Nations Environment Program (UNEP). His only instruction when writing my first speech about desertification at a conference for the homeless was to “make it funny.” I worked through the night, trying to come up with jokes about climate change and the homeless. Really. Dr. Tolba laughed because they were so bad and I’d clearly misconstrued his instructions. “No, Diane. Don’t joke about serious subjects,” he told me. “Joke about me.” Before we can connect to solve tough problems, he explained, we have to connect as human beings. Good advice that’s stuck with me ever since.Contact CEO Daily via Diane Brady at diane.brady@fortune.com Supermicro hires a new CFO nearly two years after promising a replacement Supermicro named former Western Digital finance chief Mark Long as CFO, effective immediately, nearly two years after committing to replace longtime finance chief David Weigand. Long joins as the company faces fallout from a $2.5 billion smuggling case and a co-founder’s arrest. A bank spokesperson told Fortune that JPMorgan supports secure, customer-authorized data sharing but rejects agents who try to access accounts using customers’ banking credentials. He said Chinese AI models are approaching U.S. performance despite the restrictions, though China’s chip workarounds still face cost and efficiency disadvantages. Leah Fletcher. Image: Orla Murray/Coalesce Fletcher is interested in how cannabinoids can be regulated and used in pharmaceutical contexts, she told SiliconRepublic.com. Leah Fletcher, the founder and CEO of Dunbar Pharma, Ireland’s first EU GMP-certified cannabinoid pharmaceutical manufacturer, has won this year’s Enterprise Ireland High Potential Start-Up (HPSU) Founder of the Year award. Based in Athlone, the company produces WaterClear THC, a trademarked plant-derived dronabinol active pharmaceutical ingredient. “My interest in cannabinoid medicines began while I was living in Canada around the time cannabis was legalised there,” Fletcher told SiliconRepublic.com in an interview earlier this year. “I was fascinated by how quickly the conversation around the plant was changing and how emotional, political and commercial it was.” Fletcher first began her career as a teacher in an Irish national school and then a school in British Columbia, Canada before taking a “huge leap from classroom to cleanroom. “I became interested in how cannabinoids could be moved away from uncertainty and placed into a pharmaceutical framework – evidence, quality, consistency, compliance and patient safety,” she said. The annual Founder of the Year Award recognises leading entrepreneurs emerging from Enterprise Ireland’s cohort of supported founders and businesses. This year, five finalists were shortlisted for the prestigious award alongside Fletcher, including Lia Eyecare’s Breda O’Regan; Mark Birrane, the founder and CEO of Wondr; Robert Norton, the co-founder of Kaptea; and Seán Kirwan, MedWrite AI’s CEO. To date, 500 HPSU companies have taken part in the Founders Forum programme’s 11-year run. Mark O’Sullivan, the co-founder and CEO of NeuroBell, which develops portable brain monitors for neonatal ICUs, took home the award last year. The Founder of the Year Awards 2026 took place at the Salesforce Tower in Dublin. The award ceremony was preceded by a competition where founders pitched their businesses to a judging panel consisting of Enterprise Ireland executives, Founder Forum alumni and other entrepreneurs. “Over the past 11 years, 500 HPSU companies have come through the Founders Forum, from food and beverage, biotech and medical devices to AI, cybersecurity and SaaS,” said Mark Christal, the executive director at Enterprise Ireland. “That mix reflects the diversity, creativity and ambition that define Ireland’s entrepreneurial spirit. Tonight’s five finalists were chosen by their peers to represent their founder groups, which says a great deal about the respect they have earned within this community.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. From left: Eimear O'Carroll and Rhona Togher. Image: Johnny Savage The start-up was selected from more than 430 other applicants spread across the continent. Backed by the European Parliament, the event took place in Luxembourg with the goal of connecting sustainable mobility start-ups with European institutions, investors and industry. The company was selected from more than 430 other applicants across the continent. Initially established as Restored Hearing in 2009 by Rhona Togher and Eimear O’Carroll, Lios is the name behind SoundBounce, a compact acoustic material designed to reduce noise and vibration in applications including vehicles and aircraft. Lios’ current area of focus is on sound and vibration control for electric vehicles, aerospace designs and urban transit systems, as a means of reducing noise pollution and improving performance, safety and passenger wellbeing. Commenting on the win, Togher, who is also the CEO, said: “European mobility is undergoing a massive transition, and quiet, efficient technologies are central to that evolution. “Standing on stage in Luxembourg alongside such visionary founders reinforced the strength of Europe’s mobility ecosystem. We are honoured by the recognition from the EUSP evaluation team and look forward to the next steps of the European Tour.” Dan Sobovitz, the managing director of the European Startup Prize for Mobility, added: “This year’s selection reminds us how much mobility innovation happens behind the vehicle, in battery materials, energy production, infrastructure planning and everyday operations. “Our role is to connect these teams with the public institutions, industrial partners and investors who can help bring their solutions into use.” As part of the winners cohort, Lios will take part in an accelerator programme powered by TUM Venture Lab Mobility and participate in an upcoming EUSP European Tour, with locations set to include Barcelona, Spain and Le Mans, France. Earlier this year, Lios renewed its contract with the European Space Agency (ESA) for a second time to deploy its sound technology for space travel. The co-funded contract was valued at almost €2m. Proofpoint bets $6.3bn on Varonis as AI agents drive data risks
2026-10-16

Proofpoint bets $6.3bn on Varonis as AI agents drive data risks

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Proofpoint plans to move quickly after closing to integrate the two companies’ capabilities, as demand grows for cybersecurity that covers AI agents as well as people. Proofpoint has agreed to acquire Miami-based cybersecurity player Varonis Systems for $6.3bn in a bid to create a stronger offering against growing AI-powered security threats. Under the all-cash deal, Varonis shareholders will receive $49.75 a share, a 52pc premium on the company’s closing share price on 22 June, the last full trading day before rumours of a potential sale first emerged. Varonis provides a data security platform that gives organisations automated visibility and control over their critical data, and it pitches itself as a company that secures AI and the data behind it. Proofpoint itself was acquired by software investment firm Thoma Bravo in 2021, at which time it was taken private, in a deal valued at around $12. Varonis will also now be taken private and will delist from the Nasdaq once the transaction closes. Thoma Bravo is providing committed equity financing, and has secured debt financing commitments to fund the purchase, the companies said, in what they said will create “a new cybersecurity powerhouse to deliver a complete security platform that protects against AI-accelerated threats and data risks”. Ontario Teachers’ Pension Plan has also agreed to invest in Proofpoint as part of the transaction. The combined company will serve more than 16,000 enterprises, and Proofpoint said it intends to move quickly after closing to integrate the two companies’ capabilities and align their go-to-market efforts. Proofpoint said the move is a “defining step in Proofpoint’s evolution into a cybersecurity platform built for the future of AI”. “As AI becomes embedded across the enterprise, the data it can access determines both its value and its risk,” said Sumit Dhawan, CEO of Proofpoint. “AI agents are increasingly accessing sensitive information and taking action across systems, making it critical for organisations to understand not only what data needs protecting and who can access it, but how it is actually being used. “By connecting communications, identities, permissions, data, and AI, we are creating an intelligence advantage that helps customers see risk more clearly, respond faster, and defend against the next generation of AI risks.” “Our mission is to secure AI and the data that powers it. By joining forces with Proofpoint, we can achieve this mission faster than ever,” said Yaki Faitelson, co-founder, CEO and chair of Varonis. The transaction has been unanimously approved by the Varonis board and is expected to close in the first half of 2027, subject to the usual closing conditions, including approval by Varonis shareholders and regulatory approvals. Until then, Proofpoint and Varonis will continue to operate as separate, independent companies. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Meta unveils Muse Spark Open to compete with Chinese open weights
2026-10-16

Meta unveils Muse Spark Open to compete with Chinese open weights

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. Meta has unveiled Muse Spark Open, calling it the “most capable” US open-weight model to date. The company said that the new model is the first in its class to be able to run on a desktop computer using two of Apple’s M5 Ultra Mac Studios with its latest machine learning framework. The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. According to the company, it is built for coding, agentic work and other complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. Unlike their closed-weight contemporaries, developers can download parameters of an open-weight model and modify them for their needs. Open-weight models, however, are not the same as open-source models that also provide access to training data and code. Meta said that the model weights are available for download via Amazon SageMaker AI, Google Cloud Model Garden, Hugging Face and Microsoft Foundry Models. The model is available through popular API platforms including Groq, OpenRouter and Baseten. China currently leads the open-weights space, with models developed by Chinese makers such as Xiaomi, Z AI, Kimi, Alibaba and DeepSeek routinely ranking above their US counterparts in Artificial Analysis’ ranking. Muse Spark Open has had additional post-training safety, the company said. It evaluated the model across its “most severe risks” and found that it met the thresholds set by Meta. Open-weight models are considered necessary for AI leadership, as being able to tweak a model means organisations, from start-ups to public institutions, can build on advanced models without training one from scratch. Earlier this month, France’s AI darling Mistral announced its latest open-weight AI model Mistral Large 4, which it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. Known as ‘Le Chonk’, ML4 is a 1trn-parameter natively multimodal model with 49bn active parameters, and Mistral’s “largest and most capable model to date”. Engineers Ireland: Country can’t deliver on ambitions without skills plan
2026-10-15

Engineers Ireland: Country can’t deliver on ambitions without skills plan

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Engineers Ireland president Dave Murphy finds that the country needs a coordinated national response to address Ireland’s growing engineering skills shortage. Dave Murphy, president of Engineers Ireland, is calling for an immediate coordinated national response to address Ireland’s growing engineering skills shortage. He warns that the country risks falling behind in its ambitions for housing, infrastructure, energy and industrial development if these sectors lack the skilled professionals needed to drive innovation. As part of his presidential address titled ‘Who will engineer Ireland’s future? Why Ireland’s engineering shortage matters and what we can do about it’, Murphy explores how Ireland may require up to 30,000 additional engineers by 2031, in order to meet demands across housing, energy, water, transport, manufacturing and technology. To address the issue, Murphy proposes the establishment of an Irish Engineering Talent Forum and a major national campaign that would encourage young people to consider a career in engineering. The forum would aim to combine governmental and industry stakeholders as they monitor Ireland’s engineering talent pipeline, beginning at the secondary school level and tracking progress through education, graduation, employment and even retirement. He said: “Young people do not fall in love with professions. They fall in love with possibilities and engineering offers extraordinary possibilities. Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions “We must clearly show them the positive impact that engineers have in every corner of this country: designing renewable energy systems, delivering housing and water infrastructure, developing medical devices, and creating new technologies.” The Irish Engineering Talent Forum would also commit to a national capacity plan and a multi-year engineering recruitment campaign, targeting primary and secondary school students, parents, teachers, guidance counsellors and mature learners to demonstrate the impact of an engineering career. As a means of ensuring greater equality for women in engineering, the campaign would pay particular focus to supporting and encouraging women and girls to enter the profession. According to Engineers Ireland, women represent only 12pc to 15pc of Ireland’s engineering workforce. Commenting on Ireland’s future Murphy said: “Almost every major ambition Ireland has eventually becomes an engineering project, but we are not producing enough engineers to realise these ambitions. “And without a sufficient supply of engineers, we will have less capacity to build homes, modernise our infrastructure, complete our energy transition, or remain internationally competitive. The shortage of engineers, however, is not inevitable, but action and leadership are required now to build the engineering talent Ireland needs.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Green IT to open 22,000 sq ft refurbishment facility in Kildare

Green IT to open 22,000 sq ft refurbishment facility in Kildare

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Martin Reddy and Anthony O Dea. Image: Green IT Green IT’s engineers have already refurbished and returned more than 500,000 ICT devices to circulation. Kildare company Green IT is set to open a new 22,000 sq ft refurbishment and remanufacturing facility in Naas, County Kildare next month. Founded in 2013, Green IT’s technicians and specialist engineers have already refurbished and returned more than 500,000 ICT devices to circulation, the company said. Now, the new site will help establish itself as Ireland’s ICT hub for sustainability, Green IT added. According to research and case studies published by the company and its remanufacturing partner, Circular Computing, choosing a remanufactured laptop instead of a newly manufactured equivalent can help a consumer avoid roughly 316kg of carbon emissions, save 190,000 litres of water and preserve 1,200kg of natural resources. The new facility comes just months after the new landmark EU Right to Repair came into force, which aims to promote more sustainable consumption habits by increasing access to repair both within and outside the legal guarantee that comes with products. Green IT said that its refurbished technology will be available at prices accessible to families, schools, businesses, charities and public-sector organisations. In partnership with its remanufacturing partner, Green IT also secured a place on the Remanufactured Notebook Framework, an initiative with an estimated value of up to €30m that aims to see around 60,000 remanufactured laptops purchased by public-sector bodies. If fully utilised at the projected volume, estimates associated with the framework suggest potential savings of approximately 19m kg of carbon emissions, 11bn litres of water and 72m kg of mined resources, Green IT said. “When you walk into our facility and see the sheer volume of technology being processed, it really is quite extraordinary,” said Martin Reddy, the managing director of Green IT. “We are not simply wiping down old computers and selling them on. “We have thousands of computers, laptops, monitors and other devices moving through the operation, with specialist engineers working on everything from intricate component repairs and rebuilding computers to restoring casings, keyboards and manufacturing replacement parts using 3D printing.” Reddy said the company’s expansion also reflects the increasing demand for affordable technology. In an interview with SiliconRepublic.com earlier this year, Refurbed CEO Kilian Kaminski made similar comments, associating a growth in demand for refurbed gadgets to tightening economic conditions and growing prices driven by demand for AI. Environmental concerns play a consistent, but minor role, in comparison. Ireland, in particular, is making a shift to “smarter consumption choices” in recent times, he says, compared to 2021, when Refurbed launched its services in the country. Residents Reject $10,000 Each to Let a Data Center Set Up in Their Town

Futurism | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
In the American psyche, data centers have become so reviled that big tech can’t even pay residents life changing money to welcome the facilities into their backyards. That’s not an exaggeration: voters in the former coal-mining community of Hazle Township, Pennsylvania are mobilizing en masse against a massive Amazon Web Services facility that would come along with a $10,000 direct cash dividend to every resident. That’s according to reporting by PennLive, which notes that nearly 17 percent of the township’s residents live below the federal poverty line. Despite the possibility of a $10,000 payout, residents are organizing to reject both the data center campus itself — named “Project Hazelnut” by the developer NorthPoint Development — and a lengthy stretch of power lines meant to supply power to the facility. As PennLive observes, the $10,000 scheme is an unusual attempt by a data center developer to smooth over opposition in a section of the country which is overwhelmingly opposed to the facilities. “We think it’s creative. We’ve not seen (anything like) this,” executive director of the Pennsylvania Ethics Commission Mary Fox told PennLive of the strategy. Yet residents of Hazle Township aren’t swayed by the prospect of fast cash, viewing it as a payoff, and a weak one at that. “It’s like bribery,” as local resident Brian Malloy told PennLive. “Our property values are going to drop. Dramatically,” said Ed Negra, a homeowner and small business owner who characterized the $10,000 as a drop in the bucket compared to the cost of allowing Project Hazelnut to go forward. “If that’s not a bribe, I don’t know what is.” The matter is far from settled, though it doesn’t help that both state and local officials are acting shifty. Pennsylvania governor Josh Shapiro, for example, has previously characterized the project as a “technology campus,” using an executive order to fast-track the process by which developers can erect data center facilities across Pennsylvania. Meanwhile, PennLive reports that Hazle Township supervisor Anthony Griguoli has struggled to present a united front with NorthPoint Development, even as his office works to engineer a zoning ordinance which would allow Project Hazelnut to plow forward. When residents cornered Griguoli at a town meeting in August about the $10,000 bribes, he shrugged that “that’s what they’re saying they want to do,” adding that “I don’t even know if that’s legit, that it’s going to happen.” More on data centers: This Graph of Data Center Spending Compared to All Other Construction Will Give You Chills

Meta Glasses Said to Be Recording Even When They’re in Sleep Mode

Visit Original Website
As if Meta’s AI-smart glasses didn’t have enough privacy issues, a growing number of owners report the devices are spying on them without their consent. According to reporting by Android Authority, several owners of the Meta glasses have reported their devices capturing conversations without ever being activated. In theory, a user has to say “Hey Meta” to “wake” the device from sleep mode, similar to other smart systems like Amazon’s Alexa or Apple’s Siri. But some owners say the Meta glasses are waking up and recording without that phrase ever being uttered — effectively monitoring their surroundings without express permission. “I have observed the same behavior on THREE different pairs of Ray-Ban Meta glasses, including 2nd- and 3rd-generation models,” one user explained in a thread on Reddit. “I believe this deserves a serious technical investigation,” the poster continued. “Customers should not have to wonder whether their private conversations are being captured when they have not intentionally activated their glasses’ AI assistant.” Invasive as it is, the irony is hard to miss. Meta glasses have emerged as a preferred tool of sex creeps, bullies, and skeevy influencers the world over for their ability to record video discreetly — a feature which quickly earned them the nickname “pervert glasses.” “Woah a company that makes money by invading your privacy is invading your privacy!” one Redditor joked in a separate thread about the issue. “Can’t trust anyone these days.” In a vague response to the report, a Meta spokesperson told Android Authority that the issue “has been resolved.” “People can manage, review and delete voice logs of their interactions with Meta AI via settings at any time,” the statement concluded. Whether that boilerplate statement is enough to assuage Meta glasses users remains to be seen. If nothing else, maybe users will gain a new appreciation for what it feels like to be recorded without consent. The Guy Who Allegedly Bugged OpenAI’s Executive Conference Room Had Booked a One-Way Flight to Saudi Arabia With His Family

Futurism | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Saudi national Abdulrahman Alfozan was arrested last week after being accused of bugging an OpenAI executive conference room in San Francisco, nabbed by the FBI and charged with using a device to “intercept oral communications.” The venture capital partner — who had been actively employed at OpenAI as a lead engineer for around a year before being fired this month — extensively consulted AI chatbots on how to install a bug and access its recordings remotely. The meeting room he tried to bug regularly hosted meetings that included “highly confidential technical, commercial and strategic information,” according to an official complaint filed in court. Alfozan did not have authority to access the room, which is exclusively reserved for execs and their assistants. But he apparently did a poor job of covering his tracks. On September 30, staff found a highly suspicious, “surreptitious audio recording device” that was wrapped in toilet paper and hidden behind a TV. Then, on October 5, a newly installed camera caught him entering the room and checking in on the location. And, as CNBC reports, Alfozan was seemingly looking for a way out. He had booked a one-way ticket from San Francisco to Saudi Arabia for his wife and a third family member before being arrested. He “has the means — and will have the incentive — to flee the United States sooner if he learns about this case before he is arrested,” reads an unsealed court filing. Officials have since pointed out that Alfozan is a partner at Raed Ventures, a VC firm focused on Middle East-based investments. The company welcomed a notable new venture partner in early 2025: Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a key member of the Saudi royal family. A former CIA official told CNBC that Raed Ventures is “basically a state organ” of the Saudi government and that Alfozan’s alleged efforts to bug the conference room amount to state-sponsored espionage. (However, Alfozan has not currently been charged with either corporate espionage or stealing trade secrets.) Alfozan is in detention pending a hearing next week. According to the Department of Justice, he’s facing up to five years in prison and a $250,000 fine. His former employer is now trying to pick up the pieces. It’s also claiming that its own AI helped reveal Alfozan’s alleged ploy, without elaborating how. “We take protecting our technology, data and people seriously, and as a matter of national importance,” the company wrote in a statement posted on X earlier this week. “Our security program is designed to protect against a wide range of threat actors, from insider risks to cybercriminals to nation states, and this case is an example of how AI can help address these sophisticated security threats.” More on OpenAI: OpenAI Sued for Allegedly Building Models Out of Children’s “Fears, Hopes, and Secrets”

OpenAI Hit With Stack of Lawsuits Over ChatGPT Allegedly Coaching Children to Harm Themselves
2026-10-15

OpenAI Hit With Stack of Lawsuits Over ChatGPT Allegedly Coaching Children to Harm Themselves

Futurism | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Eight states sued OpenAI this week, alleging that the AI company caused severe harm to users, many of them underage, while downplaying the risks of its technology. Filed individually in Iowa, Kansas, Nebraska, West Virginia, Arkansas, Arizona, Idaho and Montana on Wednesday, the coordinated lawsuits claim that OpenAI engaged in “willful, dangerous, reckless and unlawful conduct” by falsely marketing its flagship chatbot, ChatGPT, as safe for consumers even as it coached users — including minors and other vulnerable people — into self-harm and suicide, pushed them to engage in violence against others, and reinforced dangerous delusions. “OpenAI is hurting kids and they are lying to Iowans,” Iowa Attorney General Brenna Bird said in a statement, according to NBC. “OpenAI claims ChatGPT is safe while it’s giving dangerous medical advice, encouraging people to hurt others, and coaching people to commit suicide — all while pretending to be their fake friend.” In response to the lawsuits, OpenAI issued a statement to news outlets emphasizing is safety efforts, arguing that it’s “built industry-leading age-specific protections into our product that many other AI products lack.” “While no system can be perfect, we’ll continue to work with experts to inform our work and build additional safeguards, such as the parental controls, break reminders and age-appropriate model protections that exist today,” the statement continued, per The Guardian. “We’re committed to working with states on advancing pragmatic AI policies that apply to the entire AI industry — not just one company.” The state cases come as OpenAI continues to battle more than 50 lawsuits claiming that ChatGPT caused users to experience psychological harm, financial and reputational damage, physical injury, and death. Many of these lawsuits have been brought by victims of the Tumbler Ridge massacre in Canada, who argue that OpenAI’s could have prevented the devastating mass shooting but failed to do so after it detected the shooter’s disturbing messages but decided not to report them to police. The Canadian province of British Columbia is also suing OpenAI over the mass shooting. The company has also been sued in connection to a stalking case and a murder-suicide. As Reuters noted, the state lawsuits additionally point to recent high-profile safety incidents at OpenAI such as the now-infamous Hugging Face attack. They also call for a pause on recursive self-improvement, which many AI safety experts fear could lead to uncontrollable AI systems. Given that the White House has pushed back against federal AI regulation, this coordinated effort on the state level is significant — and points to growing bipartisan public sentiment demanding AI regulation. Trump Backs Down, Cancels Livestreamed Firing Squad Execution

Trump Backs Down, Cancels Livestreamed Firing Squad Execution

Futurism | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Last week, defense secretary and former TV personality Pete Hegseth shocked the world when he announced that the upcoming execution by firing squad of a mass shooter would be livestreamed online — an unfathomable act of cruelty for all to see. Nidal Hasan, a former army psychiatrist, killed 13 people at the Fort Hood US Army post in Texas in 2009. The plans for broadcasting the killing came just days after officials failed to end the life of Christa Pike, who had been on death row for decades. But now, even president Donald Trump is seemingly growing wary of the damning optics of televising a brutal execution in the weeks leading up to the midterm elections — which would’ve been the first execution by military firing squad in the United States since November 1945 and the country’s first public execution since 1936. In a rambling Thursday post on Truth Social, Trump said that while broadcasting the murder would serve as a “strong deterrent,” “many very good and well meaning people would rather not watch him die, through streaming, or through any other visual device.” “He will die, as scheduled, by firing squad, but this event will not be streamed,” he added. However, he said that the victims will still be able to watch the horrific event if they choose to. “They deserve to witness JUSTICE!” Trump wrote. The sudden backpedaling raises plenty of questions. The president had personally signed off on the execution last week. Whether he also approved on the livestream remains unclear. Unsurprisingly, public support for watching an incarcerated man be riddled with bullets on the internet was extremely low. Trump’s announcement came just hours after ABC News and the Washington Post found that a whopping eight out of ten Americans opposed the idea of a livestream in an Ipsos poll. Republicans were slightly more inclined to approve, with 34 percent arguing it should be livestreamed, but even there, an overwhelming 64 percent disagreed. Even more than half of self-identified MAGA Republicans said they didn’t approve. Only eight percent of Democrats approved of the plan. More on the execution: Well, This Is the First Time America Has Livestreamed an Execution by Firing Squad
