Trump’s New Press Secretary Is an Incompetent Liar. She’s Also Perfect for the Job.
2026-10-15

Trump’s New Press Secretary Is an Incompetent Liar. She’s Also Perfect for the Job.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. There are two types of press secretaries in a Donald Trump administration: Those who lie slickly and those who lie embarrassingly. So far, Katie Zacharia, who debuted as the White House’s top spokesperson on Tuesday, is looking like the latter. The opening days of Zacharia’s tenure have been defined by questions over claims she has made about her background. She has a law degree from Pepperdine University (go Waves!) and has publicly identified herself as the “general counsel” for Fix California, a right-wing nonprofit. Trump has described Zacharia as an “attorney,” as did chyrons for TV appearances that Zacharia amplified on social media, CNN reports. Asked about her legal background on Tuesday, Zacharia claimed to have passed the bar “when I moved to China,” where she has also said she worked at a large Chinese law firm and as “outside counsel” to the International Finance Corporation and the World Bank. But foreign nationals aren’t allowed to take the bar in China, nor can they practice Chinese law. (She subsequently claimed to have misunderstood the reporter’s question.) The other shoe dropped last night, when the Wall Street Journal reported she has not passed the bar in the U.S., a revelation Zacharia seemingly confirmed on Thursday. But if Zacharia did misleadingly airbrush her credentials, it might make her the ideal spokesperson for an administration that regularly struggles to tell the truth. The president himself is the government’s main fount of misleading-at-best, mendacious-at-worst information, lies that the staffers around him then have to scramble to defend or make real. And if a press secretary willing to shamelessly deceive the public is Trump’s main requirement for the job, Zacharia is a perfect fit. That doesn’t mean she won’t run into trouble, of course. Beyond her apparently zhuzhed-up LinkedIn, Zacharia’s debut appearance as press secretary on Tuesday earned some brutal reviews. Zacharia spoke haltingly, read off notes for long stretches, took just three questions, and at one point said “my president” when she meant “my husband.” She hasn’t held an on-camera briefing since. Still, theater critics zinging Zacharia’s lack of polish are missing the point. Journalists have sometimes let themselves get ensorcelled by Trump’s more capable press secretaries, whose relative poise disguised a shameless willingness to deceive on his behalf. Some reporters praised Karoline Leavitt, whose departure in August cleared the way for Zacharia, for her professionalism while glossing over how she actually spent much of her tenure: misleading the public. It’s true that Zacharia’s job isn’t just to answer on-camera questions but also to work with reporters behind the scenes, credibly speak for the president, and paint the administration in a positive light. By that standard, her days may already be numbered. “Getting behind the podium is tough, but she got off on the wrong foot,” an administration official texted an MS NOW reporter after Zacharia’s debut. Still, the president appears to be sticking by her. “I thought she was very good,” he said Tuesday. Whether that holds is anyone’s guess. But in a perverse way, it could end up being a good thing for the rest of us if the lies coming from the briefing podium are as ham-fistedly delivered as the ones on Zacharia’s résumé. Separating rhetoric from reality has been one of this political era’s defining challenges, both for journalists and for the public. Having someone as clumsy as Zacharia for a mouthpiece makes it easier to tell which is which. I can’t speak for Katie Zacharia, but I’m willing to bet that you’ve earned some relaxation after a long day. For your evening entertainment, my colleagues suggest: Watching a new Oscar contender: La Bola Negra is a sprawling Spanish epic that grapples with the links between fascism and homophobia. It also features sparkling cameos from Penélope Cruz as a sultry cabaret dancer and Glenn Close as a Spanish-speaking American professor. The Odyssey has some competition for the Best Picture Oscar! Learning about gay rights before Stonewall: It’s LGBTQ+ history month, which means it’s a great moment to learn more about our queer forebears. While activists and heroes usually get most of the public’s attention, queer historian and author Timothy Stewart-Winter argues that it’s just as important to focus on the more complicated figures. He tells the story of LBJ’s right-hand man, who got arrested for bathroom cruising in 1964. The incident reveals a lot about what gay life looked like before Stonewall. Appreciating the best part of The Great British Bake Off: Cookbook author Nigella Lawson is a new judge on GBBO, and she is a breath of fresh air, according to ICYMI host Kate Lindsay. While the other judges sometimes treat dessert as something illicit, Lawson’s unabashed enthusiasm for baked goods is a welcome change. Celebrating 20 years of 30 Rock: Join Slate’s culture team in toasting Tina Fey’s iconic sitcom, which premiered 20 years ago this month. Revisit some of the show’s best jingles and parodies, appreciate Alec Baldwin’s singular performance as Jack Donaghy, and test your memory with this quiz. If you’re a 30 Rock fan looking for a similar show to enjoy, check out this ranked list of 14 spiritual successors that my colleagues and I put together. Happy watching, and see you tomorrow! The Worst Tech Bros and the Worst Christian Nationalists United Behind an Extreme Plan. Then It All Fell Apart.
2026-10-15

The Worst Tech Bros and the Worst Christian Nationalists United Behind an Extreme Plan. Then It All Fell Apart.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. President Donald Trump on Thursday walked back his administration’s plan to livestream the firing squad execution of Nidal Hasan, who killed 13 soldiers and shot dozens more at Fort Hood in 2009. The execution will move forward, and the victims’ immediate families will be able to view it in person, Trump said, but it won’t be broadcast for the public. Trump may be in retreat because, when Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced the planned livestream a week ago, the condemnation was swift, brutal, and broad—and not just among the administration’s typical critics. Joe Kent, who served as the director of the National Counterterrorism Center from 2025 to 2026, called it “a stain on our morality.” A daughter of one of the men Hasan killed said livestreaming the execution “stabs us in the back.” Even some conservative personalities shared their distaste. “Public executions are soul-twisting and we should not go down that road,” the conservative radio host Erick Erickson said on social media. Almost all Republican lawmakers who spoke about the idea did so with dismay. “Live-streaming executions is what jihadists do,” wrote Rep. Austin Scott of Georgia. Vice President J.D. Vance was lukewarm to the idea—declining to criticize it but saying he would not watch—and Trump was reportedly having doubts last week before he announced the cancellation Thursday. The opposition to the now-nixed livestream was not, however, universal: A strange coalition of the most ardently Christian nationalist evangelical leaders joined by a group of new-right tech bros made common cause over the idea that it’s not only acceptable, but good, for the federal government to ensure the public can watch agents of the state execute a murderer. These supporters represented a relatively new strain in conservative politics. These elements of the right, both religious and secular, are at the vanguard of anti-woke politics, pushing in online discussions for bolder and bolder ideas to remake American society into an imagined 1950s suburban fantasy. And while members of this coalition weren’t the only ones to support the idea of a livestreamed public execution, they were the ones most gleeful about it: The idea had already found traction in those circles, which act as an incubator for reactionary proposals. But when the rest of the MAGA world didn’t follow, and when Trump reversed course, the unpopularity of the idea became abundantly clear—and these Christian- and techno-nationalist elites were shown to be standing atop a largely illusory movement. It turns out that there are some things that are too far even for MAGA. In a post on Sunday, Palmer Luckey, the co-founder of Anduril Industries, wrote that it was “normal, good, and aligned with our natural and national order” to “revel in the death of those who truly deserve it.” Luckey, a donor to the Republican Party, is one of a number of Silicon Valley figures involved in the “Tech Right.” There are variations within this group’s politics, but Luckey has a background well-aligned with Hegseth’s hypermasculine code. Anduril is a military technology company, and it produces drones and surveillance systems. Musing on the idea of public executions, Luckey argued that “executions should be public and brutal” because “they are the ultimate example of the state’s monopoly on violence, and the only way to keep that from running out of control is to confront voters” with the act. Luckey wasn’t the only one to make this argument. Joe Lonsdale, a co-founder of the military tech firm Palantir, which helped Immigration and Customs Enforcement track down undocumented immigrants, shared Luckey’s mentality. “Our society today is too soft in ways that cause our fellow citizens harm,” he wrote. This wasn’t a new position for him. In December, after the U.S. military hit a boat containing alleged drug traffickers with a missile, Lonsdale chided “leftist schoolmarm leaders” who opposed the extrajudicial killings, accusing them of breeding “violence and evil in our civilization.” To create a peaceful society, he argued, “bold, virtuous men” should “deter evil” by hanging criminals in public. Such “masculine leadership” would “protect our most vulnerable.” These Tech Right figures talk of wanting to correct the ills of what they see as an overly bureaucratic, feminized society. Luckey and Lonsdale, like Vance, claim Palantir’s Peter Thiel as an influential mentor. Thiel has long expressed skepticism about democracy; he and the other tech oligarchs claim to think bigger and more experimentally. These champions of “free thought” landed on boldness and strength as guiding stars. And in this, they share common ground with the New Christian Right. In evangelical America, there is a small network of whiskey-drinking, bearded men with podcasts who have built brands around uncompromising Reformed theology. These men lead small churches but have much larger followings online, and they take provocative stances, such as ones against women’s suffrage or interracial marriage, that are often rewarded by the algorithm. The religion reporter Bob Smietana has dubbed them the “Jim Crow Theobros” because of their penchant for fantasizing about a racially unified—white—country. Like the Tech Right, these men see noble masculinity as the solution to most of society’s ills. They, like Elon Musk, were eager participants in the discourse about the “sin of empathy.” Both groups believe the most important thing is for strong men to protect the weak and vulnerable—sometimes from themselves. Women will be happier once they have their liberties stripped from them, these Christian Nationalists claim: They will find personal and spiritual fulfillment in being submissive wives and mothers. It’s a fulfillment they cannot find with careers or later-in-life pregnancies or the angst of feminism. Just as Thiel looms large in the Tech Right, these Christian Nationalists share a connection with the Idaho pastor Doug Wilson. Wilson, who is older and slightly less caustic than the more podcast-bro types but no less radical, leads a network of churches that includes Hegseth’s. Vance has his Silicon Valley friends. Hegseth has his Theobros. They all flirt with violence in pursuit of a more traditionally ordered society. “If we’re televising executions, let’s start the trials in DC,” the pastor and content creator Joel Webbon wrote on social media over the weekend, before suggesting that Mike Huckabee, the U.S. ambassador to Israel, should be executed for treason. The Arizona pastor Dale Partridge, another member of the New Christian Right, has proposed fixes for our society that include banning women from elected office and putting only white Christian men in leadership positions. When it came to the idea of public executions, he saw another promising idea. “If we executed criminals swiftly and publicly on a weekly basis,” he wrote, “America would be a safer and more beautiful place.” Hegseth believes in his own righteousness. He saw no problem with bragging about a public execution—something that conjures images of Jim Crow–era lynchings or Taliban sports stadium killings—because to him, righteousness is firmness and discipline. Hegseth has spoken of a wrathful and warlike God, as when he prayed for “overwhelming violence of action against those who deserve no mercy.” So he may have been caught by surprise when the news of the plan was met in the evangelical world with ambivalence, wariness, and, occasionally, alarm. Unlike Roman Catholics, who uniformly condemned the announcement, evangelicals have no theological qualms with the death penalty itself, and a solid majority of Americans still see capital punishment as justified for murder. But Walter Kim, the president of the National Association of Evangelicals, argued that the livestream “risks turning one of the state’s most solemn responsibilities into a public spectacle, promoting a culture of death, rather than a culture that upholds the dignity of every human being.” Bart Barber, who led the Southern Baptist Convention, the nation’s largest Protestant denomination, from 2022 to 2024, warned that “this culture isn’t serious enough to watch something like this,” predicting that “people will hoot, howl, and thump their chests instead, as though they just delivered the kill shot in [a first-person shooter] game.” Even House Speaker Mike Johnson, a die-hard Southern Baptist, seemed to indicate he thought it went too far, arguing that it would serve as a propaganda tool for terrorists. There were, of course, the people who have wedged themselves so firmly into the Trump machinery that they cannot break from it. A few edgy right-wing Christian political influencers bragged about the administration’s strength. As did a few more standard Christian Nationalist leaders, including the controversial charismatic preacher Mark Driscoll; the dean of the School of Theology at the Southern Baptist Theological Seminary, Andrew Walker; and Robert Jeffress, the pastor of one of the nation’s most influential Baptist megachurches. Jeffress, in particular, offered a theological defense of livestreamed executions: “Capital punishment is commanded by God in Genesis 9:6 to remind people of the sanctity of human life,” Jeffress said in a social media post. “The more people that can see that truth illustrated through a public execution, the better.” But these figures, members of the traditional evangelical elites, do not speak for the entire community of evangelical elites. They instead stood out against the other evangelicals who made pleas for a sense of decorum and human dignity. It seemed clear, in their jumping to defend such a cruel decision, that even as Trump’s political power wanes, they see their role as performing loyalty to Trump. They were different from the dissenting evangelical leaders, who, it seems, still hope to impose their own religious values onto the conservative public, rather than the other way around. And they were different from the Theobros, who hope to lead by pulling the entire MAGA movement to the right. So the true champions of public executions remain the Theobros and Silicon Valley oligarchs, the truest match for Hegseth’s wrathful, hypermasculine theology. For a brief moment, they scored a kind of symbolic victory. But in the end, the episode backfired on them. Gleefully supporting public executions didn’t make these men look strong. It made them instead seem too grotesquely bloodthirsty for even today’s conservative movement. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The People Who Asked Zohran Mamdani Why He’s Killing Kids Explain Themselves
2026-10-15

The People Who Asked Zohran Mamdani Why He’s Killing Kids Explain Themselves

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Libby Lenkinski was walking toward Union Square in New York City last week on the evening of Oct. 7, when she first encountered a sea of Palestinian flags. Lenkinski is an Israeli American who has spent more than two decades working against Israel’s oppression of Palestinians and helping lead organizations that pushed for democracy and equality there. She also lost friends in Hamas’ Oct. 7, 2023, attack. She had come that night to read a eulogy for an Israeli killed in Gaza at a vigil that would also remember Palestinians killed, an event organized by Israelis for Peace, a group of left-wing Israeli expatriates. She wasn’t expecting to find herself on the opposite side of a barricade from people she had spent much of her adult life marching alongside. “I couldn’t quite hear what was happening. I couldn’t quite decipher what I was seeing. I was like, is this part of it or is this something else?” she recalled. Then she noticed someone wearing what appeared to be a Hamas headband. When Mayor Zohran Mamdani appeared at the vigil, the protesters began to shout at him: “Zohran, you can’t hide, you support genocide.” “Traitor, traitor, come on out, you sold all your people out.” “Mamdani, what do you say? How many kids did you kill today?” What exactly was this about? For Lenkinski, the disruption was painful on several levels. “What happened that night at the vigil was brutal,” she said. “It was megaphones from before we started until after we left. You couldn’t get through a single testimony—including from families in Gaza—without being disrupted by these chants.” When video of the incident began to spread, it was also confounding far beyond New York. Conservatives reacted with glee; many liberals scratched their heads. Had the left flank already turned on Mamdani, not even a year in? It’s clear that the majority of the New York City voters who brought Mamdani into office have not, in fact, turned on him. In a Suffolk University poll in September, some 56 percent of voters said he was doing an “excellent” or “good” job, and another 17 percent rated him “fair.” More than 6 in 10 voters said he was performing better than they expected. But those numbers may not reflect some discontent in the left-most movement where the future mayor earned the early enthusiasm that helped vault him into office. It wasn’t just one rally, either. There had been rumblings of frustration in criminal justice circles over policing, for which Mamdani has largely retained the status quo in New York. After Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents shot a man several times in a vehicle while a 5-year-old child sat in the back seat, Mamdani’s self-described “political home,” the Democratic Socialists of America, demanded the removal of NYPD Commissioner Jessica Tisch if she did not “end NYPD complicity with ICE.” Even one of Mamdani’s handpicked congressional candidates joined the call. When a reporter asked Mamdani about that this week, he replied flatly, “Jessica Tisch is the police commissioner. I have no plans to change that.” (Mamdani has demanded ICE leave New York City.) If Mamdani has so far shown a pragmatic streak in how he governs that can feel at odds with the loftier promises of his campaign, some friction with his most hard-line voters might be inevitable. But I wondered specifically what motived the protestors on Oct. 7, who managed to make international headlines. A City University of New York law student who helped organize the march agreed to speak with me on the condition I not publish his name. He said the demonstration began as a student walkout, with protesters converging on City Hall to demand action on Palestinian rights. They marched to Union Square after learning Mamdani would be attending the vigil. The law student objected in particular to Mamdani’s response to protests against West Bank property sales he said were promoted at New York synagogues, arguing that the mayor had defended policing those demonstrations without doing enough to stop the sales, along with similar protests against CUNY’s investments in Israel. He was also unhappy with the mayor’s Strategy to Combat Antisemitism, announced in September, which he said conflated anti-Zionism with antisemitism in some pro-Palestinian groups. “Why are we targeting Mamdani? Because Mamdani won on Palestine. The only reason he won this election is because of Palestine. If it were not for Palestine, he would not have won. And now that he is mayor, it’s our job to pressure him,” the organizer told me. “He’s going to make mistakes. He’s going to fall for a lot of pressure where he’s going to accept Zionist demands. And when he does do that, which he has done repeatedly, it’s up to us, the people, to pressure him to choose his principles over his position.” He had no regrets about pursuing Mamdani to the vigil. “I think it was a victory,” he said. I asked whether he knew the vigil had included testimonies from families in Gaza, which Lenkinski said the protesters had drowned out. “I—I did not hear that,” he replied. I noted Mamdani’s explanation that the vigil had mourned both Israeli and Palestinian deaths. “That just shows that he’s appealing to both sides and he’s not taking a principled stance,” he said. The law student told me Mamdani had never gone far enough to earn his vote, but protesters with him that day included an ex-Mamdani intern. Nerdeen Kiswani, the Palestinian American co-founder of Within Our Lifetime, one of New York’s most confrontational pro-Palestinian organizations, applauded the protest and said more should be on the way. “I was critical of him from when he first announced his campaign, and I don’t think I shifted,” she told me. “I have been consistent.” Many supporters, she said, voted for Mamdani expecting to hold him accountable once he took office. “He has executive power, so rhetoric is not enough. He has to take action to make material change.” “I’m glad that he’s in this position,” she said. “It’s precisely why we need to keep protesting him and people like him.” Kiswani said unease over Mamdani’s handling of the NYPD was also fomenting some of the pushback, at least in her slice of the political spectrum. “No mayor has retained the same police commissioner from the administration prior to theirs since 1964,” she said, referring to Tisch, who was elevated under the previous mayor, Eric Adams. “All of these frustrations that people have had for a while exploded.” It is fair to say not everyone saw Oct. 7’s events in New York the same way. Rania Batrice, a Palestinian American political strategist who was a deputy campaign manager for Bernie Sanders’ 2016 presidential campaign, was disturbed. She knew some of the Jewish activists at the vigil and considered them among the most committed opponents of Israel’s occupation. “My first reaction was: This is absolutely ridiculous,” she told me. She described the attacks on Mamdani as “wildly unhelpful.” “This whole idea that people aren’t allowed to mourn or remember is really troubling to me,” she said. Mourning Israeli civilians, she argued, did not negate the suffering of Palestinians or diminish the struggle against Israel’s government. For Batrice, the danger wasn’t simply that the confrontation looked terrible. It was that years of political marginalization could make activists so sensitive to perceived betrayal that they might fail to recognize the gains they had achieved. “The reality is, you have to look at the totality here, not a single moment,” she said. “You can have feelings about a single moment, but that doesn’t negate everything else he’s done and said. He’s shown up in the toughest circumstances. I think it fractures the movement, honestly. And that’s not something any of us need right now.” Debbie Almontaser, a longtime Muslim community organizer who helped elect Mamdani, wasn’t surprised by the confrontation. She has spent decades fighting for the kind of political influence New York’s Muslim communities now have. (In 2007, she was pushed out as the founding principal of the city’s first Arabic-language public school amid a campaign of anti-Muslim attacks; a federal agency later found that the city had discriminated against her.) But even while she understood it, she didn’t necessarily see its wisdom. “This happens with every elected official who ran on specific promises that, in some shape or form, did not quickly materialize,” Almontaser said. “He is the mayor of over 8 million people in New York City, and he is accountable for their safety, security, and well-being. And there needs to be room for him to be able to be true to his role and his position.” Almontaser had a measured perspective on criticism the mayor has received, not just from pro-Palestinian activists but from pro-Israel critics as well: “For decades, people who’ve been in political positions—whether it’s in the Republican Party or the Democratic Party—have always had one-sided position to support Israel in a way that has alienated, isolated, and marginalized the Palestinian people, the Palestinian cause.” She remembered a time when Arab and Muslim New Yorkers struggled to find elected officials willing to acknowledge their experiences at all. Now one of their own had become mayor, and some Jewish New Yorkers were experiencing a sense of alienation that she recognized. “Nobody should feel that way,” she said. Just eight days before the vigil, Almontaser had publicly endorsed Mamdani’s new strategy to combat antisemitism—the same initiative that had helped provoke the student protest. Lenkinski had welcomed it, too. Both women had spent years advocating for Palestinian rights while maintaining relationships with Jewish organizations. What some activists saw as a concession to Zionist political pressure they considered part of the difficult work of building a city in which different communities could feel safe. Mamdani, for his part, has been steadfast in his response to the criticism. Asked about being called a traitor and worse, he told reporters, “I am the mayor of New York City, and this is what I signed up for.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Microsoft bringing Defender to Linux desktops. Repeat: Microsoft bringing Defender to Linux desktops
2026-10-16

Microsoft bringing Defender to Linux desktops. Repeat: Microsoft bringing Defender to Linux desktops

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The year of Corporate Compliance on the Linux desktop is here It might not be the Year of the Linux Desktop yet, but it could be the Year of Corporate Compliance on the Linux Desktop as Microsoft releases Defender for Linux Workstations. The product is an extension of Microsoft's existing Defender for Endpoint. According to the Windows behemoth, it brings "enterprise-grade protection, detection, vulnerability management and response capabilities to Linux workstations." Linux desktop users will get services including Defender's anti-virus functionality. The penguin-primed product also has management tools for inventory and security settings, as well as device isolation. "The result," said Microsoft, "is broader Linux protection, delivered through the tools and processes security teams already trust." The release is currently in public preview and requires the standalone Microsoft Defender for Endpoint Plan 2 or Microsoft 365 E5 and E7. Support is based on the underlying Linux distribution and kernel version, with RHEL, CentOS, Ubuntu LTS, SUSE Linux Enterprise Server, and Oracle Linux among others on the list. There's no separate desktop agent to deploy or maintain. Defender works the same way as it does on Linux servers, so administrators shouldn't face many challenges. End users… well… that's another story. As enterprises gain visibility into Linux workstations alongside the rest of their endpoint estate, some friction is possible and it won't be technical. For some, compliance through Microsoft's tooling is anathema, no matter how penguin-friendly it claims to be nowadays. As for the why, we're told it's simple business and customer demand. Microsoft noted that more developers are using Linux desktops, which in turn makes them more attractive to attackers. "As Linux adoption expands beyond the datacenter, organizations need a consistent approach to protecting Linux wherever it is used," Microsoft said. The idea of Microsoft Defender being able to peer at Linux desktops may alarm some, but Microsoft announced Defender ATP for Linux back in 2020. The better question is: why has it taken so long? We'd also quibble with the premise "As more developers begin to use Linux desktops." Many already do: the 2025 Stack Overflow survey had Windows leading for professional work at 49.5 percent, but Ubuntu wasn't far behind at 27.7 percent. So less "begin," more "enthusiastically continue." ® Core devs revert up to 10 features, push release to November, and blame better tooling rather than vibe coding UK school admits data protection bungle as children's data at risk PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics Migration targets, security rules, and management metrics take center stage at this year's event New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career School staff seemed to be trying to get ransomware – for a second time Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? Postgres 19 delayed after AI reviewers find more bugs than humans can fix
2026-10-16

Postgres 19 delayed after AI reviewers find more bugs than humans can fix

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Core devs revert up to 10 features, push release to November, and blame better tooling rather than vibe coding The PostgreSQL Global Development Group has delayed the release of PostgreSQL 19, the latest iteration of the popular open source database, and held back an unusual number of features. Once slated for release in late September or early October, PostgreSQL 19 is now expected to be generally available around November 19. Developers have also dropped between eight and 10 significant features. Explaining the number of reverts — features whose rollouts have now been postponed to next year's release, PostgreSQL 20 — Peter Eisentraut, veep at EDB, a PostgreSQL support and consultancy, said the reason could be at least in part attributed to the LLM code reviews used by developers. In a blog post, Eisentraut, who is a leading PostgreSQL committer, said some of the reverted features had significant architectural defects that would have been hard to fix "quickly and during beta." However, the introduction of LLM-assisted code review also uncovered a long tail of “relatively harmless issues involving various edge cases” which, in past times, developers might not have noticed until much later, most likely issuing bug fixes in the five years following launch. “With LLM-assisted code review, these kinds of things can get found much faster, and then you’re staring at a list of forty defect reports. Even if each of them requires only a three-line fix on average, there is a fixed overhead to dealing with each of these, and then you run out of time.” When the big updates for PostgreSQL 19 were written, LLM-assisted code review was not as useful as it is now, we're told, but in the months leading up to the launch, the AI-supported reviews became better understood and more effective. “The effective quality bar has been raised after the time the feature was written and committed. Unless the capabilities of LLM-assisted code review keep improving at the current rate, this situation should sort itself out for future development cycles,” Eisentraut said. He was also at pains to point out that the reverts were not down to “vibe coding” in development, as the technique was not available when most of the new features were written. Postgres was first proposed by Michael Stonebraker in 1986. In 1994, Andrew Yu and Jolly Chen replaced the query language POSTQUEL with SQL, and in 1995 made the database freely available and modifiable under a more permissive license, renaming it PostgreSQL. It has since become the most popular database among professional developers owing to the services the big three hyperscalers built on it as well as the range of wire-compatible distributed systems available. Among the features pulled from the PostgreSQL 19 iteration is support for SQL Property Graph Queries (SQL/PGQ), which became part of the SQL standard in 2023. The feature provides syntax for exploring relationships between nodes connected by edges. PostgreSQL's implementation was removed over unresolved issues. At the time, PostgreSQL contributor Tom Lane warned: "At this point I'd be willing to bet dinner that if we ship it in v19 there will be post-release bug discoveries that are unfixable until v20." Despite the delay, Eisentraut promises PostgreSQL 19 will still be “a great release,” that includes REPACK CONCURRENTLY, which avoids the table-lock VACUUM FULL operation used to reclaim disk space. “And it will be very robust, after all the additional scrutiny,” he said. The features reverted from PostgreSQL 19 will likely make it into PostgreSQL 20. Just a few months ago, developers expected PostgreSQL 20 to have no new features, just bug fixes. Now, it is likely to have the new features left over from the earlier release, Eisentraut said.® Core devs revert up to 10 features, push release to November, and blame better tooling rather than vibe coding UK school admits data protection bungle as children's data at risk PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics Migration targets, security rules, and management metrics take center stage at this year's event New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career School staff seemed to be trying to get ransomware – for a second time Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? Thief snatches school snapper's SD cards in smash & grab
2026-10-16

Thief snatches school snapper's SD cards in smash & grab

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
UK school admits data protection bungle as children's data at risk An old-fashioned smash-and-grab job on a photographer’s vehicle threatens the security of pupils’ data as a UK secondary school says it faces a potential data protection pickle. Jon Curtis-Brignell, headteacher at Priory School in Lewes, said in an email this week that a company the school hired to take pupils’ annual school photos has alerted it to a data theft case after a vehicle was broken into. “Pret-a-Portrait has informed us that, overnight between 6 and 7 October, one of its vehicles was broken into and a camera bag containing SD memory cards was stolen,” Curtis-Brignell wrote. “Some of these cards may have contained photographs taken at Priory School, together with images of the QR code cards used for school photographs, which show pupils’ names and class names.” The headteacher said that the same equipment used to take the photos at Priory School was also used for three additional photography jobs after Pret-a-Portrait visited the school on October 1. Pret-a-Portrait, which has offices in the UK, Qatar, and the UAE, told the school that it believes “most, if not all” of the files containing pupils’ data will have been overwritten as a result, but it cannot be certain. Curtis-Brignell said that the potentially affected information included photographs, pupils’ names, and their “class information associated with the photography process.” Other types of data, such as addresses, contact details, financial information, medical information, and safeguarding records stored in the school’s own systems remain untouched. Nick Kerr, owner of Pret-a-Portrait, told The Register that no physical, printed photos were stored in the vehicle at the time of the break-in, and that it was not possible to determine the potential scope of the data protection violation. “As the email from Priory School explains, it is likely to be somewhere between a small subset of their pictures and none of them,” said Kerr. On a positive note, he said that there is nothing to suggest the files have been improperly accessed. “Our analysis reveals that no improper access to pictures from this job occurred in between the time of the theft and the pictures being taken offline.” Per the headteacher’s email, Pret-a-Portrait confirmed that it took a number of steps to mitigate the potential damage, including taking all school photos offline, changing the IDs used to access them, and changing its procedures so that all SD cards are wiped at the end of each commission. The company also “reviewed and strengthened its procedures following the incident,” and reported the matter to both the police and the ICO. “I appreciate that receiving an email of this nature may understandably cause concern,” Curtis-Brignell added. “We have worked closely with our Data Protection Officer and Pret-a-Portrait to understand what happened, establish the potential risk and ensure that appropriate action has been taken. “We wanted to be open with families about what has happened, while also reassuring you that there is currently no evidence that the information has been accessed or misused and that a number of steps have now been taken to prevent access to the affected photographs and reduce the risk of a similar incident occurring again.” ® Core devs revert up to 10 features, push release to November, and blame better tooling rather than vibe coding UK school admits data protection bungle as children's data at risk PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics Migration targets, security rules, and management metrics take center stage at this year's event New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career School staff seemed to be trying to get ransomware – for a second time Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? Open source sovereignty gets down to the boring business of making it work
2026-10-16

Open source sovereignty gets down to the boring business of making it work

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Migration targets, security rules, and management metrics take center stage at this year's event AI and digital sovereignty dominated this year's Open Source Summit Europe. But beyond the AI noise, the sovereignty discussions offered something useful: practical plans for putting open source to work. The recurring theme of digital sovereignty started right from the first day's keynotes, with Geopolitics, Sovereignty, and the Fight to Stay Open, by the Linux Foundation's Nithya Ruff and Johan Linaker from Research Institutes of Sweden, whose name is considerably longer than its URL: ri.se. This isn't just some European issue. Ruff stressed that it mattered "from Canada, to Sierra Leone, to Trinidad and Tobago." This was followed by Open Source Security as the Foundation of European Sovereignty, focusing on OSS supply chains. Then ORBIT Launchpad: Building the Due Diligence Bridge Between Manufacturers and Open Source, with speakers from Dell and Microsoft discussing OpenSSF's efforts to build catalogs of open source components to help companies achieve CRA compliance, such as CRABFOSC – the CRA Baseline For Open Source Consumption. The EU's Cyber Resilience Act (CRA) is now starting to bite: reporting obligations for actively exploited vulnerabilities and severe security incidents began last month, with its main requirements applying from December 2027. Hearing Dell and Microsoft discuss how to meet those requirements, rather than simply why they matter, was encouraging. Éléonore Daxhelet presented the 2026 report from the EU's Open Source Observatory, or OSOR. Emphasizing that this isn't just an EU matter, she highlighted Diaa, from the Ministry of Digital Transformation of Ukraine, as well as the Swiss EMBAG legislation we recently covered. The slides are online [PDF]. Then, as if to emphasize that further, Kazuki Imamura from Japan's Innovation Platform Agency (IPA) presented the Japan-Type OSS Model. The IPA looked at hundreds of companies in seven countries, and more than 30,000 GitHub repositories, and found that while a lot of organizations are talking about open source, few are actually doing it. Japan has its own three-axis approach, where the public sector attempts to plant seeds and cultivate efforts by local commercial vendors, with a strong emphasis on the need for machine-readable legislation and rules. Again, the slides are online [PDF] and contain some uncomfortable numbers. To pick out the single talk with the most information, it might be Strategic Approach To Demonstrating the Value of OSS Efforts by Dr Dawn Foster of Fast Wonder. She started with the Linux Foundation's report on Measuring OSPO Value, examining open source program offices, and then presented a remarkable amount of guidance and info from the CHAOSS Project. It stands for Community Health Analytics in Open Source Software, and Foster's dense slide deck [PDF] linked to multiple different guides from the CHAOSS Practitioner Guides. There is a lot of reading here, including a series on viability metrics, a guide on demonstrating organizational value, and a guide on how to research software impact. To our eye, this looks like pragmatic stuff: info on how to introduce and run FOSS programs, on how to talk to users, and, perhaps most importantly, on how to explain it to executives. Many talks made reference to Schleswig-Holstein's moves away from the US cloud. One was from two of the people behind Berlin's Strategy for Digital Sovereignty. Berlin is both a city and a German state, with more residents than Schleswig-Holstein. The city government is working with PwC to manage its migration – not the first name that springs to mind in FOSS, but a sign that the giant consultancies see business here too. We were not surprised to see the only mention of KPIs at the entire summit here; we were slightly more surprised by the timescales, which aim to make 70 percent of the software stack on administrative workstations open source by 2032. The slide deck [PDF] reveals a rather bureaucratic planning process, but that's not a big surprise; nor is quite a lot of discussion of Governance, Dependencies, and Measures. Executives, in our experience, tend to like numbers they can measure and targets they can aim for. Emiel Brok from SUSE talked about the State of the OSS Union: Assessing Your Stack's Autonomy Under EU Law. This covered the European Commission's proposed Cloud and AI Development Act (CADA) and how organizations can measure their readiness for it. Some of this revolves around the EU Cloud Sovereignty Framework [PDF], and its definitions of eight Sovereignty Objectives (SOV-1 to SOV-8) and Sovereignty Effectiveness Assurance Level, the SEAL scale, from SEAL-0 (No Sovereignty) to SEAL-4 (Full Digital Sovereignty), defined as "complete EU control, subject only to EU law, with no critical non-EU dependencies." Brok mentioned SUSE's online Cloud Sovereignty Framework Self Assessment, which lets you get an idea of where your organization stands in ten minutes. All this may sound dry beside the kernel GPU driver discussions happening elsewhere at the event. But plans, targets, and measurements are signs that organizations are treating open source as infrastructure rather than an experiment. User refused to change her password because everyone already knew it
2026-10-16

User refused to change her password because everyone already knew it

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
School staff seemed to be trying to get ransomware – for a second time Change is constant, but The Register likes to keep some things the same – such as using Friday mornings to bring you a new installment of On Call, the reader-contributed column that shares your tech support stories. This week, meet a reader we'll Regomize as "Daniel" who told us that in 2020 he was hired to replace a two-person IT department at a UK school. "The IT manager had left amid some 'accounting irregularities,' mainly involving high-end gaming PCs being purchased using school funds but never making it onto the school site," Daniel told On Call. "His deputy stepped up just in time to have to deal with a ransomware attack that took the school's entire network offline for three months." The school eventually hired external contractors to rebuild the network and restore from the only available backup, which was months old. The deputy IT manager then quit without serving their notice period. Daniel took the vacant job and in his first week the school principal asked him to audit the network and deliver a report explaining how to get things up to scratch. "I found a few quick wins, including the password policy which at the time was set to six letters. Just that, no complexity, no expiry," Daniel told On Call. "And users hadn't been forced to change them since before the ransomware attack. Or, indeed, ever." The principal approved Daniel's proposed password policy and a requirement for everyone to change their password immediately. "I was not prepared for the backlash this would create," Daniel wrote. The school's data manager proudly said she had used the same password since 1998 and justified keeping it on the grounds that everyone on staff knew it. "She said, 'If I'm not in, they can log in as me and run the reports.'" It gets worse: the password was "letmein" and the data manager had written it on a piece of paper and taped it to her desk. As Daniel rolled out the new password policy, more complaints rolled in. The school principal eventually told Daniel to direct all enquiries regarding the password changes to him. "Within two days, he came into my office, sighed, put his head in his hands, and ordered me to revert the change and to remove the requirement for everyone to change their password," Daniel wrote. "I resisted, I really did," he wrote. "But it was his school. I got him to put the request in writing." The principal left three months later, and a nonprofit took over the school. Daniel's new bosses agreed with his stance and reinstated his password policy. The data manager again fought the change. "After a meeting with me and the nonprofit's IT director, she decided to take early retirement, so she wouldn't have to learn the new systems – or change her beloved password." Have management or users rejected your advice? Give him a hand: Dev publishes 25-year-old Palm OS tools to GitHub
2026-10-15

Give him a hand: Dev publishes 25-year-old Palm OS tools to GitHub

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Aaron Ardiri opened his old development archive after discovering enthusiasts were still improving apps for the platform A small but active community of retro computing enthusiasts is working to keep Palm OS alive, and a developer who recently became aware of their existence has just handed them a treasure trove of code he had no idea was still useful. Swedish developer Aaron Ardiri published around a gigabyte of Palm OS development resources on GitHub on Thursday in a bid to preserve them for community consumption. Prior to their upload, Ardiri told The Register that the files were languishing in his own personal backups for more than 25 years, preserved only for his own records as part of his past as a developer of Palm OS applications. “Everything in the archive was accumulated during my years as an active Palm OS developer,” Ardiri explained in an email. “It includes SDKs, technical documentation, conference presentations, development utilities, examples, and some limited system source code that was originally made available to developers to help them understand the operating system's UI and internal components.” One gigabyte of such info might not seem like a lot today, but back in the halcyon era of the PalmPilot, that was a whole heap of valuable data. Ardiri had previously published source code from his own Palm OS apps on GitHub for those who were interested in it, but he had assumed it was just for public record and that no one would really want to muck about with old Palm OS apps. “A developer recently submitted a pull request to my original Lemmings source repository, adding HiRes+ display support for the Palm Tungsten T3,” Ardiri explained. “I had no idea people were still actively developing and improving this software more than two decades later.” That sent him down a rabbit hole. He soon realized there were Palm OS enthusiasts on Reddit, Discord, and other online communities all looking for resources to keep their vintage hardware alive and their retro software viable. “It made me realize that the old development resources sitting on my backup drives might actually be useful to people today,” he said. And now here we are, with a nice, fat GitHub repo full of resources Palm OS users can make use of. It’s worth noting that just because Ardiri has published this stuff on GitHub, that doesn’t mean it’s open source and ripe for the picking. He’s taken steps to avoid stepping on any copyright toes by leaving out things like Palm OS ROM images, firmware, simulators, and commercial apps that he felt he ought not to distribute as he didn’t have explicit permission. Ardiri sees this as a historical preservation effort, not an attempt to circumvent IP laws. “A lot of early mobile development knowledge has disappeared with old websites, developer forums, and documentation archives. Some of it survives only in personal collections like mine,” Ardiri told us. He said that he’s thrilled to have been introduced to the world of Palm enthusiasts, and recently passed off more than 50 old Palm OS devices to Russian dev and Palm OS preservationist Dmitry Grinberg. “This has been an unexpected rediscovery of a period of my career that I thought was largely behind me,” Ardiri told us. “It's clear that Palm OS has a much more active community than I realized … the repository is my contribution to those efforts, providing a historical record of what development looked like when Palm OS was a thriving commercial platform.” It makes you wonder what other digital gold is sitting in backups just waiting to be discovered. Take this as a sign to take a look through your old drives – you never know what you might find that someone has long been looking for.  ® Core devs revert up to 10 features, push release to November, and blame better tooling rather than vibe coding UK school admits data protection bungle as children's data at risk PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics Migration targets, security rules, and management metrics take center stage at this year's event New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career School staff seemed to be trying to get ransomware – for a second time Microsoft's Go rewrite gets some competition, with a developer claiming his port runs twice as fast Silicon giant budgets up to $64B for expansion while keeping an eye on construction progress and power supplies Torvalds says contributors must understand their code, whatever tools they use What are they puffing? Time is a-changin’: say goodbye to the leap second
2026-10-16

Time is a-changin’: say goodbye to the leap second

The Verge | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Plans to retire it in 2035 are being expedited to prevent potential havoc on tech infrastructure.﻿ Plans to retire it in 2035 are being expedited to prevent potential havoc on tech infrastructure.﻿ An important change will soon be made to global timekeeping that aims to prevent potential infrastructure failures akin to the Y2K bug scare. On Thursday, the International Bureau of Weights and Measures (BIPM) voted to scrap the leap second, an ad hoc time adjustment that’s occasionally added so tracked time standards mirror the Earth’s real (and inconsistent) rotation. BIPM members previously agreed in 2022 to cease using leap seconds on or before 2035, but this is now being expedited to take effect on May 20th next year, aligning with World Metrology Day. There’s an unprecedented reason for its retirement being brought forward: changes to the Earth’s rotation may soon require timekeepers to subtract time instead of adding it in order to keep astronomical time in line with atomic time. This has never been attempted before, and there are concerns that doing so could wreak havoc on critical infrastructure that relies on time synchronization. Those fears aren’t unfounded given Foursquare, Reddit, LinkedIn, Yelp, and many other tech platforms have suffered outages in the past when leap seconds have been added. Companies including Meta, Google, Microsoft, and Amazon have all supported efforts to scrap the system. Following the BIPM vote, timekeepers will instead switch to using “leap hours,” allowing atomic and astronomical time to gradually drift apart until an entire hour needs to be added — which isn’t expected to happen for around 3,000 years. The Tesla Roadster flew, drove upside down, and hit 60mph in under a second
2026-10-16

The Tesla Roadster flew, drove upside down, and hit 60mph in under a second

The Verge | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The next-gen Roadster has been in development for nearly a decade. But perhaps it was worth the wait. The next-gen Roadster has been in development for nearly a decade. But perhaps it was worth the wait. Elon Musk promised a flying car. And the Tesla Roadster did exactly that. Tesla demonstrated its next-generation Roadster at an event in Waco, Texas, where the electric supercar drove upside down, briefly flew, and accelerated to 60mph in under a second. It was an impressive demonstration of a car that likely will come with some heavy caveats and restrictions if it ever goes into production. The event was less than 30 minutes and felt more like a stunt show than a product reveal. Indeed, there was no mention of price, release date, or other such mundane details. Musk promised the hypercar would go into production in 2028, but after nearly nine years who’s counting. The people were here to see a car fly, and that’s what Tesla gave them. First, the Roadster drove up a very tall, multi-story ramp, using its incredible downforce to cling to the vertical roadway. When it reached the top of the ramp, it flipped around using its cold-gas thrusters to bring it gently back down to earth. The car uses a fan-based suction system, supplied by SpaceX and mounted underneath the vehicle, which is capable of generating up to two Gs of suction force. Next, the Roadster flew over the rest of Tesla’s lineup, including the Model 3, Model Y, Cybertruck, Semi, and Cybercab — doing what Tesla VP for Engineering Lars Moravy called “a kick flip” in the process. Musk explained that it features attitude-control thrusters as well as main thrusters at the rear, giving the Roadster rocket-like capabilities. “So it can basically fly like a rocket,” Musk said. “And it could do rolls. It could do, like, crazy gymnastics. Whatever you want, really. Now, I should say that this is not necessarily a safety-optimized car. But it takes the fun meter to 11 out of 10.” The Roadster two ultra-high pressure air tanks with a combined volume of 600 liters, containing approximately 250 kilograms of air, and uses 10 nozzles to execute its maneuvers. The Roadster can even recharge its own tank, without requiring a specialized chemical propellant. The stored compressed air supplies the thrust needed to lift the car and perform the demonstrated maneuvers. Musk claimed “This will be an actual car that’s on the roads” — though later the company admitted it would not be road legal. This seems obvious, of course. Imagine having to satisfy strict regulatory requirements from the Federal Aviation Administration and the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. The Roadster could go into production in 2028, as Musk promised — or it could be another decade of missed deadlines and broken promises. Again, who cares? Car flew. As for the Roadster itself, Musk called it “the last best driver’s car.” Moravy said that Tesla drew inspiration from aerospace designs such as the SR-71 reconnaissance aircraft. It’s a two-seater, with seats molded directly into the chassis. Its steering and driving controls use steer-by-wire technology, including a steer-by-wire yoke, brakes, and throttle. The rear of the car features the thrusters and fans housed around the diffuser area. And it has a retractable rear wing for added downforce. Tesla has also said that the Roadster can accelerate from 0-60mph in less than a second. And during the event, the company claimed it did so in 0.81 seconds. Musk explained that increasing the applied force while reducing the vehicle’s mass help boost acceleration. In fact, Musk says the production Roadster should improve on the demonstrated result, although he acknowledged the extreme forces involved and the need to avoid subjecting occupants to extreme acceleration. “You’ve got to crack halfway to the speed of sound, I think,” Musk teased. “Mach 0.5. Maybe a little higher.” After the event, Tesla updated its website with details about the three distinct performance tiers for the next-generation Roadster, outlining how its controversial cold-gas thruster systems scale across the lineup: The next-gen Roadster certainly took the scenic route in getting here. A prototype version was announced as a “one more thing” style surprise during the 2017 Tesla Semi event. The specs sounded ludicrous even back then: 0-60 mph in 1.9 seconds, a top speed over 250 mph, a 200 kWh battery delivering up to 620 miles of range, and a $200,000 starting price tag. Musk said deliveries would start in 2020, which sounded perfectly doable at the time. Early adopters quickly threw down $50,000 deposits (or $250,000 for the limited Founders Series), effectively interest-free loans to help fund Tesla’s scaling ambitions. But then Tesla seemed to forget about the Roadster, as cementing its status as the ultimate vaporware. Production deadlines slipped as Tesla prioritized mass-market vehicles like the Model Y, while also wrestling with the overly complicated Cybertruck. Occasionally, when Musk would remember that the Roadster existed, he would up the ante about its capabilities. This culminated in the “SpaceX package” that would equip the supercar with cold-gas thrusters, bestowing hover powers and a 0-60 time under one second. Musk has also sought to downplay the Roadster’s significance, calling it a “small potato situation” that will not “move the needle in a major way financially.” He’s framed the supercar more as a joint engineering collaboration between Tesla and SpaceX, even questioning whether it’s “even a car anymore” given its experimental nature. Some analysts theorize that the decision to re-release the Roadster is an attempt to set the stage for a rumored Tesla-SpaceX merger. Tesla’s failure to bring the Roadster to market allowed other automakers to slip past the company with their own hyper-powered EVs. Rimac has smashed performance records with the Nevera, Porsche has refined electric performance with the Taycan, and Chinese giants like BYD are producing track-ready supercars at a fraction of the cost. The Roadster is no longer shocking the world as an impossible prototype; it’s playing catch-up to an industry that spent nine years building what Tesla merely rendered. The tech backlash is now in theaters

The tech backlash is now in theaters

The Verge | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
Between The Social Reckoning, You Can See Everything, Musk, and Artificial, filmmakers are taking Big Tech to task. Between The Social Reckoning, You Can See Everything, Musk, and Artificial, filmmakers are taking Big Tech to task. Though movies about disruptive tech founders are nothing new, 2026 has been unusually stacked with features that take a much more critical look at how Silicon Valley is reshaping our reality. Aaron Sorkin’s The Social Reckoningframes Mark Zuckerberg as a robotic villain whose ambitions are destabilizing society. Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s You Can See Everything paints a damning picture of Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes as she prepares for her ongoing stint in prison. After years of being widely celebrated as a visionary, you can see how the larger narrative around Elon Musk has shifted in projects like Alex Gibney’s Musk documentary. And while Luca Guadagnino’s Artificialis a dark comedy, it is also a character study that makes Sam Altman look like a manipulative sociopath. Even though some of these movies are fictionalized dramas, each of them draws from the wealth of critical reporting that has revealed their subjects to be less-than-savory characters. You get the sense that these filmmakers are trying to tell stories that are meant to resonate with audiences whose views of Big Tech have long since begun to dim. But it also feels like some of Hollywood’s biggest studios are finally embracing the idea that the tech backlash isn’t letting up anytime soon. Especially when you compare them to classics like The Social Network, the latest wave of cinematic dramas based on Silicon Valley power struggles has been far less sympathetic to their central figures. David Fincher’s 2010 film starring Jesse Eisenberg presented Zuckerberg as a complicated and misunderstood savant, but The Social Reckoning builds its story around the idea that the Facebook / Meta head is a man whose obsession with growth poses an existential threat to democracy. By focusing on the allegations leveled by whistleblower Frances Haugen, the movie is able to highlight Zuckerberg’s public transformation into a powerful executive who wields autocratic control over a multibillion-dollar social media empire. What The Social Reckoning lacks in timeliness (The Wall Street Journal began reporting Haugen’s warnings in 2021) and stylistic flair, it makes up for in the way it connects Facebook / Meta’s past internal decisions to events that are still playing out in real time. The movie’s scenes that dig into Instagram’s negative impacts on teens’ mental health and the relationship between Facebook’s algorithm and the spread of misinformation feel as if they’re speaking directly to present-day concerns about how much influence Meta has over people’s lives. Similarly, Gibney’s Musk documentary acts as both a chronicle of the xAI / SpaceX head’s past and an inquiry into why the public was ever willing to let Musk accrue so much power within governments across the world. Musk’s response to Gibney’s film has been telling. X has blocked the feature from being advertised on the platform, and Musk — a self-proclaimed “free speech absolutist” — has threatened to take legal action against Gibney for producing a “hit piece.” It wasn’t entirely surprising to see Musk personally badmouthing a project that casts him in a negative light, but it was curious to see the Musk ads initially missing from other social media platforms, including YouTube, Facebook, Instagram, and TikTok. Though the Meta subsidiaries and YouTube ultimately reversed their decisions, TikTok has stuck to its guns — which feels notable given the company’s indirect connections to the Trump administration by way of Skydance Corporation CEO David Ellison. David’s father — Oracle cofounder Larry Ellison — is a longtime Donald Trump supporter and one of the American billionaires who led the consortium of investors that purchased TikTok’s US business late last year. Ellison has reportedly promised Gibney that Musk will air on HBO (now a part of Skydance) sometime next January. The film is currently screening in select theaters, but it is hard to argue that it hasn’t had to deal with an unusual amount of outside interference in the weeks leading up to its release. Guadagnino’s Artificial — which was originally going to be released by Amazon — also faced unexpected marketing challenges this year. The streaming giant backed out of its deal to distribute the film this past June, just months after announcing that it was investing $50 billion in OpenAI. All of that made it seem very much like Amazon and other studios financially involved with AI companies might be cooling on the idea of releasing projects critical of the technology’s creators. Now that Artificial has begun screening and people have a sense of how it takes Altman to task, it’s easy to see why Amazon didn’t want to have its name attached. More than anything else, that decision may be what convinces people to see Artificial in theaters when it releases widely on December 25th, and OpenAI’s impending IPO could make the movie’s messages about Altman feel that much more relevant. What sets Fielder’s You Can See Everything apart from each of these other recent tech films is how direct a view it gives you into the actual life of its subject. The documentary’s production began well after Holmes had already been sentenced to more than 11 years in prison, and Fielder uses his access to grill her about why she doesn’t believe that she defrauded Theranos investors. Invited to live with Holmes and her partner William Evans in a rental home during the month before she was set to begin her sentence, Fielder allows himself to become a character who can’t entirely tell whether his hosts’ supposed wish for radical transparency is genuine. That central tension turns You Can See Everything into a surprisingly fascinating watch that challenges you to consider who’s actually in control of the documentary. Throughout the movie, Fielder repeatedly wonders if Holmes is using him as part of her recent PR campaign, and he leaves the audience to draw their own conclusions. It often feels like Fielder is asking viewers to really listen to how Holmes presents herself on camera and then reconcile that person with what you might have read about Holmes in the news or seen in Hulu’s The Dropout. Whether or not Holmes is really just a committed performer who’s trying to manipulate the public shapes every beat of the film, and it makes You Can See Everything feel like a truly in-depth and honest examination of one of Silicon Valley’s most infamous figures — one who’s already captured so much of the public’s attention. Even though some of these films have been box office flops, their existence reflects a larger cultural shift in how people are thinking about modern technology and the powers behind it. Hollywood has spent years poking fun at and mythologizing founders, and portions of the industry are finally beginning to take a more critical view. As we continue to deal with the fallout of things like Elon Musk’s tenure at DOGE and OpenAI’s quest to bake generative AI into every aspect of our lives, audiences’ appetites for movies like these could grow. And with all of these founders racing to monopolize the next tech frontier, the entertainment industry will probably have plenty of source material to draw from. This implant can restore sight for patients with macular degeneration
2026-10-16

This implant can restore sight for patients with macular degeneration

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Age-related macular degeneration (AMD) affects more than 5 million people around the world. And until now, no treatment could restore their vision. The Prima system, which was approved in Europe in July and is awaiting FDA clearance in the US, changes that. It combines a retinal implant with camera-­equipped eyeglasses to improve sight in patients who develop a blind spot in the middle of their vision due to advanced AMD. After training sessions, more than 80% of recipients were able to read, do crosswords, and use their phones. Developed by the California-based Science Corporation, the system takes advantage of the fact that AMD doesn’t damage all retinal circuitry; only photoreceptors—the cells that convert light into an electrical signal—die off. Prima’s implant mimics those cells and transmits visual information directly to healthy eye circuitry and on to the brain. “We’re leveraging all of the body’s own natural functions to process and transmit the information,” says Frank Brodie, an ophthalmologist at the University of California, San Francisco, and medical director for Prima. Glasses outfitted with a miniature front-facing camera capture what someone is seeing in real time. The video feed is roughly five megapixels running at 30 frames per second, which is similar to TV. The raw video is transmitted to a connected processor via a fiber-optic cable. A small, handheld processor, which can be set on a table or tucked in a pocket, converts the video feed into infrared light signals that route back to the glasses. The device also includes physical buttons that let the wearer adjust contrast, brightness, and zoom. A laser projector on the glasses faces the wearer and shines pulses of near-infrared light directly at the eye at 880 nanometers—an invisible wavelength. This signal passes through the pupil and on to the retinal implant in the back of the eye. The implant is made up of 378 pixels, each with an electrode and two photovoltaic cells, which do double duty. They convert the light into a current that healthy bipolar and ganglion cells pass on to the brain. They also act as solar cells to power the chip, eliminating the need for a battery. Linda Marsa is a former Los Angeles Times reporter and an author who covers medicine, health, science, and the environment. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences.
