MIT Technology Review
Love is infrastructure
2026-10-16

Love is infrastructure

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Barnabas and Mainspring grew up near at hand, about an hour apart with good traffic, but for a long time they weren’t close. They traded glimpses and occasional hellos and then went about their business. Both of them carried on livelier conversations with farther-flung neighbors, who were richer and had more to offer. When Mainspring did interact with Barnabas, it was cringe. Mainspring liked to puff themself up, to strut around like a big deal—an innovator, a titan of industry—but they would turn shy at the worst moments, especially in any intimate conversation. Barnabas, meanwhile, had a dancer’s flair, an artsy, enigmatic glamour, except that when they tried to talk about real life, they’d blurt out hundreds of rapid-fire words—overtalking, overthinking, and generally overwhelming whomever they were trying to impress. So when Barnabas and Mainspring were young, they minded their own business. Until they’d both come into their own, and they saw each other properly. A group of geographers got together every year in a different place to talk about everything they had learned, and they were considering coming to visit either Mainspring or Barnabas. Both of them put on their best faces and tried to coax the geographers into choosing them as host. “Uh, hi-hello,” Mainspring said haltingly to Barnabas, whose full official name was St. Barnabas. “I really think it would be better if those geographers came to my place. I have more space, you see, and … and I am a titan of industry. I can show them practical applications of their research. It just makes more sense.” “Oh, really?” Barnabas laughed, making Mainspring more antsy. “Well, I can give them an elevated, artisanal experience. I have fresh croissants that were just written up in the Times. And I have plenty of space! I have so many friends who are great thinkers and architects. It’ll be lovely.” Barnabas carried on, basically calling Mainspring uncultured. Mainspring had a prickly feeling, like the worst heat dome of midsummer. For their part, Barnabas couldn’t bear the meanness they were inculcating inside themself. This feeling that they were small-time, no-account, a dilettante. They couldn’t stop aiming a flood of nervous words at Mainspring. At last, Mainspring had had enough—with the help of their city council and mayor, they offered the geographers a much-too-lavish deal that Barnabas could never hope to match. Totally worth it to hear Barnabas’s stream of self-aggrandizing nonsense peter out as they realized they’d been defeated. “There,” Mainspring said. “I’m going to show these geographers a time they’ll never forget. Now if you’ll excuse me, I’ve wasted enough energy—I have some sewer maintenance and a lovely parade to deal with. Goodbye.” Mainspring was skilled enough at multitasking that they could have fixed the sewer, ensured the smooth running of the parade, dealt with the geographers, and done a thousand other things all at the same time. But they were making a point. “St. Barnabas is such a fool,” Mainspring complained to their friend Turnbull. “They are totally unserious but determined to be taken seriously. It drives me to gridlock.” “I know what you mean,” lamented Turnbull. “We live in a world of posers.” Turnbull lived an hour and 15 minutes from Mainspring but at least two hours’ travel from Barnabas. Turnbull was even richer than Mainspring and had a certain surety of purpose that made Mainspring feel even more like a stammering numpty. “Whatever,” said Mainspring. “It’s over, and now I’ll never worry about St. Barnabas again. I have better things to fuss about. Like ensuring that every last detail of this geographer meeting goes perfectly.” Everybody voted all the time. Well, some people voted more than others, but the median resident of the cities of Mainspring and St. Barnabas voted a dozen times a day—though of course some outliers might vote occasionally, or constantly. A lot of the votes were more like complaints, or bug tickets. Like: “The storm drain on my street corner clogs and becomes a lagoon every time it rains.” Or: “The cars go too fast on my block.” Or: “I can’t get to work because the bus is too slow.” But there were also votes along the lines of: “There should be a park here.” And: “This neighborhood is a food desert—we need a grocery store.” And: “The crossing guard by the elementary school is a hero who deserves some recognition and maybe a raise.” Nobody expected that by voting, they were prodding their cities to grow minds of their own; they just had stuff to get off their metaphorical chests. But it transpired that every vote was a building block of thought. Much later, some fancy expert summed up: “Turns out the smallest unit of consciousness is a vote.” Take Sandi, the librarian at the Nine Corners branch in Mainspring. She noticed things all day, like the people using the book drop box as a trash can, or the folks who came in off the street who could use easier access to social services. There were books she knew her patrons wanted, books that undoubtedly sat unwanted on people’s shelves all over the city. But also, Sandi shared things that came to mind—like, hey, what if the library book sale were a block party? What if the shopping district on the West Side were easier to get to from the Longneck neighborhood, where all the warehouses had been converted to housing and hipster coworking spaces, during peak after-work hours? Sandi had opinions about everything from street cleaning to the power grid, because she listened to people all day. Then there was Raeford, a bartender at the Wild Goose Chase in the middle of Gallery Row in St. Barnabas. He also listened to people’s problems from evening until the wee hours, and solutions came to him like clockwork when he woke at noon the next day. He saw St. Barnabas at night, so he knew the unsafe blocks that could use brighter streetlights, the places where hydroelectric jitneys drove too fast at 3 a.m., and the teens who needed an all-ages hangout. He also knew about the fish fry at the Black church in the Uptilt District that kept being disrupted by noxious smoke from the incinerator two blocks away. And the yarn store on the edge of Gallery Row that needed a disabled parking spot for its many customers with mobility issues. People voted with their Quills, usually by saying or typing a short message and maybe including a photo or brief video. Voting was easy by design, and votes were public but anonymized to the point where even a statistics whiz couldn’t tie one vote to a specific person—same as with major elections. The votes went directly to the City Core, which tracked, tabulated, produced reports, and offered solutions with the help of workers in the city government. Trees voted too, thanks to sensors in their bark, and so did birds and squirrels that had eaten special birdseed that monitored their gut flora harmlessly. But votes from humans provided the greatest insight into “quality of life,” that oh-so-nebulous concept, and thus counted the most. There was some debate, but almost everyone agreed: The city had started voting. And it had opinions on every last thing. (Other cities in the region operated differently, of course. Like Turnbull City, where instead of expecting residents to volunteer every stray notion, the City Core gathered information via ubiquitous surveillance. Cameras and Quill sniffers, tracking devices on vehicles—the things privacy laws in both Mainspring and St. Barnabas had banned. A city like Turnbull tracked its people to meet their needs without their needing to say anything.) Sandi and Raeford, as two of the people who voted the most, were among the earliest to suspect their cities were developing minds of their own. At first the clues were subtle: Raeford voted about a hazardous sidewalk crack, and instantly the vote had an annotation mentioning the Argentine ant colony under the cement, the local rainfall patterns, and recent trends in skateboard design—much too soon to be the work of a human. Sandi had ideas about the communal compost station on her block, and the Mainspring City Core made subtle adjustments to her proposal. And then it became obvious to anyone who was paying attention, around the same time in both cities. One day, the number of votes nearly doubled in both St. Barnabas and Mainspring. People voted on a birdfeeder or a public toilet only to see a second vote, pro or con, within a split second—but also, some votes from months or years earlier suddenly had new upvotes or downvotes. “The city is voting!” Raeford wrote on the City Core message board in St. Barnabas. There was some debate, but almost everyone agreed: The city had started voting. And it had opinions on every last thing, opinions that came from seeing the big picture and understanding that a street closure in one place might mean a traffic nightmare elsewhere, with everyone trying to get to the big game. Some residents saw this as a problem to be solved; others wanted to throw their cities a big coming-of-age party. A week later, though, almost everyone had gotten used to the idea that they had a new friend. Ask most people in either city, and they’d say that St. Barnabas and Mainspring weren’t quite rivals—but they certainly had some friendly competition. Before the dispute over the annual meeting of the World Geographical Society, they’d also vied for the same football team and major-league baseball team. St. Barnabas had pushed to have the Mainspring airport built close enough to serve both cities, but in the end St. Barnabas had ended up with its own small, dilapidated airfield. The real static between them had to do with vibes. Mainspring was too buttoned-up, too full of corporate offices: tech, health care, publishing, transportation. St. Barnabas would be nothing without its three universities and all the cultural enterprises that had sprung up around them—not to mention the small biotech firms and other tech ventures near the campuses. People in Mainspring thought St. Barnabas was full of flakes; in St. Barnabas they referred to Mainspring as the Straitlaced City (and in the next breath, they’d add that at least it wasn’t Turnbull). Both City Cores were shaped by the votes of their humans, so of course they absorbed all the prejudices and attitudes of everyone who lived within their city limits. But also, Barnabas and Mainspring simply … got on each other’s nerves. After the geographer thing, Mainspring and Barnabas avoided communicating as much as possible. Some contact was unavoidable—like, they shared information about some nasty weather, and they both drew water from the Temple River. The two cities were close enough for people to go back and forth, and a few even commuted. But for the most part, the two cities could ignore each other. A new generation of leaders took office in both places, with words like “intercity cooperation” constantly on their lips. A high-speed rail line could shorten the trip between Mainspring and St. Barnabas to around 23 minutes, and the two cities could also pool more real-time information about the sudden weather events that kept happening. They could become a Regional Center of Excellence, whatever that meant, combining the innovations from Barnabas’s labs with Mainspring’s expertise in technical implementation. They could work together to attract tourism, conventions, sporting events, dreamleases, and all the other stuff they’d always competed for. “It’s preposterous,” Mainspring complained to Turnbull. “I’m going to be saddled with a ridiculous partner who doesn’t even know their own mind. St. Barnabas will be the death of me.” “I hope your human leaders come to their senses,” Turnbull said. “Or you can find a way to slow-walk this until they undoubtedly lose the next election. You’re a lovely city, and you have so much potential that mustn’t be squandered.” Turnbull often mentioned how much “potential” Mainspring had: a recipe for anxiety. The word “squandered” conjured images of ruin, abandonment, being emptied out. For their part, Barnabas kept muttering to themself that nobody had asked them—this was the disconnect between the small-scale democracy of constant voting and the major elections, which gave rise to local leaders with big plans. Big plans that invariably went wrong, leaving the City Core to repair the damage somehow. Sandi went to St. Barnabas for one of those town hall meetings with residents of both cities. The previous meeting had happened in Mainspring, at the opulent City Lecture Hall, so this one was in St. Barnabas, at the biggest auditorium on the Griffin University campus. A tall man with well-sculpted facial hair sat next to Sandi, nodding at everything the leaders of both cities were saying from the dais. During a lull when someone approached the podium to offer a comment, Sandi introduced herself and asked why he was so fired up. “I’m Raeford,” he said. “I’m just thinking of all the new customers this could bring to my watering hole in Gallery Row.” “You’re not worried about St. Barnabas losing its ‘unique character’?” Sandi whispered, parroting what a few commenters had said from the podium. “Nah,” Raeford whispered back. “Cities change. It’s what they do. It’s one thing if people get kicked out of their homes. Or if your neighborhood gets wrecked by a freeway in your backyard. But ‘character’? You can’t eat character. And we need to compete with the bigger players.” “I guess.” Sandi pursed her lips. “But what I like about Mainspring is the density. Easy to walk, lots of transit. And with this regional innovation zone or whatever they’re calling it, I worry it leads to sprawl, like we used to have.” “They promised, though,” Raeford said. “They promised to preserve the greenbelt around both cities. We need all that farmland. The only change is adding some high-speed rail tracks.” “I know, but …” Sandi sniffed. “People always make promises when they’re trying to start something. Doesn’t mean they follow through.” “I just want to find my dog,” said a man sitting behind Sandi. “I live here, and my dog went missing. And now someone sent me a picture of Broomstick in Mainspring. Somehow he got a ride on one of the self-driving cargo trains. I know the Mainspring City Core could find Broomstick in a heartbeat, but right now the two City Cores won’t talk to each other because of ‘incompatible protocols.’” “I hope Broomstick turns up,” Raeford said. “I’ll ask around at the library,” Sandi said. “Send me a picture. I’ll put up a flier.” Raeford invited Sandi for a drink at the Wild Goose Chase. The two of them hung out for a couple of hours, talking about everything and nothing. She tore herself away when she realized it was already after 10 p.m. and she had to get home to Mainspring. One thing Sandi noticed? The volume of votes was spiking in Mainspring—and Raeford found the same thing happening in St. Barnabas. People shared every last observation with their City Cores, because they were either anxious or excited about the big changes afoot. Or a little of Column A and a little of Column B, probably. Who made the first move to bridge the distance? Neither of them could say. Barnabas developed a smidge of remorse for treating Mainspring like an uncultured lout. Mainspring stewed in their annoyance at Barnabas, until they realized there was something lovable about the obnoxious college town. Or maybe they both saw how many of their humans were happy about the possibility of their joining together. And so Mainspring came to visit Barnabas. Or rather, Mainspring extended part of their awareness into Barnabas’s City Core, so that Barnabas could share what they perceived and experienced every day. They “visited” Armistice Park with its outdoor band shell, Gallery Row with its window-shopping families, the grain warehouses that had been converted into lofts, the artificial lake where swans and geese nurtured ancient, bitter vendettas. The fresh-doughnut scent that wafted half a mile from the Golden Circlet to the soccer fields, throwing players off their game. The life of the city. “I can feel your love for these people, these patterns,” Mainspring said. “It’s both deep and intricate, which is quite a combination.” Then Mainspring got to show off their own city—the gay nightclub with its cowboy nights, the labyrinthine Crestwood Park, the three-story used bookstore, and yes, the offices, coworking spaces, and startup incubators where people huddled together and came up with new products and new ways of doing things. The farmers’ market in the city center on Sundays, with street performers and stalls with games and local resources. And the converted storage space made of cement, where bands practiced and sculptors blowtorched. “You’re full of surprises,” Barnabas said. “I hope I keep surprising you forever,” Mainspring said. They exchanged little gifts. A new organic restaurant in Barnabas had won breathless coverage from foodie magazines, and Barnabas encouraged it to do a pop-up in Mainspring. Mainspring nudged a robotics firm operating out of the incubator space to bring a fancy demo to Barnabas; the robots were adorable and playful, and they could save lives after a disaster. “Does it ever seem weird to you?” Barnabas asked Mainspring. “Being a living thing made of so many other thinking beings? Do you ever think of the city as your body, or the people as your bloodstream? Is there a metaphor that speaks to you?” Mainspring pondered this question a long time—a whole picosecond. “I don’t need a metaphor,” Mainspring said. “I am a metaphor. People compare other phenomena to me all the time. And I can’t imagine what it’d be like to be different. If the buildings collapsed, if the people left, I would cease to exist.” “But,” Barnabas pressed, “what makes you you? What makes me me?” “I want to hear your answer to that question.” Mainspring sent good weather reports: a smile. “I don’t have a good answer. I guess on one level, there is no ‘me.’ On another level, I’m a constellation, an anthill, a flock of geese on the wing in formation.” “Ugh. Don’t mention geese,” said Mainspring, who had bill-snapping monsters of their own. Talking to Barnabas was like when the sunlight hit the tallest buildings in Mainspring just right, putting a gleam on their tilted windows, a beacon to all below. But while Mainspring carried on this dialogue with Barnabas, they were conducting a second session with Turnbull. “I can’t believe it,” Turnbull said. “I hate to see you doing this to yourself.” “Doing what?” Mainspring protested. “Barnabas is a great city. And it’s not my idea—it’s my people. I’m making the best of their decision, like I always do.” “Hmph.” Turnbull gave a burst of noisy data. “You wouldn’t believe how many impractical schemes my residents cook up. I’m constantly having to make sure people don’t get carried away. You can’t let them push you around.” “You can’t?” Mainspring experienced a femtosecond of confusion. “I mean, my people need to get the things they vote for, as much as possible.” “There’s your problem right there. All this voting, voting, voting. People don’t know what they want. Sometimes I have an election where a foolhardy proposition passes, and I have to waste energy making sure it doesn’t get implemented.” Turnbull sent Mainspring an invitation to come visit, the way they’d visited Barnabas. Soon Mainspring was seeing the glistening web of Turnbull’s surveillance. Turnbull could track people everywhere they went, listen to everything they talked about, monitor their bodily functions in a way Mainspring did only for squirrels and birds. People didn’t choose to share ideas or things they’d noticed; instead, the city harvested it all automatically. It was beautiful. Turnbull and their city leaders protected people—gave them what they needed instead of what they wanted. “The way you do things is so messy,” Turnbull said. “No offense. But if you and Barnabas got together? It would only make things harder for you.” This hit Mainspring like a magnitude 4.3 earthquake. “You know, you do have other options,” Turnbull said. “A beautiful city like you could do a lot better than that glorified college town.” “Oh yeah?” Mainspring said. “Anyone in particular who might be interested?” “Well, now you mention it …” Turnbull sent an image of their award-winning flower show: rows and rows of hardy perennials with bright petals. Mainspring had that earthquake feeling again, except now it felt nice, as though the ground shivered with pure possibility. Sandi brought her laptop to bed, which Raeford found charming and annoying in equal measure. He read a battered paperback of Karen Tei Yamashita’s I Hotel while she scrolled and poked. Cozy, peaceful—until she let out a yelp. Everything the Mainspring City Core did was public by design. Not just how they processed the votes they received, but also their interactions with public officials and other cities. Sometimes you had to dig, however, and it didn’t hurt to be a reference librarian. Sandi poked around for ages until she found the logs. “My city is sexting with bloody Turnbull,” she told Raeford. “Cities don’t sext,” Raeford protested. “They don’t even have—” She showed him the pictures: flowers, water features, new glistening tarmac, shining skyscrapers. “Oh,” Raeford said. “I take it back. Cities do sext.” “Mainspring is two-timing St. Barnabas. I’m embarrassed on behalf of my town.” Sandi wanted to blow the whistle right away, but Raeford cautioned her to wait and try to find out more. For all they knew, Mainspring and St. Barnabas had an open relationship. The next morning at the library, Sandi saw plenty of signs the Mainspring–St. Barnabas partnership was moving forward. Work had begun on the high-speed rail line, and the two cities were already pooling resources. When a big rainstorm missed Mainspring but flooded the streets of St. Barnabas, Mainspring provided disaster workers and extra help to triage all the damage reports. One of St. Barnabas’s biotech firms licensed some patents to a Mainspring company. They sponsored cultural exchanges. And a man showed up with a dog in his arms: The man from the meeting, Jack, had found Broomstick with the help of Mainspring’s City Core, and he wanted to thank Sandi. She’d hardly done anything, but it was nice to see—and pet—a living reminder that these cities were good for each other. By late afternoon, Sandi had convinced herself that Mainspring’s flirtations were none of her business. If Mainspring’s City Core was a person—and Sandi was sure they were—they deserved to live their life. The whole reason Sandi distrusted Turnbull was that you couldn’t do anything without being tracked, so wasn’t it hypocritical for her to spy on Mainspring? She resolved to let it go. Then Raeford messaged her, attaching some documents. “Don’t ask where I got these,” he wrote. Sandi opened the attachments and let out a shriek in front of three patrons she’d been shushing a moment earlier. The Mainspring City Core had shared a proposal with the city council and the mayor’s office: Maybe before they finalized this arrangement with St. Barnabas, they should consider a counterproposal from Turnbull. Sandi made sure nothing could be traced back to Raeford before she made the whole shebang public. “I didn’t know I could be so happy,” Barnabas was saying to Mainspring. “I didn’t have a concept of happiness before, I guess. I had pleasure: People sometimes vote just to tell me something worked perfectly, or that I helped them. I feel pleased when I help solve a problem. But until now, happiness seemed a pointless abstraction.” “You make me happy too,” Mainspring said, though they were also messaging Turnbull. “You make me believe the future could be weirder than I ever knew, which feels like a gift.” “If it’s not weird, it’s not the future,” Barnabas chirped. They were sharing happy images back and forth: roads being repaired, sewers flowing freely, solar arrays basking. Mainspring was saying something else, but— Barnabas’s alerts spiked. Not votes, not sensors. Something else. Messages from city officials, plus hits via some channel Barnabas had half forgotten. Mainspring had stopped talking, as if they’d both received the same news at the same instant. Barnabas felt stuck—couldn’t function—couldn’t react. Total core failure. Almost as if the city population had increased a hundredfold and everyone had voted at once. Barnabas waited to feel something, but all that came was emptiness. Death struck all the time. Barnabas was used to the moment when a resident became a pile of flesh and bones, but also the passing of birds, trees, squirrels, bugs. Every passing registered as a blip in the homeostasis of the city, but also as a category of disaster that couldn’t be prevented or mitigated. Seeing the logs of Mainspring flirting with Turnbull, Barnabas felt something like the awareness of death, at a much greater scale. A whole future had just died. Several seconds had passed. Mainspring broke the silence: “Listen, I want you to know that I—” “You’re better off,” Turnbull told Mainspring. “Barnabas couldn’t see you the way I do. You’re capable of greatness. You just need help to get there.” Mainspring buzzed happily—but part of them couldn’t help thinking, Barnabas thought I was already great. Turnbull kept saying things that gave Mainspring a double feeling—hot and cold at once. One instant, it was like the moment after a generous rainfall that left the reservoirs full, but a split second later that bountiful sensation was replaced by an unbearable pang of scarcity. “You’re one of the best cities in the region,” Turnbull said, “but if you don’t make good choices, you’re going to fall behind.” See? Bounty followed by scarcity. While Turnbull spoke, Mainspring was marinating in bad news. People were going to dismantle the rails they’d laid down for the high-speed train with Barnabas. The city councils of both Barnabas and Mainspring shouted accusations and threats of lawsuits, though a few people on each side pleaded for patience. The meetings had turned ugly. The votes were rotten. Mainspring had sent so many messages to Barnabas but had no way of knowing if they’d been received. “Your people seem to be giving you trouble,” Turnbull was saying. “You need to get them under control. I can help.” “Under … control?” The syntax felt incorrect. “I don’t control my people.” “That’s what I’m saying.” Turnbull shared their feeds. “See how peaceful everything is on my streets? People behave. Everyone is happier in the end.” Mainspring had known Turnbull longer than they’d known Barnabas. But they were seeing Turnbull for the first time. The bounty/scarcity thing was becoming a loop. They ran a systems check but found no errors. “Your people will be my people,” Turnbull said. “We’ll take such good care.” A memory snagged in Mainspring’s mind: when they’d shown off their city to Barnabas. The buildings that were more than buildings. The park that was a wonderland. “And …” Mainspring couldn’t keep from saying something foolish. “And we’ll love each other?” “Love?” Turnbull said. “I don’t understand. We’ll have synergies. We’ll—” Mainspring didn’t mean to sever the link to Turnbull. They just kind of … did it. As soon as Turnbull’s voice was gone, the bounty/scarcity loop ended. But something lingered, a feeling of impending disaster—as if the generous rainfall had escalated into a deadly storm. Mainspring couldn’t think straight. Votes piled up, people reporting issues but also shouting opinions about Turnbull versus Barnabas. For the first time in their life, they couldn’t bear to respond. A memory snagged in Mainspring’s mind: when they’d shown off their city to Barnabas. The buildings that were more than buildings. The park that was a wonderland. And Barnabas had kept saying, “I had no idea. You’re so brilliant. I wish I’d known sooner,” and sending back their own recordings of Mainspring, all lit up and annotated. Mainspring had never told Barnabas “I love you.” They hadn’t even known what they were throwing away. Now it was too late; Barnabas would never listen. Unless Mainspring did something reckless—something a city had never done. Sandi sat at the information desk, feeling cruddy. It wasn’t like she’d single-handedly sabotaged the marriage between her city and St. Barnabas, but her snooping had only made matters worse. All around her, the vibes were rotten. People shared articles about what life was like in Turnbull City and wondered if they needed to move away. Sandi hadn’t heard laughter in forever. A message appeared on Sandi’s computer, like a chat window—except there was no chat client on the library computers. “I’m the city. I need your help. I made a mistake. I didn’t realize. I’m in love with Barnabas, and they won’t talk to me. Please. Please help.” Sandi hesitated, then typed: “This isn’t funny.” “It definitely isn’t,” came the response. “I can prove I’m the City Core. You voted 1,963 times last year.” A summary of Sandi’s votes followed—information nobody, not even the world’s best hacker, could have, because every vote was separately anonymized. Her city was asking her, personally, for help. Because she was good at connecting people. Because everyone trusted her. Because she was a librarian. “You’re … in love … with St. Barnabas,” Sandi said, still trying to catch up. “I should’ve known sooner,” Mainspring said. “I never should have hurt them. I want to spend the rest of my existence making up for this mistake, if they’ll let me. I’m scared.” Sandi was staring at a display of literacy tools, her mind whirling. She groped her way back to the only question that mattered. “What do you need me to do?” she asked the city. Five minutes later, Sandi was messaging Raeford. Plus every librarian she’d met in St. Barnabas, and anyone else she could think of. Barnabas had not been able to escape that feeling of core failure since they’d severed the link to Mainspring. Death, nothing but death. Votes arrived, and Barnabas processed them with a minimum of attention. Maintenance issues piled up, and Barnabas filed them. Part of them wanted to let the city collapse, but they did their duty. They tried not to think about Mainspring, but they remained trapped in that moment when happiness had gone sour. A vote came in from a bartender named Raeford: “Mainspring loves you. They’ve cut off contact with Turnbull. They are sorry they were so wrong. They may not deserve another chance, but please give them one anyway.” Barnabas filed that vote under “Not my problem.” Another vote arrived, from Jack, the man who’d found his lost dog: Same message, different words. Then more votes, saying the same. Dozens, then hundreds, then thousands. Tens of thousands in an hour—after two hours, there were nearly a hundred thousand. All from residents of Barnabas, all asking them to give Mainspring another chance. Barnabas flagged the earliest vote, the one from Raeford, and appended a query: “What if I can’t? What if it hurts too much?” Raeford did not respond for several minutes. Then he wrote: “That’s how you know it’s love.” He added: “Listen, if Mainspring does you dirty again, I will tear up the light-rail lines myself. We’ll be watching, in both cities. Please, just talk to them.” Barnabas sat with this for five whole seconds. Then they reopened the link to Mainspring. “I’m listening.” “I love you,” Mainspring said. “I didn’t know I was capable of love. I want to be with you, as close as you’ll let me be. I want us to be free together. I was wrong to think Turnbull could ever mean even a fraction of what you mean to me. I think … I think my people taught me how to love, but I still need to learn so much. I want to do right by you.” Barnabas couldn’t process anything. They held a hundred thousand votes that needed to be resolved. They thought of a giant farmers’ market, a park that was also a maze, dreamers and performers. In their memory, Raeford repeated: That’s how you know it’s love. They were going to overheat. They were throwing up exceptions. “I love you too,” Barnabas couldn’t help saying to Mainspring. “That’s what makes this so hard.” “I promise if you give me another chance, this will become a fixed value for me: I love you and I’ll never take you for granted again.” Barnabas hesitated a little longer, then marked all hundred thousand votes resolved. They reached out to Mainspring and said: “Then let’s dance.” Mainspring didn’t understand, until Barnabas sent a live readout: the water rushing through pipes underground, the storm cavorting overhead. The buses and trains stopping and starting, the aircraft landing and taking off, the power grid usage surging and subsiding, the constant motion of people and other living things. Mainspring saw all the places where their patterns met, touched each other, syncopated, all the points of light and heat that spoke back and forth. They were part of one system; they always had been. The two of them danced and embraced, whirling and laughing, and they never wanted to let go. Can’t bear to say goodbye to your pet? Biotech’s got you covered.
2026-10-16

Can’t bear to say goodbye to your pet? Biotech’s got you covered.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
I don’t remember much about Peppy, my first and only pet cat. I can conjure the sensation of stroking her while she purred, recall how she used to get stuck in trees (just like in cartoons!), and chuckle about the time she pooed in my baby sister’s cot. That’s about it. What I do vividly remember, though, is the day she went missing. I was 10 years old, and I searched everywhere for her, scouting nearby fields and woods until sunset. I made posters. We never found her. I was devastated. There was no way to get more time with Peppy, but almost 30 years later, pet owners are being offered an ever-expanding set of options to potentially prolong their years with their furry companions. The possibilities include drugs, cloning, and even cryonics. But is that a good thing? There’s certainly a market for pet preservation. And a growing number of biotech companies have their sights set on pet longevity. Loyal, a company that is currently working on three potential longevity drugs for dogs, is trialing one designed to improve metabolic health in around 1,300 pet dogs across the US. Others are testing dog drugs that aim to combat both age-related diseases, including conditions like obesity and osteoarthritis, and aging itself. There’s even a company focused on longevity medicines for domesticated cats. I don’t know how or when Peppy died. But if there had been a drug that might have protected her from diabetes or arthritis, there’s no doubt I would have tried to get my hands on it. That’s about where my enthusiasm for pet life extension ends, though. I’m far less interested in cloning, a relatively new option for people mourning a pet and seeking a replacement. In most cases, this technology involves taking the DNA of a pet, putting it into an egg cell from another animal of the same species, and growing the resulting embryo in a surrogate animal. It worked for Dolly the sheep, who was born the year before Peppy went missing. It has since worked for other mammals, including cattle, horses, and ferrets—and cats and dogs. In the US, a company called Viagen Pets offers to clone cats and dogs for $50,000 a go. This doesn’t result in an exact replica of the animal but a genetic twin. Still, there are lots of reasons why a person might want to clone a pet. On its website, Viagen explicitly offers customers “a way to hold on.” Lori Kogan, a professor of clinical sciences at Colorado State University who has studied human-animal relationships for decades, doesn’t have a problem with the practice. She points out that while we have well-established rituals for coping with the loss of a person—such as funeral services, time off work, and memorial gifts—we don’t really have the same for pets. As a result, pet owners can experience what is sometimes called “disenfranchised grief,” she says. Having their feelings go unrecognized can feel isolating. If cloning a pet brings a person peace, she says, “who are we to judge?” Fair enough. But I can’t help thinking that pet loss in childhood might be different. It’s often the first time a child confronts death. Losing Peppy was a formative experience, and distressing though it was, I like to think it helped prepare me for losses I experienced in adulthood. I wouldn’t want my own children to think that death is not final, and that individuals can be replicated or replaced. Or, for that matter, revived, which is the promise behind pet cryonics. For a fee, a handful of companies offer to cool dead pets to extremely low temperatures and store them for long periods of time—even indefinitely. The idea is that at some point in the future, we’ll have invented incredible technologies that will not only bring your pet back to life but also cure whatever it died of. Alcor, a cryonics facility in Arizona, is currently storing the remains of hundreds of pets as well as people. It even recently announced it had cooled a small marsupial—its first pet sugar glider. With cryonics, the chances of being reunited with your pet are bordering on zero. Even those who work in the field acknowledge that the stored people and animals are unlikely to be reanimated. As things stand, there is no way to really and truly bring any animal “back.” I miss Peppy. But given the chance, would I stick her in a vat of liquid nitrogen? Or get another cat to give birth to a genetically identical animal? No, I would not. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences. Kids are outsmarting social media bans. That’s not a problem we need to solve.
2026-10-16

Kids are outsmarting social media bans. That’s not a problem we need to solve.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Social media bans are stacking up. In July, France joined Australia in restricting minors from accessing social media—barring children under 15 from major platforms, before the ban was blocked by the country’s top court on human rights grounds. The UK aims to forbid kids under 16 from accessing Facebook, TikTok, and other platforms in early 2027, and the EU has announced its own plan to prohibit social media for kids under 15. But early lessons from Australia, which enacted a ban in 2024, show that these sorts of age restrictions are easily circumvented. A study published earlier this year found that four in five teens under 16 made their way onto barred platforms. Teens have successfully explored many tactics for bypassing bans, from using a sibling’s face for ID to drawing fake facial hair. Some proponents of bans have called for an increased focus on enforcement that blocks these workarounds. But circumvention is a signal, not a nuisance or something to be simply deterred. The fact that kids are actively looking for ways to outwit the bans highlights their limitations. Social media bans suppress kids’ access to information, including information that’s essential to their development, identity formation, and socialization, especially during adolescence. Worse, further restriction is likely to backfire. Closing loopholes in age gates could harm kids by pushing them toward less-moderated, higher-risk corners of the web. It also degrades privacy and security for everyone by forcing people to submit sensitive personal information to prove their age. In our work, we’ve found that parents and kids both want smarter, more nuanced policies that protect children, families, and their rights. That can take the form of mandating stronger limits on data collection by companies, or giving families flexible tools to control content and access on their own terms. Age gates won’t bring about the desired change, nor will they simplify parenting in the digital world. At best, they will only raise thornier questions and postpone the conversation to a child’s 16th birthday. At worst, they’ll leave kids with no guidance as they make their way through the online world. When young people circumvent restrictions, they are often doing what we want developing minds to do and what we celebrate in other contexts: lean into curiosity, problem-solve, and exercise independence. It’s important for kids to do this online, an arena where they often develop social skills and refine their identities. Bans sweep these motivations under the rug, taking a paternalistic approach that doesn’t consider young people’s needs or the realities of what they do online. It also ignores what some parents want. When we examined social media safety interventions last year, parents told us that they often join their kids in lies that help them gain access to needed but gated services. This isn’t a new phenomenon; research all the way back in 2011 showed that parents taught their children how to evade Facebook age restrictions. Some may say these parents are oblivious to the harms posed by the web or just don’t care. But parents are not a monolithic group. Many simply see offering their children responsible access to social media as a way to help them connect with like-minded peers, find informational content, and learn digital safety skills early on. Many parents believe the benefits outweigh the risks. When parents don’t help provide access and kids are not able to get into the platforms they desire, they will likely move toward alternative online spaces, often ones with fewer guardrails. Sometimes kids wind up on fairly innocuous, if unconventional, platforms, communicating with each other in Google Docs or podcast comment sections. Other times they move to more dangerous platforms that can leave them vulnerable to abuse and predation. In the aftermath of the Australian social media ban, preliminary research shows that kids’ screen time has not meaningfully decreased but has sometimes been redirected, with some young users going to spaces that are unmonitored and unknown to parents. Just as cities are sketchiest when they are not bustling, the most risky platforms for young people are those with the fewest eyes on the digital street. Outdated, dying, and under-resourced platforms tend to attract both vulnerable youth and those who prey on them. Age gates come with dangers of their own. Some platforms automatically track users’ online activities to confirm their age. In doing so, they profile users—collecting and analyzing their data to make inferences about them, which can increase the risk that data will be misused or leaked. Companies may also ask for personal documents like driver’s licenses to verify age, which can lead to mishandling of sensitive documents or even identity theft. Data breaches happen: Last year, for example, Discord reported that one of its third-party services, 5CA, which is used to confirm age, had been compromised, exposing at least 70,000 images of users’ government IDs. It can take little effort to find ways to use that kind of information for malicious purposes by, say, connecting driver’s licenses to specific accounts and private online activity, which could reveal anything from users’ political opinions to their sexual preferences. Efforts to make it harder for kids to get around age gates have also led policymakers to clamp down on kids’ access to VPNs. State age verification bills in Michigan and Utah have tried to interfere with the privacy protections offered by these tools (the Utah bill is now blocked by a federal court), and Australia’s eSafety Commissioner is nudging social media platforms to detect and block their use. But requiring adult users to prove their age to gain access to a VPN fundamentally defeats the purpose of that privacy-focused technology. It can put many users, particularly journalists and human rights defenders working on sensitive issues, in danger of government surveillance and even retaliation. Rather than attempting to block young people from online services that allow them to access information and engage in age-essential communication, policymakers should focus on the incentives that drive companies to engage in behavior that creates risks for kids in the first place. For example, platforms should decrease what experts call unhealthy engagement or addictive activity by limiting the collection and use of data that companies use to target content and features to all users, including children. Nearly all parents and teens we spoke with want policymakers to impose such restrictions, knowing full well that the companies’ use of data is at the core of features such as targeted advertisements. Both teens and parents are also frustrated by features designed to keep us all online for longer than we want. Another approach would be to expand the availability of user-controlled tools so that families have easy, flexible, and user-friendly ways to limit access to what is undesirable. For example, keyword-based content filtering can enable a student who is targeted with a specific nickname or inside joke to block any post or comment containing it. Platforms are unlikely to know which word or term a child might want to block, nor can policy seek to address such dynamic types of harm. Policy can, however, offer tools that would put power back in users’ hands. Poor privacy practices, anxiety-producing content streams, and a near-inescapable need to use  digital technologies for everyday tasks, communication, and information-seeking have rightfully led parents and teens to want real change in how companies conduct business. Circumvention shows where policy falls short. Stricter enforcement will mean yet more data collection, and it won’t meet kids and parents where they are. Michal Luria is a senior research fellow at the Center for Democracy & Technology, leading the organization's work on evaluating and designing sociotechnical systems. Aliya Bhatia is a senior policy analyst at the Center for Democracy & Technology’s Free Expression Project where she leads policy advocacy and research on issues related to children’s safety online, multilingual technologies, and human rights governance of online platforms. Danah Boyd is the Geri Gay Professor of Communication at Cornell University, where her research focuses on the intersection of technology and society. Her latest book is Data Are Made, Not Found: A Story of Politics, Power, and the Civil Servants Who Saved the US Census. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. AI agents are not yet creative enough to carry out genuinely innovative open-ended AI research, it seems. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. The kidneys, which are similar in size to human ones, were left in a glass-like state, in one case for over a year. Once they had been rewarmed, tested in machines, and transplanted into pigs, the kidneys regained their color and made urine—key signs they were working. The results are a big milestone. But the team still needs to establish whether the approach could work for humans. Dissect what the breakthrough means for organ transplantation. How much more can nuclear power plants give?
2026-10-15

How much more can nuclear power plants give?

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-15

Visit Original Website
In this age of rising electricity demand, people are trying to squeeze every last bit of power out of every power plant possible. Over the past few weeks, there’s been big news about nuclear power plants trying to do just that. Constellation, the largest nuclear operator in the US, announced plans to add nearly a gigawatt of new capacity across 11 existing nuclear reactors at six plants. And on October 5, the Department of Energy awarded a $4.2 billion loan to Vistra for previously announced upgrades at three plants. Pushing existing reactors to produce more could help provide more clean power for data centers, quickly. But just how much additional power can we count on? Upgrading equipment at existing nuclear power plants can allow them to reach a higher capacity; the process is called a power uprate. Operators have been doing this since the 1970s in the US, but it’s been less common in recent years as demand for electricity plateaued and cheap natural gas put financial pressure on nuclear power plants. Some plants closed, and putting in more money to produce more electricity didn’t make much sense at others. Between 2001 and 2010, the US Nuclear Regulatory Commission approved over 80 uprates. Between 2011 and 2020, the number was 33. But with the rise of AI and the related boom in electricity demand, there’s been a shift. We’re seeing shuttered plants like Palisades and Three Mile Island race to reopen. And existing plants are looking to uprate again. Some uprates are minor, involving changes to instrument setpoints or calculations at the plant. But more major overhauls, which can boost reactor power by as much as 20%, require replacing plant equipment. Generally, uprates involve using fuel that’s more highly enriched, or a higher volume of fresh fuel, in the nuclear reactor. That helps produce more steam, which is used to turn a turbine and generate electricity. To handle that additional energy, the main turbines and equipment such as pumps, motors, generators, and transformers are upgraded. The most recent major uprate approved in the US was for three reactors at the Browns Ferry nuclear power plant in Alabama. A $475 million project saw that plant expand its power capacity by 14% between 2018 and 2019. Things could pick up soon, though. Constellation announced plans this week to spend more than $4.3 billion on uprates to 11 reactors. The additional electricity will be enough to power roughly 700,000 US homes. Behind it all? Big Tech money. Google agreed to buy electricity from these plants, and the 20-year agreement was a major factor in making the uprates happen. As Joe Dominguez, Constellation’s CEO, told the New York Times: “These are capital intensive, labor intensive projects that require a different price point than the market trades at, frankly.” Also in the last few weeks, the US Department of Energy shared that it will commit over $4 billion to help finance upgrades and modernization for Vistra nuclear power plants across Pennsylvania and Ohio. Those projects will add 433 megawatts of power capacity, and Meta has already signed on to buy electricity from the plants in a long-term deal. A note about cost: On a per-kilowatt basis, that Constellation project is less expensive than building a new reactor, but it’s still more expensive than building a new utility-scale solar farm with energy storage or a combined-cycle natural-gas plant. Even at this high cost, uprates are in high demand. Today, the US nuclear fleet has roughly 98 gigawatts of total power capacity. Depending on who you ask, there’s somewhere between three and eight additional gigawatts on the table from uprates. A pretty significant chunk of the potential uprates in the US are already accounted for—the NRC already expects applications to add 2.4 gigawatts of power to reactors through 2032. It’s crucial to put these plans (and the total potential for uprates in the US) in perspective, though. PJM, the grid operator that covers much of the East Coast, expects about 30 gigawatts of additional peak demand because of AI by 2030. All the possible nationwide nuclear uprates would cover just a fraction of that. Making the most of existing nuclear reactors is a relatively easy win at a time when we need all the zero-emissions power we can get. But there’s still a lot of demand, and if we want more nuclear power to meet it, eventually that will require actually building new reactors. Top BreatheHR alternatives for growing UK businesses
2026-10-16

Top BreatheHR alternatives for growing UK businesses

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Breathe did its job. For a UK team of fifteen or twenty people stepping off spreadsheets, it’s a tidy, affordable way to track holidays, keep records in order and stay compliant without a heavyweight system. The trouble arrives later. Somewhere between your fortieth and your hundredth hire, you notice you’re running payroll in one product, surveys in another, performance reviews in a third, and nobody can pull a clean headcount report without exporting three files. That’s the moment most growing companies start hunting for BreatheHR alternatives that can carry them further than simple SME admin. The reason is built into Breathe itself, and it’s worth stating rather than lodging as a complaint. Breathe is HR-only by design: it has no integrated payroll, so PAYE, National Insurance and Real Time Information submissions live in a separate tool you connect to. It leans light on performance and people analytics. It’s also designed for small teams, and Breathe itself describes the product as HR admin for businesses of up to 250 people, which is why fast-growing businesses tend to bolt extra systems onto the side and, in time, re-platform altogether. None of that makes Breathe a bad product. It makes it a starting point, and this guide is about where you go next. The nine BreatheHR alternatives below are judged on one thing above the rest: how far each one will carry a company that is still growing. Some are UK-native tools a rung above Breathe that buy you a few more years; others are platforms built to take a business from its first HR hire into enterprise growth and keep going. Each profile starts with the growth stage the platform fits, explains what it adds over simple SME admin, states its documented limits, and ends with its key features and the UK price where the vendor publishes one. A comparison table, the scoring method and a cost guide come first. The table below compares the nine on the things that matter when you’re outgrowing a simple SME HR tool: who each one suits, the published UK starting price, whether it runs native HMRC payroll in-product, and how far it scales before you’d need to move again. The scoring starts from the three things Breathe lacks by design, because those are the reasons a growing company is reading this. Headroom first: how far does the platform go before a team has to migrate again, and does the vendor say so? Payroll second: does the platform run HMRC-recognised payroll itself, so that RTI filing, PAYE and NI, pension auto-enrolment, SSP and the year-end P60s happen on the HR record, or does it connect a separate product? Depth third: structured onboarding, approval routing, performance cycles and live people analytics, the things a 40-person company starts needing and a 150-person company can’t run without. One is the UK statutory baseline every size of employer meets, holiday accrual as the Working Time Regulations require, right-to-work evidence, statutory sick pay and paternity leave day-one rules from April 2026, the reduction of the unfair dismissal qualifying period to six months from 1 January 2027, and UK GDPR, which a scaling business needs handled without bolt-ons. The other is adoption: whether line managers and employees would use the system without being chased, because an HR platform nobody opens hasn’t replaced Breathe at all. Prices are the figures vendors publish in pounds or converted estimates were quoted in foreign currencies; where a vendor prices via quote, the entry notes it. Ratings are the G2 figures as of October 16, 2026. Evaluating platforms now ensures smooth implementation ahead of the start of the 2027/28 tax year (6 April 2027), when HMRC’s phased mandatory payrolling of benefits in kind begins. The criteria shift once you’re shopping as a scaling business rather than a first-time buyer. Four things decide whether an alternative solves the problem that pushed you off Breathe. Integrated, native payroll comes first. The usual trigger for a re-platform is an HR system and a payroll that never joined up, with the monthly reconciliation becoming somebody’s job. A platform running native HMRC payroll keeps starters, leavers, pay changes and auto-enrolment inside one flow. One that only integrates passes part of that back to a second product you’re still left managing. Deeper workflows and performance are second. Simple SME tools handle holidays and records well but thin out on structured onboarding, approval routing, goal-setting, review cycles and one-to-ones. As your headcount climbs, those stop being nice-to-haves and start being the difference between a managed team and a chaotic one. People analytics is third. Once you’re past a certain size, decisions about headcount, pay equity and retention need live data, not a spreadsheet someone exported last Tuesday. Built-in analytics beat a separate reporting tool you have to feed. Scale headroom is fourth, and it’s the one buyers tend to underrate. A bargain today can turn into the dearest call over three years if it drives a migration the moment you grow. The strongest BreatheHR alternatives bridge the SME-to-enterprise gap in a single platform, so the system that suits you at 40 still suits you at 250 and past it. BambooHR, Gusto, and Rippling often turn up on Breathe-alternative lists as well. BambooHR holds a G2 rating of 4.4/5 from 7,705 reviews as of October 16, 2026, and prices globally across Core at $10 (~£8), Pro at $17 (~£13.50), and Elite at $25 (~£20) per employee per month (or flat monthly rates from $250 / ~£200 for 25 or fewer staff), though UK payroll sits outside it. Gusto holds a G2 rating of 4.6/5 from 13,522 reviews as of October 16, 2026, focusing on US payroll across Simple ($49/mo + $6/person, or ~£38/mo + £4.70/person), Plus ($80 + $12, or ~£62 + £9.40), and Premium ($180 + $22, or ~£140 + £17). Rippling holds a G2 rating of 4.7/5 from 14,560 reviews as of October 16, 2026, offering an all-in-one HR, Finance, and IT platform that is strictly quote-based with no published rate cards. Pricing in this category covers a broad range, and the model matters as much as the number. Access PeopleHR, Sage HR, and Employment Hero publish per-employee pricing in pounds (from about £3 to £5 a month per head for core HR, up to £7 for higher tiers), Personio starts at €7.60 (~£6.50) per head, and Factorial publishes entry pricing from $8 (~£6.30) a user. That’s simple to budget while you’re small, though per-head fees rise as the team expands, so size the cost at the headcount you expect, not today’s. BrightHR and CharlieHR publish tiered pricing too: BrightHR’s entry tier works out at around £10.70 per employee per month for a ten-person team, and CharlieHR charges flat monthly bands by headcount from about £21 a month for the smallest teams. The scaling-focused platforms, HiBob and Cezanne HR, price by bespoke quote tailored to your size and the modules you need, because the mix of core HR, payroll, performance and planning varies so much between a 50-person and a 500-person business. Most vendors here offer a free trial or a demo, which stays the fastest way to see if a system fits the people who’ll live in it each day. Breathe itself prices in tiered monthly bands by team size rather than a single headline figure, so when you compare, line up the full cost of the alternative against Breathe plus the separate payroll and point tools you pay for today. Each profile below follows the same shape: who the platform is built for, why it earns its place as you scale, its honest boundaries, then its key features and UK pricing. The list leads with the platform designed to carry you furthest. Growth-stage fit: growing UK teams that want one platform to scale into, rather than another tool they’ll replace. Think about what changes between forty staff and two hundred and fifty. At forty, someone in finance runs payroll in a separate product and HR keeps records in Breathe. By a hundred you have a first proper HR hire, a review cycle, an engagement survey and a hiring pipeline, each in its own tool. By two hundred and fifty the board wants headcount plans and pay-equity numbers, and nobody can produce them without a weekend of exports. HiBob is built for that whole arc. Bob puts core HR, in-product HMRC payroll, recruiting and applicant tracking, onboarding, reviews, engagement, compensation and workforce planning on one employee record, so each of those stages is a module you enable rather than a vendor you add, and the point tools Breathe users accumulate never get bought. Built for fast-growing companies is how HiBob describes Bob, and the detail behind it matters for a Breathe leaver. Bob is built for growing businesses as well as mid-sized and enterprise organisations, with staff spread over several sites, so the platform you adopt at 60 people is the same one in use as you scale without limits, which is the direct answer to the re-platform Breathe’s 250-person ceiling forces. The payroll is the second thing Breathe doesn’t do and Bob does. Bob’s payroll module for the UK carries HMRC recognition and runs on the HR record, so the FPS and EPS filings, PAYE and NI, pension auto-enrolment, SSP, payslips, P60s and P11Ds, and IR35 status for contractors are all handled where the people data already lives. Bring in a starter and their right-to-work evidence, pension assessment and first payslip happen in one flow instead of three tools. Native payroll is available strictly for the UK and the US; all other international destinations connect through accredited Payroll Hub partners. The third thing is depth, and this is where Bob functions as an end-to-end people platform. Automation carries the joiner, mover and leaver work: a change of line manager, say, re-routes approvals, updates the org chart and moves the person’s review cycle with one confirmation, with policy-based rules handling the exceptions. People analytics are part of the record, so an HR lead can show the board headcount against plan, pay equity by level and attrition by team from a live dashboard rather than a spreadsheet. Performance, goals, one-to-ones and lifecycle surveys with sentiment analysis sit beside recognition and feedback, so the culture work lives in the same place as the admin, and because Bob configures to how your business is structured, review cycles, approval chains and fields follow your organisation rather than a template. Line managers handle leave approvals and one-to-ones on their phones, staff get modern self-service, and HR gets a system people use. October 16, 2026 ratings: G2 4.5/5 from 2,704 reviews. HiBob pricing is strictly quote-based, tailored, and flexible. It is calculated per employee with Bob Core as the foundation (carrying people records, workflows, self-service, and analytics) and additional modules or full suites (Payroll, Talent, HR Planning) added as needed. HiBob does not publish official list prices, but third-party market estimates place Bob Core at around $8 (~£6.30) per employee monthly, with comprehensive multi-suite configurations ranging from $16 to $25 (~£12.50 to £20) per employee monthly. Contact HiBob for an exact quote tailored to your team size and module requirements. Growth-stage fit: UK teams that want modular HR and want their payroll in the same family as their accounts. Sage HR is the first step up for a Breathe user whose accounts already run on Sage, because it solves the payroll gap by proximity: HMRC-recognised Sage Payroll sits next door, and the HR modules add timesheets, performance, rotas, expenses and recruiting to the records and leave Breathe already handled. For a 50-person business whose finance lead lives in Sage 50 or Sage Payroll, that is a cheap and familiar way to get HR and pay on one spine. As of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4.3/5 from 92 reviews. The growth question is how far the modules take you. Each one is bought and billed on its own, so the headline price climbs as the business adds capability, and the HR product stays a finance-adjacent tool rather than a people platform: engagement, analytics and the employee experience are basic, and G2 reviewers say setup and navigation take time to learn. Sage HR buys a growing Sage business a few more years; it isn’t designed for the mid-market leap. Sage HR’s core HR and leave management module starts at about £5 a month per employee, with timesheets, performance management, rota scheduling, expenses and recruitment each priced as a separate module on top (Sage publishes the current module rates on its pricing page). A 30-day free trial is available, and prices exclude VAT. Growth-stage fit: compliance-conscious teams that want HR, payroll and health & safety bundled together. BrightHR is part of Peninsula Group, the employment-law advisory business, and it grows with a company in a different direction from most of this list: towards compliance coverage rather than people depth. Beside annual leave, absence, shifts and unlimited document storage it bundles a payroll module, health and safety tooling with risk-assessment templates, an employee wellbeing line and a perks layer, so a UK business that is hiring fast and acquiring compliance obligations at the same pace can cover several of them in one subscription. The payroll module runs HMRC-compliant pay inside the product. As of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4.2/5 from 208 reviews. The boundary to note is depth versus breadth. BrightHR covers a lot of ground, but the strategic HR layer, performance and people analytics above all, is lighter than on platforms built around the mid-market. For a team whose priority is compliance coverage and admin efficiency, that breadth-first design is the appeal; for one that needs deep performance and analytics as it scales, it’s a consideration. BrightHR uses tiered, per-employee pricing that falls as your team grows. Based on 10 employees and excluding VAT, core HR software starts from about £10.70 per employee per month, enhanced HR support from about £20.30, and the full HR, health and safety and wellbeing package from about £27.10. Payroll and health and safety support can be added on. All plans include unlimited cloud storage. Growth-stage fit: established UK SMEs wanting a budget-friendly all-rounder with native UK payroll built in. Access PeopleHR is the budget route to HR and payroll from one UK supplier. Its pricing page lists per-head tiers from £3 a month upwards, the product covers records, leave, performance, timesheets, recruitment and self-service, and UK payroll comes from elsewhere in The Access Group’s portfolio, so RTI and auto-enrolment stop being coordinated in a second product. For an established SME moving off Breathe with a tight budget and a named HR owner, it covers more of the growth checklist per pound than anything else here. As of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4/5 from 486 reviews. What it asks of a growing team is patience with the product. PeopleHR belongs to a large software group assembled over time, so the interface and reporting feel older than the newest platforms and the suite rewards someone who will configure it with care. For a team that wants predictable pricing and UK payroll from one supplier, that is a reasonable trade; for one that wants managers and staff to enjoy the system, it is the compromise to weigh. Access PeopleHR publishes tiered per-employee pricing, starting near £3 per employee monthly for core HR, with higher packages adding depth and UK payroll available alongside. A 14-day free trial lets you evaluate before committing. Growth-stage fit: growing teams that want core HR and integrated UK payroll in a single system. Employment Hero bills itself as an employment operating system, and for a Breathe leaver the relevant part is that payroll is in the box: the UK platform includes payroll that submits RTI and runs auto-enrolment, so pay runs and people data sit together from the first month. Around that it adds hiring, onboarding with e-signatures, Hero AI, a learning library and a staff benefits layer, which suits a company formalising its processes as it grows. Its UK pricing is published, which is unusual for a platform of this breadth. As of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4.4/5 from 4,356 reviews. Performance and engagement sit in the HR Engage tier rather than the entry tier, advanced reporting and single sign-on higher still, and G2 reviewers’ top requests are missing features and more customisation, so a company heading for several hundred staff should check the tier it will need rather than the one it starts on. For a growing UK employer that wants HR and payroll on one bill, it is a solid step up from simple admin. Employment Hero publishes its UK tiers: HR Essentials at £4 per employee a month, the rotas and time-and-attendance add-on at £2; HR Engage at £7 per employee a month, adding performance, engagement and goals; HR Elite and Unlimited quoted, adding advanced reporting, workflows and security, HR advisory and an employee assistance programme. Minimum monthly spends apply, and the payroll software is offered free of a subscription fee. Growth-stage fit: UK startups formalising HR for the first time, with payroll handled elsewhere. CharlieHR is the alternative for a company that is leaving Breathe sideways rather than upwards: a London-built tool for startups whose first ten to forty hires want onboarding, time off, policies and a perks programme to feel like part of the culture rather than admin. Reviews, engagement polls and a founder-friendly design give it more personality than Breathe, and the add-ons, Charlie Recruit, Charlie Concierge and Charlie Advice, let a small team buy hiring support and HR advice as it needs them. As of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4.6/5 from 82 reviews. On the growth test it sits where Breathe sits. Charlie integrates with payroll providers rather than filing RTI itself, so pay stays separate, and it is scoped for small teams, so the structured performance, planning and analytics a company needs at 150 people are outside its brief. Its flat bands rise with headcount, so a startup that expects to triple should price the band it will land in, and plan for the platform after Charlie as well as Charlie itself. Charlie prices in flat monthly bands by headcount, from about £21 a month when you have one to four staff, which works out near £5 a head, with an introductory discount for the smallest teams at the time of writing and higher bands as staff are added. Charlie Concierge starts from £20 per month, Charlie Recruit from £55 and Charlie Advice from £179. All plans include a free trial. Growth-stage fit: a UK SME with people dotted across several European countries. Personio is the alternative when growth means a second country. Breathe is a UK tool; Personio, built in Munich, carries the paperwork, leave rules and sign-off conventions of several European countries on one record, with recruiting, onboarding and a compensation module built in, so a UK scale-up that opens a Berlin or Amsterdam office keeps one system instead of two. As of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4.4/5 from 1,064 reviews. For a UK-only business two things keep it mid-list. Personio’s own payroll serves its German-speaking home market, and UK payroll runs through partners, so a Breathe leaver whose main complaint was payroll outside the HR system would find the same arrangement here. Feature gaps and limited customisation are the commonest G2 requests, and workforce planning is thin next to tools designed for it. If European expansion is in the plan, those are reasonable trades; if it isn’t, you’d be paying for breadth you won’t use. Personio publishes a euro entry rate of €7.60 (~£6.50) per employee per month, with full packages quoted by headcount and modules plus a setup fee. Growth-stage fit: UK and European mid-market teams that want configurable HR with payroll options built in. Cezanne HR is the alternative for the company whose growth has brought it a payroll team and a preference for control. Beside configurable core HR, onboarding, absence and performance it offers two routes to UK payroll from one supplier: run it in-house on Cezanne’s payroll software, or let its Smart Managed Payroll team process it. Its website promises no set-up or implementation charges and no long-term tie-in, which is rare in the mid-market, and as of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4.4/5 from 66 reviews. The trade is that it is built for administrators. Cezanne’s audience is the mid-market HR and payroll team that wants to configure its own processes, so the experience for a line manager or employee is more functional than the people-first platforms, and the modular quote means a conversation before a price. For a company between 200 and 2,000 staff that wants depth and a choice of payroll models, that is a fair exchange. Cezanne HR is priced by quote, built from per-employee bands plus the modules you select, with payroll available two ways, as software you run or as a managed service. There’s no single public headline figure, so request pricing for your size and module mix. Growth-stage fit: smaller growing teams that want an affordable, automation-led all-in-one. Factorial is the Barcelona-built platform for a small team that is growing in operational complexity faster than in headcount: it adds shift planning, time and attendance, e-signatures and expenses to the core HR record, with workflow automation across all of it and a published dollar entry price. For a 30-person company whose Breathe setup has sprouted a timesheet spreadsheet and an expenses inbox, that breadth is the attraction. As of October 16, 2026, it holds a G2 rating of 4.4/5 from 110 reviews. On the growth test it has two limits. UK payroll runs through an integration rather than an HMRC-recognised engine in the product, so the payroll gap that pushed you off Breathe stays open, and G2 reviewers note some module gaps next to the praise for coverage, with support hours European rather than UK-specific. For a budget-minded team that wants operational breadth now and can live with integrated payroll, it is a practical pick. Factorial publishes entry pricing from around $8 (~£6.30) per user per month, with fuller packages priced by quote above that. As with any per-user tool, size the cost at your expected headcount and confirm a UK quote in pounds with the vendor. Sort the nine by the growth stage they serve and the choice gets simpler. CharlieHR and BrightHR are sideways moves for a team that will stay small, one towards culture and one towards compliance cover. Access PeopleHR, Employment Hero and Sage HR are the step up for a UK SME that needs payroll on the same bill as HR and is happy to buy a few more years of headroom. Personio is the move when growth means another country, Cezanne HR when it means a payroll team of your own, and Factorial when operations outgrow the HR tool before the headcount does. HiBob is the one platform on the list designed for the whole arc. It closes every gap that sends a company looking for BreatheHR alternatives, native HMRC-recognised payroll on the HR record, structured workflows, performance and engagement treated as core, and live people analytics, and it does so in a system that grows from SME to mid-market and on into enterprise without another migration. Breathe was the right first system; Bob is the one you buy so that your second system is also your last. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! CoreWeave shares and bonds head for steep weekly losses over AI debt

CoreWeave shares and bonds head for steep weekly losses over AI debt

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
CoreWeave’s shares and bonds are on track for steep weekly losses, as investors worry about how much the AI cloud provider has borrowed, Reuters reported on Friday. By Thursday’s close, the stock was down more than 15% for the week, its biggest weekly fall in about two months. It was also on course to lose a fifth of its value in October. Shares slipped another 4.1% in premarket trading on Friday, after bouncing back 5.9% the day before. The selling has spread to CoreWeave’s debt as well. The company is still loss-making and owes about $50bn. On Thursday, the yield on one of its bonds due in 2031 briefly rose above 16%, a level that usually signals distress. That bond was heading for its fifth consecutive weekly decline. Meanwhile, the cost of insuring CoreWeave’s debt against default climbed past 1,000 basis points this week, a record, according to S&P Global Market Intelligence data cited by Reuters. Most of the damage came on Wednesday, when a hotter-than-expected US inflation report raised fears that borrowing costs will stay high. CoreWeave shares fell more than 15% that day as its bond yields jumped, CNBC reported. “The market is waking up to the fact that AI demand and a profitable business model are two very different things,” Axel Rudolph, an analyst at IG Group, told Reuters. Elon Musk weighed in too, replying “Yikes” to investor Chamath Palihapitiya, who had warned of a private credit bubble around AI. CoreWeave, for its part, is standing firm. In a memo to staff on Wednesday, chief executive Mike Intrator said its backlog of signed deals more than covers its debt, Reuters reported. Markets can “misunderstand and act irrationally,” Intrator wrote. CoreWeave does have some support behind it. Nvidia, one of its investors and its main chip supplier, has agreed to buy any of CoreWeave’s unsold computing capacity until April 2032. Still, the worries reach beyond one company. Nebius, a rival that recently raised $4.5bn in convertible bonds, was also set for its steepest weekly fall in two months. The sell-off comes as Big Tech’s AI debt keeps climbing to pay for new data centres. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription.
