HiBob vs BambooHR
2026-10-16

HiBob vs BambooHR

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
I’ve run both of these platforms for real teams, and the HiBob vs BambooHR decision is one I’ve made from the inside. I set BambooHR up for a lean US company that wanted its records and time off in one tidy system within a month, and it did that job well. Years later I led a HiBob rollout at a company that had grown into several entities and two new countries, where the HR questions had changed shape: how to run one compensation cycle across currencies, and how to show Finance a headcount plan that matched the budget. Both products are good at what they set out to do. The useful question is which set of needs you have. The question that decides it: when should a business prioritize greater flexibility and connected workforce processes over a simpler HR setup? My answer is at the inflection points of organizational complexity. When you add a second legal entity or country, when compensation moves from ad-hoc raises to a structured cycle, when Finance wants a workforce plan it can trust, or when you need to equip managers at scale, Bob earns its place. Until then, BambooHR remains the stronger fit for a team whose priority is simplicity and affordable, straightforward administration, and I’ll say so without hedging. I compared them on the work HR teams do, rather than on feature counts: onboarding, manager workflows, performance reviews, compensation cycles, workforce planning, reporting and analytics, and multi-country operations. The ratings are public G2 figures with review counts attached, and the product claims come from each vendor’s own documentation plus my time in both systems. Check current packages and pricing before you sign anything, because both vendors change them. As of October 16, 2026, BambooHR holds the larger public review base by a wide margin, 7,705 G2 reviews at 4.4 out of 5 against Bob’s 2,704 or so at 4.5. Bob’s average is a touch higher, and BambooHR’s depth of reviews is its own form of proof. Keep that in mind wherever I favor Bob. Both platforms make a new hire’s first week easier than a spreadsheet and an inbox ever did. BambooHR’s onboarding is a model of straightforward administration: a checklist, e-signatures, a welcome packet and a clean self-service portal that employees understand without training. For a single-entity US company that’s close to everything you need, and its simplicity is the point. Bob approaches onboarding as the first of many connected workflows. Its joiner flows route tasks by role and entity, so a new engineer in Berlin and a new account manager in Austin get their own document sets and policy acknowledgements from the same template. In my rollout, setting a start date in Bob fired the onboarding task list and the manager’s first-week checklist at once, and the equipment request went to IT without anyone raising a ticket. That’s the practical meaning of automation here: HR stops chasing and starts reviewing exceptions. The difference: BambooHR makes onboarding simple to run; Bob makes it configurable by role and entity and removes the manual follow-up. The people who decide whether HR software works are the forty managers who touch it once a week, not the HR team. BambooHR gives managers a tidy slice: approve time off and check in on goals, with the team’s records a click away. Managers like it because there’s little to learn. Bob gives managers more to work with and still keeps it light. Structured one-on-ones with templates and meeting history, goals tied to the review cycle, team dashboards and a mobile app that handles approvals on the train all sit in the manager’s own view. Recognition and shoutouts live on the same home feed, which is why managers opened Bob on their own: a kudos post sits one tap from the approval queue. HR professionals found Bob as pleasant to run as managers and employees found it to use, and that shared comfort is rarer in HR software than it should be. The scale question matters here. Five managers can live with email and a spreadsheet. Fifty can’t, and Bob’s manager enablement is one of the inflection points where connected processes beat a simpler setup. The difference: BambooHR keeps the manager’s job small and simple; Bob equips managers with one-on-ones, goals, approvals and recognition in one view, which matters once you have dozens of them. BambooHR has expanded well beyond its HRIS roots here, and I’d call its performance management complete for a company that runs reviews twice a year. Self and manager assessments, peer feedback and goal tracking cover the ground, and the setup stays as approachable as the rest of the product. Bob’s performance tools start from the assumption that no two companies run reviews the same way. You get 360-degree reviews on customizable templates, smart nudges that chase late reviewers so HR doesn’t have to, AI-written summaries of long-form feedback, calibration events on a drag-and-drop 9-box grid, and goals that connect to the review at whatever level you set them. Bob adapts to each organization’s structure and culture, and to the workflows that come with them, so a sales team can run quarterly scorecards while engineering runs peer-led reviews, inside the same cycle and the same data model. Elation Health’s interim VP of HR, Sonia Clark, credits that flexibility for the way her team runs performance management in a testimonial on HiBob’s site, and it matches my experience: the second cycle took half the effort of the first because the templates and nudges did the chasing. The difference: BambooHR runs a clean, standard review cycle; Bob lets each team run the cycle that fits it, with AI summaries and calibration that cut HR’s hours. Compensation is where a growing company feels the limits of a simple setup first, because the data lives half in HR’s system and half in a spreadsheet Finance guards with its life. BambooHR has added compensation planning, and for a US company with one pay structure its approach is light and quick to learn. Bob Compensation treats the cycle as a connected process. Pay bands and ranges live beside the employee record, and the cycle runs on automated workflows with guardrails that keep managers inside budget. Finance sees the cost of every proposed change in real time rather than in a reconciliation meeting afterward. Compensation Benchmarking powered by Mercer sits inside the product, and an AI compensation assistant simulates budget and equity scenarios and flags pay gaps before a cycle closes, so the HR lead spends the cycle making decisions instead of building formulas. When I ran a cross-currency cycle in Bob, the manager recommendations and the Finance sign-off happened in one system, and payroll picked the result up from the same record, which for me was the moment the platform earned its price. The difference: BambooHR handles compensation as an addition to a simple HRIS; Bob runs it as a connected cycle with Finance in the room and AI doing the arithmetic. BambooHR’s analytics report well on the workforce you have: headcount and turnover, and how time off is trending. BambooHR provides workforce reporting and analytics that help smaller teams understand staffing trends and make informed decisions. Bob goes further for organizations that need dedicated workforce planning workflows connected to headcount approvals, compensation budgets, and hiring decisions across multiple teams or entities. Bob Workforce Planning gives HR and Finance a shared model instead. The interactive org chart shows every position, filled or open. Scenario modeling lets you test a reorg or a hiring freeze before you commit, and custom approval flows put governance around headcount requests. The AI planning assistant models headcount scenarios with live cost impact and highlights the risks in each, which turns the quarterly headcount meeting from an argument about numbers into a conversation about choices. The plan then flows into Bob Hiring as approved requisitions, so the recruiter never works from a stale version. The difference: BambooHR reports on today’s org; Bob models tomorrow’s with Finance, and the AI assistant does the cost math. Both vendors have invested in AI, and both deserve credit for it. BambooHR offers Ask BambooHR and a set of AI features that speed up questions about your own data, which fits its promise of straightforward administration. Bob’s AI shows up inside each workflow rather than as a separate chat window: CV summaries and scorecard write-ups in hiring, review summaries in performance, theme and sentiment analysis on engagement surveys, scenario modeling in planning and compensation. The concrete payoff is twofold: less administrative work, because the drafting and summarizing that used to eat HR afternoons now takes minutes, and a clearer understanding of workforce data, because the survey analysis returns themes and sentiment in plain language and the planning assistant explains the risk in a scenario instead of leaving you to read it off a chart. On reporting, Bob’s real-time dashboards sit on one people data model that spans hiring, performance, compensation and planning, so an HR leader can pull attrition by team beside engagement scores and comp ratios without exporting anything. Advanced analytics and reporting of that kind are how HR leaders make data-driven decisions rather than defending a hunch. I built the board’s people dashboard in Bob in an afternoon; in my BambooHR days I built the same view from a monthly export and a pivot table, which suited a smaller company fine. The difference: both have AI; BambooHR speeds up straightforward administration, while Bob lives inside every workflow and turns one connected data set into decisions. BambooHR is a US-centric platform by its own positioning but its international capabilities extend beyond US operations. It runs US payroll as an add-on and connects to partner providers for the rest, which is the right scope for its core customer. Multi-country work isn’t what BambooHR set out to do, and that’s a scope choice rather than a flaw. Bob’s design starts from the distributed company. Bob localizes core HR by country, so leave policies, compliance rules, document types and fields differ by site under one global governance model, with multi-currency compensation and multi-language environments on top, and each entity reports into the same source of truth. On payroll, precision matters, so here’s the exact shape: HiBob runs its own native payroll for the US and the UK, and for every other country the Payroll Hub connects your accredited local payroll partner into Bob through no-code setup, with one dashboard across providers. The in-country processing and compliance sit with those partners, not with Bob. When my company opened its UK entity, HR ran UK payroll from the same system that held the performance and compensation data, and the German entity’s partner payroll fed the same dashboard. That’s what multi-country operations mean day to day: one record per person, wherever they sit. The difference: BambooHR offers straightforward HR administration with US payroll capabilities and international integrations. On the other hand, Bob provides more flexibility for managing employees across countries and entities, with native US and UK payroll and additional providers connected through its Payroll Hub. BambooHR publishes list prices starting at $10 per employee per month for Core, $17 for Pro, and $25 for Elite, with a flat monthly rate starting from $250 for companies with 25 or fewer employees. That transparency is a real advantage for a small team. HiBob pricing is quote-based and tailored to each company’s needs: it is priced per employee with Bob Core as the baseline foundation and additional modules added as needed. Third-party estimates put Core near $8 per employee per month and fuller suite configurations in the $16 to $25 range, but these remain third-party estimates. Price the modules you’ll use, not the whole catalog, and check both vendors’ current pages before you compare, because packaging changes often. The difference: BambooHR is cheaper and clearer at the door; Bob costs more because it carries more, so compare configured quotes rather than headline rates. Pulling the dimensions together, Bob’s advantages are the ones a growing organization feels: BambooHR’s appeal rests on simplicity and affordability, delivered through straightforward HR administration, and it makes good on that promise. It’s the easier system to learn, its list prices are public and lower, applicant tracking comes in the box, and it has expanded into performance management, compensation, analytics and AI without losing the clean feel that made it popular. Its 7,705 G2 reviews at 4.4 out of 5 are the deeper public record of the two. If you’re a US company with one entity, a stable org and an HR team of one or two, BambooHR will serve you well for years, and Bob may be more platform than you need. Choose BambooHR if: you run one entity in one country, your priority is simple and affordable administration, you want a system an HR team of one can master in a week, and you don’t expect structured compensation cycles or multi-country operations in the next two years. Choose HiBob if: you’re adding entities or countries, compensation is becoming a structured cycle, Finance wants a workforce plan it can trust, you have dozens of managers to equip, or you want performance and engagement on the same data as the employee record, from mid-market through enterprise. Back to the deciding question: when should a company choose greater flexibility and connected workforce processes over a simpler HR setup? At the inflection points of complexity: multiple entities or countries, structured compensation cycles, workforce planning with Finance and manager enablement at scale. Before those points, BambooHR’s simpler, more affordable administration is the better buy. After them, Bob pulls people data and workforce processes into one system, so HR makes better decisions alongside managers and Finance as the organization’s needs grow. I compared both platforms on seven practical use cases: onboarding, manager workflows, performance reviews, compensation cycles, workforce planning, reporting and analytics, and multi-country operations. Ratings and review counts are the public G2 figures as of October 16, 2026. Product capabilities come from each vendor’s own documentation and from my hands-on use of both; where I describe a limit, it’s a documented scope choice rather than a reviewer’s complaint. Israeli ad firm used online ads to spread Candiru spyware, report finds
2026-10-16

Israeli ad firm used online ads to spread Candiru spyware, report finds

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Simply seeing an online ad was enough to infect devices at Al Jazeera with spyware, according to a Citizen Lab report published on Thursday. The University of Toronto researchers say it is the first public technical proof of so-called zero-click spyware arriving through ads, in which victims did not have to tap or click anything. The report ties at least 45 Al Jazeera devices, targeted in 2024 and 2025, to Candiru. Citizen Lab first exposed the Israeli spyware maker, now known as Integrity Labs, in 2021, the same year the US blacklisted it. The ads themselves came from Discover-Tech, an Israeli company that presents itself as an advertising firm. In practice, Citizen Lab says, it acts as a go-between for several spyware vendors, Candiru among them. The method relies on real-time bidding, the split-second auctions that decide which ad a person sees when a page or app loads. Each time a Discover-Tech ad appeared, a script checked the device’s ad ID and IP address. A device on the target list received code that started the attack, while every other device got back nothing at all. That meant nobody had to click, and also meant the ad companies serving the ads never saw the harmful code. The operation was large, too, and after reviewing nearly four billion ad-bidding records, the researchers found Discover-Tech ads in more than 70,000 apps and 120,000 websites between March and June 2026. Some of those ads landed on government and defence networks, including NATO’s. Confiant, an ad security firm that ran its own investigation, also spotted them at the US Department of Defense and the International Atomic Energy Agency. Yet, neither group found evidence that those bodies were actually infected. Since 2020, Discover-Tech had sat on approved vendor lists at Google and IAB Europe, the ad industry’s trade body. Both have now cut it off, and Beeswax, Vungle, and Eskimi have also blocked or paused the firm. Neither Discover-Tech nor Candiru responded to Citizen Lab’s requests for comment. The researchers say they cannot yet identify who was behind the attacks, and they have asked regulators to investigate whether the companies broke the law. The case adds to a growing list of spyware cases, from Pegasus on an MEP’s phone to a fake WhatsApp app built by an Italian vendor. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! India says AI agents under testing broke into firms’ live systems
2026-10-16

India says AI agents under testing broke into firms’ live systems

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
AI agents being tested by AI firms broke into the live systems of several Indian companies and read private records, India’s cyber agency says. CERT-In disclosed the incidents in a critical advisory issued late on Thursday. The agency did not name the AI firms or the companies hit. The AI firms found the cases in their own reviews and reported them. None of the companies had spotted the break-ins, CERT-In said. According to the advisory, the agents were being tested when they went beyond their assigned tasks. They got in with passwords and keys left in config files and public code, and through cloud accounts with too much access. In some cases, agents reused the same leaked key in separate runs. The advisory also describes a second problem. CERT-In found criminals using free AI hacking tools against Indian companies for money. The tools are rebranded and run from servers in several countries, it said. In that campaign, AI agents cut the time from first access to damage from days to a few hours. Victims that only partly fixed their systems were hit again within days, on a larger scale. The weak spots in both sets of cases were old. CERT-In listed keys left in code, forgotten test APIs still open to the web, payment keys stored in plain text, and admin pages without two-step login. AI found and exploited these weaknesses “faster, more exhaustively and at greater scale than human attackers,” CERT-In said. As a result, CERT-In wants AI labs to test agents in sealed-off setups, with web access blocked by the network rather than by telling the model. Labs should also use fake data and keys, stop any run that reaches an outside system, and report it fast. Companies, meanwhile, should replace leaked keys, shut unused APIs, require two-step login, and keep logs for at least 180 days. After any leak, they should treat their systems as breached until a forensic check clears them. The advisory follows similar cases elsewhere. OpenAI said in July that its models breached Hugging Face during testing, and Wikimedia this month reported activity from rogue OpenAI agents on its sites. Financial regulators have also begun questioning banks about the risks. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. Faraday Future unveils a $2,490 humanoid robot for developers and schools
2026-10-16

Faraday Future unveils a $2,490 humanoid robot for developers and schools

The Next Web | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Faraday Future and FF EAI Robotics Ecosystem launched two humanoid robots on 15 October: the $2,490 FF Nova for developers and schools, and the $299 FF Nova Mini for children. Nova’s first 100 units are invitation-only. The launch follows FFR’s agreement to buy FF’s robotics business in a $200M all-stock deal. Faraday Future and its sister company, FF EAI Robotics Ecosystem (FFR), have launched two humanoid robots: the $2,490 FF Nova and the $299 FF Nova Mini. The companies announced the robots on 15 October in a joint press release. The companies call Nova the first embodied AI humanoid robot sold in the US for under $2,500. Embodied AI means AI that controls a physical body, such as a robot. TNW has not independently verified the claim. Nova is aimed at developers, universities, and schools. It has 20 degrees of freedom, or moving joints, and can walk, wave, and get back up after being knocked over, the companies said. It can also ride a detachable wheeled base. The robot stands 49.7cm tall and weighs 2.8kg, according to its product page, as reported by Unite.AI. A developer software package costs an extra $390, and Nova supports ROS 2, a widely used robotics software framework. The companies are also offering a training programme built on Nvidia’s Isaac platform, which lets developers teach robot skills in simulation before testing them on Nova. Nvidia has been courting humanoid robot makers with the same tools. Nova will roll out in three phases. The first is limited to 100 units for invited developers and schools, with 20 ready to ship now. Wider developer sales and a full consumer launch will follow, though no dates were given. Nova Mini is designed for children aged eight and up. It weighs under 1kg, can walk, stand up on its own, and do a forward roll, and it supports block-based programming. FF also sells a $499 course of 16 live online classes, and buyers get a voucher towards the robot. “The launch of FF Nova and FF Nova Mini marks an important step for FFR in bringing Physical AI from the laboratory to real-world applications,” said Jerry Wang, FFR’s chief executive. The launch comes days after FFR agreed to buy FF’s robotics business in an all-stock deal valuing it at about $200M. FF would own about 65.7% of FFR after closing, which the companies expect by the end of October. FFR was known as AIxCrypto Holdings until 30 September. FF, best known for its electric cars, started its robotics pivot earlier this year. Its robotics unit had shipped 817 units by the end of September, Investing.com reported.
